Optimal Detection for Language Watermarks with Pseudorandom Collision
T. Tony Cai
Affiliation: University of Pennsylvania
Xiang Li
Affiliation: University of Pennsylvania
Qi Long
Affiliation: University of Pennsylvania
Weijie J. Su
Affiliation: University of Pennsylvania
Garrett G. Wen
Affiliation: Yale University
October 22, 2025
Abstract
Text watermarking plays a crucial role in ensuring the traceability and accountability of large language model (LLM) outputs and mitigating misuse. While promising, most existing methods assume perfect pseudorandomness. In practice, repetition in generated text induces collisions that create structured dependence, compromising Type I error control and invalidating standard analyses.
We introduce a statistical framework that captures this structure through a hierarchical two-layer partition. At its core is the concept of minimal units—the smallest groups treatable as independent across units while permitting dependence within. Using minimal units, we define a non-asymptotic efficiency measure and cast watermark detection as a minimax hypothesis testing problem.
Applied to Gumbel-max and inverse-transform watermarks, our framework produces closed-form optimal rules. It explains why discarding repeated statistics often improves performance and shows that within-unit dependence must be addressed unless degenerate. Both theory and experiments confirm improved detection power with rigorous Type I error control. These results provide the first principled foundation for watermark detection under imperfect pseudorandomness, offering both theoretical insight and practical guidance for reliable tracing of model outputs.

1 Introduction
Recent advances in generative artificial intelligence have profoundly transformed the creation and consumption of digital content. Systems capable of generating human-like text, images, and audio are now widely accessible, with large language models (LLMs) being particularly influential [36, 29]. The ability of LLMs to produce fluent text at scale enables powerful applications, from creative writing to automated code generation. However, this proliferation also precipitates pressing concerns over provenance and authenticity. In high-stakes domains such as education, journalism, and scientific research, the misattribution of AI-generated content can have severe consequences, including undermining academic integrity, eroding public trust, and compromising research reproducibility [47, 33, 51, 46, 41, 8]. This landscape highlights an urgent need for reliable methods to distinguish between human-written and machine-generated text.
While many detection methods rely on identifying linguistic artifacts, a more principled and statistical approach is LLM watermarking, which has seen internal implementation by OpenAI and Google DeepMind [1, 9].
This technique embeds a verifiable statistical signal into the text generation process using pseudorandom variables derived from a secret cryptographic key [24, 22]. In effect, the key initializes a pseudorandom generator that governs how texts are generated, thereby creating a hidden statistical dependence between the generated text and the key. This dependence enables rigorous hypothesis testing for provable detection [27, 26]. In a typical implementation, a provider deploys a watermarked LLM. A user, such as a student, interacts with the model to produce a text. A verifier, such as a teacher, who has been granted access to the cryptographic key, can then analyze the text to determine if it was generated by the watermarked model.
To formalize the watermarking mechanism, it is instructive to first recognize that LLMs sequentially generate a token in a probabilistical manner.11
1
Here, a token represents a word, subword, or punctuation. For example, the sentence “Hello, world!” can be tokenized into four tokens: [“Hello”, “,”, “ world”, “!”]]. See https://platform.openai.com/tokenizer for examples. To produce the tt-th token, denoted by wt{w}_{t}, the model first computes a next-token prediction (NTP) distribution 𝑷t\bm{P}_{t} over its vocabulary based on the preceding tokens w1:(t−1):=w1⋯wt−1{w}_{1:(t-1)}:={w}_{1}\cdots{w}_{t-1}. For a watermarked LLM, the sampling of wt{w}_{t} from the NTP distribution 𝑷t\bm{P}_{t} is governed by a pseudorandom variable ζt\zeta_{t}, which is typically generated by a cryptographic hash function 𝒜{\mathcal{A}} that takes a private 𝙺𝚎𝚢{\mathtt{Key}} and the recent context window w(t−m):(t−1){w}_{(t-m):(t-1)} as input. While the resulting token wt{w}_{t} still marginally follows the original distribution 𝑷t\bm{P}_{t}, its realization is now tied to ζt\zeta_{t}. Consequently, while the marginal distributions of the tokens may be indistinguishable from unwatermarked text, their joint distribution with the pseudorandom variables is not. Without a watermark, the tokens and pseudorandom variables are statistically independent, while with a watermark, they become dependent. This induced dependence is the statistical underpinning for detection, whereby a verifier reconstructs the sequence of pseudorandom variables ζ1,…,ζn\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{n} and constructs a test statistic to capture their association with the observed text.
Two of the most commonly used watermarking schemes are the Gumbel-max watermark [1] and the inverse-transform watermark [24].
Both, along with most existing watermarking schemes, are theoretically grounded in a fundamental assumption that the pseudorandom variables ζt=𝒜(w(t−m):(t−1),𝙺𝚎𝚢)\zeta_{t}={\mathcal{A}}({w}_{(t-m):(t-1)},{\mathtt{Key}}) are independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) for t=m+1,…,nt=m+1,\dots,n.
This assumption is justified when the context window w(t−m):(t−1){w}_{(t-m):(t-1)} is unique for every position tt, since the cryptographic design of the hash function ensures that its outputs behave as independent uniform draws.22
2
The hash function is sensitive to its inputs, that is, 𝒜(w(t−m):(t−1),𝙺𝚎𝚢){\mathcal{A}}({w}_{(t-m):(t-1)},{\mathtt{Key}}) is independent of 𝒜(w(t′−m):(t′−1),𝙺𝚎𝚢){\mathcal{A}}({w}_{(t^{\prime}-m):(t^{\prime}-1)},{\mathtt{Key}}) whenever the text windows differ, w(t−m):(t−1)≠w(t′−m):(t′−1){w}_{(t-m):(t-1)}\neq{w}_{(t^{\prime}-m):(t^{\prime}-1)}, for t≠t′t\neq t^{\prime}, as 𝙺𝚎𝚢{\mathtt{Key}} is randomly selected. In practice, however, language is inherently repetitive, particularly in specialized domains like programming and mathematical writing [18]. When a segment of text repeats such that w(t−m):(t−1)=w(t′−m):(t′−1){w}_{(t-m):(t-1)}={w}_{(t^{\prime}-m):(t^{\prime}-1)} for some t≠t′t\neq t^{\prime}, the deterministic nature of the hash function forces ζt=ζt′\zeta_{t}=\zeta_{t^{\prime}}. This phenomenon, which is known as pseudorandom collision [50], is surprisingly common. It typically becomes more frequent when the LLM generation is relatively deterministic (e.g., during code generation or list completion, where the entropy of the NTP distributions is low) or when the context window size mm is small (see the left panel in Figure 1). Importantly, collisions are not merely implementation artifacts but an intrinsic feature of language. They cannot be eliminated entirely, as even human-written documents naturally contain repeated phrases (see Table 1 for examples from classic works of literature).
Unfortunately, pseudorandom collisions fundamentally violate the independence assumption that underpins recent statistical frameworks for watermark detection [27, 26] and estimation [28]. Since these methods all rely on token-level independence of pivotal statistics, collisions make this assumption fail and render the guarantees unreliable.
Without this independence, not only are power analyses invalidated, but more critically, even Type I error control is no longer guaranteed (see the right panel in Figure 1). Among many challenges, one lies in the fact that collisions can occur anywhere within the text, leading to complex and unpredictable dependence structures.
While heuristic fixes have been proposed [14, 50, 9], a systematic statistical analysis is still largely absent. This presents a pressing statistical challenge to the reliable detection of LLM watermarks and calls into question both the framework and the optimality of detection rules derived under the i.i.d. assumption. Consequently, comparisons between different watermarking schemes that neglect pseudorandom collisions cannot be considered trustworthy. It thus leads to a central question: can we establish a new framework and design provably optimal detection rules in the presence of imperfect pseudorandomness?
Figure 1: Left: Fraction of repeated segments across different text window sizes mm for the OPT-1.3B model [52] on the C4 news-like dataset [42].
Right: Inflation of type I error when the null human-written data contains repetition, evaluated at significance level α=0.05\alpha=0.05.

1.1 Our Contributions
To address this challenge, we develop a new framework for watermark detection that explicitly accounts for pseudorandomness collisions. This framework still builds on the pivotal-statistic approach of [27], but differs by carefully capturing how text repetition affects the joint distribution of the pivotal statistics Y1:nY_{1:n}.
When no text windows repeat, the pseudorandom variables ζ1:n\zeta_{1:n} can be safely treated as i.i.d., and by the pivotal property, Y1:nY_{1:n} is also i.i.d. With repetitions, however, some pseudorandom variables and pivotal statistics become identical: if two positions t≠t′t\neq t^{\prime} share the same context window, then ζt=ζt′\zeta_{t}=\zeta_{t^{\prime}}, in which case we also have Yt=Yt′Y_{t}=Y_{t^{\prime}} whenever wt=wt′{w}_{t}={w}_{t^{\prime}}. Such collisions at the ζ\zeta-level and coincidences at the token level induce structured dependence in Y1:nY_{1:n}. To systematically capture this dependence, we introduce a hierarchical framework built on a two-level partition of pseudorandom variables and pivotal statistics. At the first level, ζ1:n\zeta_{1:n} are grouped into blocks reflecting pseudorandom collisions, while, at the second, Y1:nY_{1:n} are further divided into sub-blocks accounting for both pseudorandom collisions and token coincidences.
This two-level structure provides a refined basis for analysis. Within this framework, the detection problem reduces to testing distributional differences in Y1:nY_{1:n} conditioned on the observed two-level partitions, with a formal formulation presented in (4). This formulation serves as the foundation for developing provably optimal detection rules and sets the stage for our contributions below.
A hierarchical framework of LLM watermarks.
We propose a statistical framework for watermark detection that explicitly accounts for text repetition through the hierarchical two-layer partition. This partition captures the dependence among pivotal statistics and allows their joint distribution to be characterized without any information loss. Within this structure, we find that the pivotal statistics can be partitioned into disjoint subsets, which we call minimal units, that are mutually independent across units though not independent within each unit. Taking minimal units as the basic analytic objects, we introduce a new non-asymptotic efficiency notion that quantifies least-favorable detection power when NTP distributions lie in a belief class, casting the search for optimal rules as a minimax problem. Finally, we develop a general non-i.i.d. large-deviation bound under verifiable conditions, which provides a tight characterization of this efficiency notion (see Remark 3.3). This framework is formally introduced in Section 3.
Application to the Gumbel-max watermark.
We apply our framework to the Gumbel-max watermark in Section 4 and analyze the associated minimax problem of maximizing the efficiency notion. We find that a saddle-point pair—consisting of an optimal detection rule and the corresponding least-favorable distribution—does not always exist. When it does, we derive closed-form expressions; when it does not, we characterize the transition boundaries. Notably, the optimally derived rule reduces to discarding all repeated pivotal statistics in Y1:nY_{1:n}, a form that resonates with empirical heuristics proposed in [14, 50, 9]. Our optimal rule rigorously controls Type I error and achieves detection power comparable to, and in some cases exceeding, existing methods, as shown in numerical experiments.
However, deriving these optimal rules is more challenging than in prior work [27]. While both frameworks maximize least-favorable detection power over a class of NTP distributions, theirs operates at the token level, whereas ours must operate on minimal units within the hierarchical partition. This shift renders the minimax problem highly non-convex, as it requires accounting for all NTP distributions within a unit rather than a single token-level distribution. To tackle this difficulty, we develop new analytical tools based on Schur-convexity and geometric arguments, which resolve the optimality issues in this non-convex setting and may be of independent interest.
Application to the inverse transform watermark.
Finally, we apply our framework to the inverse transform watermark in Section 5. This case poses unique analytical challenges, as the joint distribution involves exponentially many terms and is intractable in finite form. We show that as the vocabulary size grows, the distribution converges to a simpler asymptotic limit, which makes the minimax problem tractable and yields a closed-form optimal detection rule. Our analysis further reveals that, while discarding repeated pivotal statistics remains harmless, optimal rules must still account for the dependence among statistics within each minimal unit, since they share the same pseudorandom variables. Numerical experiments corroborate these results, showing comparable detection power while maintaining rigorous Type I error control.
1.2 Related Work
Since the introduction of text watermarking for LLMs [21, 1], text repetition has been widely observed. Such repetition—often caused by relatively deterministic generation or small context windows [14, 24]—induces pseudorandom collisions. Prior analysis frameworks [27, 26, 53] and downstream estimation tasks [28] overlook this issue by assuming perfect pseudorandomness, where all pivotal statistics are assumed to be i.i.d. In practice, collisions introduce strong dependencies, since repeated contexts force correlation or even identity among pivotal statistics. As a result, empirical Type I error can be severely inflated, far beyond the nominal level [14, 50], undermining the reliability of the watermark. While some studies note that mild repetition can occasionally improve power or robustness in goodness-of-fit tests [18], this benefit comes at the cost of uncontrolled Type I error, making repetition generally undesirable.
To address this issue, we develop a new formulation and analysis techniques that explicitly account for the dependence induced by pseudorandom collisions. As a consequence, our framework not only resolves this fundamental issue but also explains why a common empirical fix—discarding repeated pivotal statistics and applying detection rules only to the unique ones [14, 50, 9]—is information-theoretically justified, as it matches the structure of the optimal detection rule.
From a statistical standpoint, the collision-induced dependence structure presents a novel challenge. Classical goodness-of-fit tests [11, 6, 7] typically assume i.i.d. samples under both the null and alternative hypotheses, whereas our problem involves a non-i.i.d. setting where the dependence structure is captured by the hierarchical
two-layer partition. Unlike traditional cases (such as serial correlation in time series [5, 44] or within-subject dependence in longitudinal data [10, 15]) where dependence takes the form of partial correlation and each observation still contributes new information, our setting exhibits a more extreme structure: some pivotal statistics are exact duplicates due to collisions, while others are intricately linked through shared pseudorandom variables. These overlaps fall outside existing frameworks, and our work offers the first formal treatment of hypothesis testing under this collision-driven dependence. In pursuing optimal detection rules, our strategy connects to the classical literature on robust hypothesis testing [19, 49, 12], which also seeks detectors optimized against least-favorable distributions from a belief class. The key difference is that our setting is considerably more complex: saddle-point solutions may fail to exist, whereas in classical formulations they typically do, due to the simplicity of their model and problem setup.
2 Preliminaries
Watermarking embedding and detection.
At a high level, watermarking modifies text generation by coupling each token with a recoverable pseudorandom variable, often referred to as a random seed in [9]. Concretely, rather than drawing the tt-th token directly from the model’s next-token-prediction (NTP) distribution 𝑷t=(Pt,w)w∈𝒲\bm{P}_{t}=(P_{t,w})_{w\in{{\mathcal{W}}}}, the process first generates a pseudorandom variable ζt=𝒜(w(t−m):(t−1),𝙺𝚎𝚢)\zeta_{t}={\mathcal{A}}({w}_{(t-m):(t-1)},{\mathtt{Key}}), where 𝒜{\mathcal{A}} is a cryptographic hash function applied to the preceding context window w(t−m):(t−1){w}_{(t-m):(t-1)} together with a secret 𝙺𝚎𝚢{\mathtt{Key}}. The token is then produced by a decoding function wt=𝒮⁡(𝑷t,ζt){w}_{t}={\mathcal{S}}(\bm{P}_{t},\zeta_{t}), which links 𝑷t\bm{P}_{t} and ζt\zeta_{t} in a deterministic way. The sequence ζ1:n:=ζ1…ζn\zeta_{1:n}:=\zeta_{1}\ldots\zeta_{n} is typically modeled as i.i.d., a valid assumption only when every length-mm context prefix is unique [3, 43]. In this work, we focus on unbiased decoders, which preserve the marginal distribution in the sense that ℙζ​(𝒮⁡(𝑷,ζ)=w)=Pw{\mathbb{P}}_{\zeta}({\mathcal{S}}(\bm{P},\zeta)=w)=P_{w}. In this way, watermarking does not degrade text quality.
To detect the watermark, a verifier reconstructs the sequence ζ1:n\zeta_{1:n} and tests for the statistical dependence between each wt{w}_{t} and ζt\zeta_{t}. This is formalized using a pivotal statistic Yt=Y⁡(wt,ζt)Y_{t}=Y(w_{t},\zeta_{t}) [27]. Under the null hypothesis H0H_{0} (human-written text), wtw_{t} and ζt\zeta_{t} are independent, by the pivotal property, YtY_{t} follows a fixed null distribution denoted by μ0\mu_{0}, regardless of the distribution of wtw_{t}. Under the alternative H1H_{1} (watermarked text), the induced dependence shifts its distribution to an alternative μ1,𝑷t\mu_{1,\bm{P}_{t}}, which depends on 𝑷t\bm{P}_{t} since in this case YtY_{t} takes the form Yt=Y⁡(𝒮⁡(𝑷t,ζt),ζt)Y_{t}=Y({\mathcal{S}}(\bm{P}_{t},\zeta_{t}),\zeta_{t}). In this way, [27, 26] formulate detection as the hypothesis testing problem:
H0:Yt∼μ0 i.i.d.,t=1,…,nvs.H1:Yt∼μ1,𝑷t,t=1,…,n.H_{0}:Y_{t}\sim\mu_{0}\text{ i.i.d.},\quad t=1,\dots,n\qquad\text{vs.}\qquad H_{1}:Y_{t}\sim\mu_{1,\bm{P}_{t}},\quad t=1,\dots,n.
(1)
The standard detection approach, which aggregates scores h⁡(Yt)h(Y_{t}), relies on the i.i.d. property of the sequence {ζt}t=1n\{\zeta_{t}\}_{t=1}^{n}. In practice, however, text repetition leads to hash collisions (that is, ζt=ζt′\zeta_{t}=\zeta_{t^{\prime}} for some t≠t′t\neq t^{\prime}), violating this core assumption. This breakdown of independence for the pivotal statistics {Yt}t=1n\{Y_{t}\}_{t=1}^{n} motivates the framework developed in this paper.
Gumbel-max watermark.
The Gumbel-max watermark [1] is the most influential unbiased watermarking scheme and has seen widespread adoption in research [37]. It builds on the classical Gumbel-max technique [16, 39], which samples from a distribution 𝑷=(Pw)w∈𝒲\bm{P}=(P_{w})_{w\in{{\mathcal{W}}}} by drawing Uw∼Unif⁡(0,1)U_{w}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(0,1) independently for each w∈𝒲w\in{{\mathcal{W}}} and selecting
𝒮gum​(𝑷,ζ):=arg⁡maxw∈𝒲⁡log⁡UwPw,where ζ=(Uw)w∈𝒲.{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{gum}}(\bm{P},\zeta):=\arg\max_{w\in{{\mathcal{W}}}}\frac{\log U_{w}}{P_{w}},\quad\text{where }\quad\zeta=(U_{w})_{w\in{{\mathcal{W}}}}.
This decoder is unbiased by construction [27]. The associated pivotal statistic is Yt=Ut,wtY_{t}=U_{t,{w}_{t}}, which is uniformly distributed on (0,1)(0,1) when the text is human-written (that is, H0H_{0}), but becomes stochastically larger under watermarking (that is, H1H_{1}) due to the watermark-induced alignment. Detection procedures exploit this shift by aggregating scores ∑t=1nh⁡(Yt)\sum_{t=1}^{n}h(Y_{t}) and declaring watermarking when the sum exceeds a threshold. In practice, effective score functions are those whose expectations are larger under H1H_{1} than under H0H_{0}. Common choices include hars​(y)=−log⁡(1−y)h_{\mathrm{ars}}(y)=-\log(1-y) [1], hlog​(y)=log⁡yh_{\mathrm{log}}(y)=\log y [24], and the optimal hgum,Δ{h_{\mathrm{gum},\Delta}} from [27], which depends on a user-specified parameter Δ∈(0,1)\Delta\in(0,1).
Inverse transform watermark.
An alternative unbiased scheme is the inverse transform watermark of [24], which uses inverse transform sampling for unbiased token generation. To produce a token w{w}, the scheme first generates a random permutation of the vocabulary, denoted by π\pi, together with a uniform draw U∼Unif⁡(0,1)U\sim\mathrm{Unif}(0,1), and combines them as ζ=(U,π)\zeta=(U,\pi). The token is then chosen via
𝒮inv(𝑷,ζ)=π−1(F−1(U;π)),where F(x;π)=∑w′∈𝒲Pw′⋅𝟏{π(w′)≤x},{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inv}}(\bm{P},\zeta)=\pi^{-1}(F^{-1}(U;\pi)),\quad\text{where }\quad F(x;\pi)=\sum_{{w}^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{W}}}}P_{{w}^{\prime}}\cdot\mathbf{1}\{\pi({w}^{\prime})\leq x\},
and F−1​(u,π)=min⁡{x:F⁡(x,π)≥u}F^{-1}(u;\pi)=\min\{x:F(x;\pi)\geq u\} is the generalized inverse of F⁡(x,π)F(x;\pi).
The corresponding pivotal statistic is Ytinv=|η⁡(πt​(wt))−Ut|Y^{\mathrm{inv}}_{t}=|\eta(\pi_{t}(w_{t}))-U_{t}|, with η⁡(w)=(w−1)/(|𝒲|−1)\eta(w)=(w-1)/(|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1) mapping token indices to [0,1][0,1]. Under human-written text (H0H_{0}), YtinvY^{\mathrm{inv}}_{t} is approximately distributed as |U−U′||U-U^{\prime}| for two independent U,U′∼Unif⁡(0,1)U,U^{\prime}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(0,1), giving rise to a triangular distribution. Under watermarking (H1H_{1}), it concentrates near zero due to alignment. As in the Gumbel-max case, detection exploits this shift through score functions. Typical examples include hneg​(y)=−yh_{\mathrm{neg}}(y)=-y and the optimal hdif,Δ{h_{\mathrm{dif},\Delta}} from [27], also parameterized by a user-specified parameter Δ∈(0,1)\Delta\in(0,1).
3 A Statistical Framework under Pseudorandomness Collision
This section introduces our statistical framework for watermark detection under pseudorandomness collisions. We begin in Section 3.1 with the two-layer partition structure that models the induced dependence, then in Section 3.2 formalize the detection problem, and finally in Section 3.3 define an efficiency notion that enables a minimax characterization of optimal detection rules.
3.1 Structural Dependence and Distribution Factorization
Text repetition induces repeated pseudorandom variables and, in turn, repeated pivotal statistics. Specifically, under the hash rule ζt=𝒜(w(t−m):(t−1),𝙺𝚎𝚢)\zeta_{t}={\mathcal{A}}(w_{(t-m):(t-1)},{\mathtt{Key}}), if two context windows satisfy w(t−m):(t−1)=w(t′−m):(t′−1)w_{(t-m):(t-1)}=w_{(t^{\prime}-m):(t^{\prime}-1)} for t≠t′t\neq t^{\prime}, then ζt=ζt′\zeta_{t}=\zeta_{t^{\prime}}.
Moreover, if wt=wt′w_{t}=w_{t^{\prime}} as well, then by the definition Yt=Y⁡(wt,ζt)Y_{t}=Y(w_{t},\zeta_{t}), it follows that Yt=Yt′Y_{t}=Y_{t^{\prime}}.
We formalize this dependence structure via a two-level partition of the index set ℐ={1,2,…,n}\mathcal{I}=\{1,2,\dots,n\}.
Two-level partitions.
The first partition focuses on pseudorandom variables.
Definition 3.1 (ζ\zeta-level partition).
The ζ\zeta-level partition is defined as Πζ:={ℐkζ}k=1K={ℐ1ζ,…,ℐKζ}\Pi_{\zeta}:=\{{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta}\}_{k=1}^{K}=\{{\mathcal{I}}_{1}^{\zeta},\ldots,{\mathcal{I}}_{K}^{\zeta}\}, where each block ℐkζ⊂ℐ{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta}\subset\mathcal{I} satisfies:
(i)
All indices in ℐkζ{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta} share the same pseudorandom variable: ζi=ζj\zeta_{i}=\zeta_{j} for all i,j∈ℐkζi,j\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta}, while distinct blocks correspond to distinct values: ζi≠ζj\zeta_{i}\neq\zeta_{j} for i∈ℐkζ,j∈ℐk′ζi\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta},j\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k^{\prime}}^{\zeta} with k≠k′k\neq k^{\prime}.
(ii)
The blocks form a disjoint partition of ℐ\mathcal{I}: ⋃k=1Kℐkζ=ℐ\bigcup_{k=1}^{K}{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta}=\mathcal{I} and ℐkζ∩ℐk′ζ=∅{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta}\cap{\mathcal{I}}_{k^{\prime}}^{\zeta}=\varnothing for k≠k′k\neq k^{\prime}.
Each ζ\zeta-block is further refined based on whether the pivotal statistics coincide.
Definition 3.2 (YY-level partition).
For each block ℐkζ{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta}, the corresponding YY-level partition is defined as ΠY(k)={ℐk,lY}l=1mk={ℐk,1Y,…,ℐk,mkY}\Pi_{Y}^{(k)}=\{{\mathcal{I}}_{k,l}^{Y}\}_{l=1}^{m_{k}}=\{{\mathcal{I}}_{k,1}^{Y},\ldots,{\mathcal{I}}_{k,m_{k}}^{Y}\}, where each sub-block ℐk,lY⊂ℐkζ{\mathcal{I}}_{k,l}^{Y}\subset{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta} satisfies:
(i)
All indices in ℐk,lY{\mathcal{I}}_{k,l}^{Y} share the same pivotal statistic: Yi=YjY_{i}=Y_{j} for all i,j∈ℐk,lYi,j\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k,l}^{Y}, while distinct sub-blocks correspond to distinct values: Yi≠YjY_{i}\neq Y_{j} for i∈ℐk,lY,j∈ℐk,l′Yi\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k,l}^{Y},j\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k,l^{\prime}}^{Y} with l≠l′l\neq l^{\prime}.
(ii)
The sub-blocks form a disjoint partition of ℐkζ{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta}: ⋃l=1mkℐk,lY=ℐkζ\bigcup_{l=1}^{m_{k}}{\mathcal{I}}_{k,l}^{Y}={\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta} and ℐk,lY∩ℐk,l′Y=∅{\mathcal{I}}_{k,l}^{Y}\cap{\mathcal{I}}_{k,l^{\prime}}^{Y}=\varnothing for l≠l′l\neq l^{\prime}.
123456789ζ1\zeta_{1}ζ2\zeta_{2}ζ3\zeta_{3}ζ4\zeta_{4}ζ5\zeta_{5}ζ6\zeta_{6}ζ7\zeta_{7}ζ8\zeta_{8}ζ9\zeta_{9}==========ℐ1ζ{\mathcal{I}}_{1}^{\zeta}ℐ2ζ{\mathcal{I}}_{2}^{\zeta}ℐ3ζ{\mathcal{I}}_{3}^{\zeta}ℐ4ζ{\mathcal{I}}_{4}^{\zeta}Πζ\Pi_{\zeta}Y1Y_{1}Y2Y_{2}Y3Y_{3}Y4Y_{4}Y5Y_{5}Y6Y_{6}Y7Y_{7}Y8Y_{8}Y9Y_{9}======ℐ1,1Y{\mathcal{I}}_{1,1}^{Y}ℐ1,2Y{\mathcal{I}}_{1,2}^{Y}ℐ2,1Y{\mathcal{I}}_{2,1}^{Y}ℐ2,2Y{\mathcal{I}}_{2,2}^{Y}ℐ3,1Y{\mathcal{I}}_{3,1}^{Y}ℐ4,1Y{\mathcal{I}}_{4,1}^{Y}blocks in ζ\zeta-level partitionthe full setΠY\Pi_{Y}sub-blocks in YY-level partition
Figure 2: Illustration of the two-level partition structure in a 99-length sequence: the ζ\zeta-level partition Πζ\Pi_{\zeta} groups indices with the same pseudorandom variable, while the YY-level partition ΠY\Pi_{Y} further groups them by shared pivotal statistic.
An example of the two-layer partition is shown in Figure 2. While this structure captures the dependencies caused by repeated context windows, it also implies where conditional independence can still hold. In particular, pseudorandom variables associated with different blocks can be safely treated as independent (see Assumption 3.1 for the formal statement). This independence follows from the input sensitivity of cryptographic hash functions: when the input contexts differ, the resulting pseudorandom outputs—𝒜(w(t−m):(t−1),𝙺𝚎𝚢){\mathcal{A}}({w}_{(t-m):(t-1)},{\mathtt{Key}}) and 𝒜(w(t′−m):(t′−1),𝙺𝚎𝚢){\mathcal{A}}({w}_{(t^{\prime}-m):(t^{\prime}-1)},{\mathtt{Key}})—are statistically independent [50].
Assumption 3.1 (Independence across blocks).
For k≠k′k\neq k^{\prime} and any i∈ℐkζi\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta} and j∈ℐk′ζj\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k^{\prime}}^{\zeta}, ζi\zeta_{i} is statistically independent of ζj\zeta_{j}, denoted as ζi⟂ζj\zeta_{i}\perp\zeta_{j}.
Corollary 3.1.
Under Assumption 3.1, for k≠k′k\neq k^{\prime} and any indices i∈ℐkζi\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta} and j∈ℐk′ζj\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k^{\prime}}^{\zeta}, YiY_{i} is statistically independent of YjY_{j}, denoted as Yi⟂YjY_{i}\perp Y_{j}.
In some cases, a finer level of independence holds between sub-blocks (see Assumption 3.2). Recall that each Yt=Y⁡(ζt,wt)Y_{t}=Y(\zeta_{t},w_{t}) is a deterministic function of both ζt\zeta_{t} and wtw_{t}. Since ζt\zeta_{t} is constant within each sub-block, this finer independence requires that the function w↦Y⁡(ζt,w)w\mapsto Y(\zeta_{t},w) induces variability across tokens. Whether this holds depends on the specific structure of the decoder 𝒮{\mathcal{S}} and the statistic YY, and does not hold universally. A notable case where it does is the Gumbel-max watermark, where ζt\zeta_{t} is a random vector with i.i.d. U⁡(0,1){U}(0,1) entries and YtY_{t} selects the entry indexed by wtw_{t}, preserving independence across tokens even when ζt\zeta_{t} is shared.
Assumption 3.2 (Independence across sub-blocks).
For any i∈ℐk,lYi\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k,l}^{Y} and j∈ℐk′,l′Yj\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k^{\prime},l^{\prime}}^{Y}, Yi⟂YjY_{i}\perp Y_{j}, whenever either k≠k′k\neq k^{\prime} or k=k′k=k^{\prime} but l≠l′l\neq l^{\prime}.
Factorization from structural independence.
The pivotal statistics Y1:nY_{1:n} are the basis for detection. A direct consequence of the above independence conditions is that the joint distribution of Y1:nY_{1:n} factorizes across blocks—and in some cases, across sub-blocks—which simplifies both analysis and inference.
Proposition 3.1 (Distribution factorization).
Let Πζ={ℐkζ}k=1K\Pi_{\zeta}=\{{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta}\}_{k=1}^{K} denote a ζ\zeta-level partition.
Under Assumption 3.1, the joint distribution of (Yt)t=1n(Y_{t})_{t=1}^{n} factorizes as
ℙ⁡((Yt)t=1n∣Πζ)=∏𝒱∈Πζℙ⁡((Yt)t∈𝒱|Πζ)\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}((Y_{t})_{t=1}^{n}\mid\Pi_{\zeta})=\prod_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi_{\zeta}}{\mathbb{P}}((Y_{t})_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}|\Pi_{\zeta})
(2)
If Assumption 3.2 holds, let ΠY(k)={ℐk,lY}l=1mk\Pi_{Y}^{(k)}=\{{\mathcal{I}}_{k,l}^{Y}\}_{l=1}^{m_{k}} be the YY-level refinement of ℐkζ{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta}, and define the full YY-level partition as ΠY:={ℐk,lY}k,l\Pi_{Y}:=\{{\mathcal{I}}_{k,l}^{Y}\}_{k,l}. Then the joint distribution further factorizes as
ℙ⁡((Yt)t=1n∣ΠY)=∏𝒱∈ΠYℙ⁡((Yt)t∈𝒱|ΠY).\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}((Y_{t})_{t=1}^{n}\mid\Pi_{Y})=\prod_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi_{Y}}{\mathbb{P}}((Y_{t})_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}|\Pi_{Y}).
(3)
Minimal units.
Proposition 3.1 establishes that, conditioned on the observed repetition pattern (represented by the tuple (Πζ,ΠY)(\Pi_{\zeta},\Pi_{Y})), the joint distribution of (Yt)t=1n(Y_{t})_{t=1}^{n} factorizes into independent components.
We denote such a component by 𝒱{\mathcal{V}}, which corresponds either to a block like ℐ1ζ,…,ℐKζ{\mathcal{I}}_{1}^{\zeta},\ldots,{\mathcal{I}}_{K}^{\zeta}, where pseudorandom variables are shared (as in (2)), or to a sub-block like ℐ1,1Y,…,ℐK,mKY{\mathcal{I}}_{1,1}^{Y},\ldots,{\mathcal{I}}_{K,m_{K}}^{Y}, where pivotal statistics coincide (as in (3)).
We refer to this element 𝒱{\mathcal{V}} as a minimal unit—the finest partition level at which this independence factorization holds.
We denote the set of all minimal units as Π\Pi, which can be either Πζ\Pi_{\zeta} or ΠY\Pi_{Y} depending on the structure. In the case of the Gumbel-max watermark, for instance, the minimal units are the sub-blocks. A key implication is that pivotal statistics from different minimal units are mutually independent, while those within the same unit might exhibit strong dependence due to pseudorandomness collisions.
3.2 Problem Formulation
With the two-layer partition structure in place, we now formalize the hypothesis testing problem. Given data Y1:nY_{1:n}, where each Yt=Y⁡(wt,ζt)Y_{t}=Y({w}_{t},\zeta_{t}) depends on the token wt{w}_{t} and its associated pseudorandom variable ζt\zeta_{t}, we begin by identifying the repetition pattern and representing it through the two-layer partitions Πζ\Pi_{\zeta} and ΠY\Pi_{Y}. The goal is to test:
H0:Yt|(Πζ,ΠY)∼μ0,t=1,…,nvs.H1:Yt|(Πζ,ΠY)∼μ1,𝑷t,t=1,…,n.H_{0}:Y_{t}~|~(\Pi_{\zeta},\Pi_{Y})\sim\mu_{0},\quad t=1,\dots,n\quad\text{vs.}\quad H_{1}:Y_{t}~|~(\Pi_{\zeta},\Pi_{Y})\sim\mu_{1,\bm{P}_{t}},\quad t=1,\dots,n.
(4)
The notation Yt|(Πζ,ΠY)Y_{t}~|~(\Pi_{\zeta},\Pi_{Y}) indicates that the joint distribution of (Yt)t=1n(Y_{t})_{t=1}^{n} follows the observed repetition structure: indices within the same block of Πζ\Pi_{\zeta} share the same pseudorandom variable, and those within the same sub-block of ΠY\Pi_{Y} take on the same pivotal statistic.
Remark 3.1 (Comparison with previous work).
The main difference from prior work [27] is that (Yt)t=1n(Y_{t})_{t=1}^{n} are no longer independent under either H0H_{0} or H1H_{1}.33
3
For theoretical analysis, we assume that 𝑷1:n\bm{P}_{1:n} is fixed but unknown. This simplification preserves the difficulty of the problem, as 𝑷1:n\bm{P}_{1:n} are still not observed. Under this assumption, [28] shows that (Yt)t=1n(Y_{t})_{t=1}^{n} are independent under both H0H_{0} and H1H_{1}. See Section 3.1 of [28] for a related discussion. The dependence arises from the two-level partition (Πζ,ΠY)(\Pi_{\zeta},\Pi_{Y}), which forces certain pseudorandom variables and pivotal statistics to be identical within groups. As a result, although each YtY_{t} still marginally follows μ0\mu_{0} under H0H_{0} or μ1,𝑷t\mu_{1,\bm{P}_{t}} under H1H_{1} when conditioning on (Πζ,ΠY)(\Pi_{\zeta},\Pi_{Y}), their joint distribution no longer factorizes across tt and instead follows the one described in Proposition 3.1. In short, pseudorandom collisions induce dependence among pivotal statistics, motivating our new formulation in (4) and the minimal-unit technique to properly address it.
At a high level, watermark detection under pseudorandomness collisions reduces to identifying distributional differences in (Yt)t=1n(Y_{t})_{t=1}^{n}, given the dependence structure specified by the two-layer partitions (Πζ,ΠY)(\Pi_{\zeta},\Pi_{Y}). By the factorization established in Proposition 3.1, it is both natural and sufficient to consider detection rules that assign score functions to each minimal unit and aggregate the resulting scores into a global test statistic.44
4
The log-likelihood ratio test also falls into this class, though it is typically impractical as it depends on the inaccessible NTP distributions.
Specifically, we propose and assign a score function h𝒱h_{{\mathcal{V}}} to every minimal unit 𝒱∈Π{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi, and write Y𝒱:=(Yt)t∈𝒱Y_{{\mathcal{V}}}:=(Y_{t})_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}} for the vector of pivotal statistics in 𝒱{\mathcal{V}}.
The detection rule then takes the form:
Tn={1,if ​Sn≥γn,α,0,otherwise,T_{n}=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }S_{n}\geq\gamma_{n,\alpha},\\
0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}
(5)
where the test statistic is defined as
Sn=∑𝒱∈Πh𝒱​(Y𝒱),S_{n}=\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}h_{{{\mathcal{V}}}}(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}}),
(6)
and γn,α\gamma_{n,\alpha} is the (1−α)(1-\alpha) quantile of SnS_{n} under H0H_{0}, ensuring Type I error control: ℙ0​(Sn≥γn,α)=α{\mathbb{P}}_{0}(S_{n}\geq\gamma_{n,\alpha})=\alpha.
In practice, γn,α\gamma_{n,\alpha} can be estimated via simulation, since the dependence structure of Y1:nY_{1:n} is fully characterized by the partitions (Πζ,ΠY)(\Pi_{\zeta},\Pi_{Y}), and each YtY_{t} marginally follows μ0\mu_{0} under the null.
3.3 Detection Efficiency and Optimal Scores
The central goal of this paper is to solve the hypothesis testing problem (4) optimally using detection rules of the form (5). To this end, we require a criterion or efficiency notion to quantify the performance of a given score function.
We follow the spirit of the asymptotic efficiency notion introduced by [26], which quantifies detection efficiency via the decay rate of the least favorable Type II error under a fixed Type I error level. Here, “least favorable” refers to the worst-case Type II error over a belief class 𝒫{\mathcal{P}}—a collection of plausible NTP distributions that the verifier assumes the true 𝑷t\bm{P}_{t} belongs to. This formulation reflects a practical constraint: the verifier does not have access to the true 𝑷t\bm{P}_{t} and must rely on prior knowledge or assumptions to evaluate efficiency.
However, this notion cannot be directly applied in our setting, as it relies on perfect pseudorandomness and thus assumes full independence among these YtY_{t}’s. To address this, we introduce a new non-asymptotic notion of efficiency that explicitly incorporates the dependencies induced by the partition Π\Pi.
Definition 3.3 (Non-asymptotic 𝒫\mathscr{P}-efficiency).
Let SnS_{n} be a test statistic computed from Y1:nY_{1:n} using a partition Π\Pi with Nn=|Π|N_{n}=|\Pi| minimal units. Let γn,α\gamma_{n,\alpha} denote the critical value corresponding to a Type I error level α\alpha. For a given family of belief classes 𝒫:={𝒫𝒱}𝒱∈Π\mathscr{P}:=\{{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}, the non-asymptotic 𝒫\mathscr{P}-efficiency of the test based on the score functions 𝐡={h𝒱}𝒱∈Π{\bm{h}}=\{h_{{\mathcal{V}}}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi} is defined as
Rn,𝒫(𝒉):=−1Nnsup𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫𝒱,∀𝒱logℙ1,𝑷𝒱(Sn≤γn,α),\displaystyle R_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}):=-\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}},\forall{\mathcal{V}}}\log{\mathbb{P}}_{1,\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(S_{n}\leq\gamma_{n,\alpha}),
(7)
where 𝐏𝒱:=(𝐏t)t∈𝒱\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}:=(\bm{P}_{t})_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}} collect the NTP distributions in the minimal unit 𝒱{\mathcal{V}}, 𝒫𝒱{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}} is the belief class associated with 𝒱{\mathcal{V}}, and the supremum is taken over all collections where each 𝐏𝒱⊆𝒫𝒱\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}} for all 𝒱∈Π{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi.
Remark 3.2 (Necessity of non-asymptotic efficiency).
Given the hash rule ζt=𝒜(w(t−m):(t−1),𝙺𝚎𝚢)\zeta_{t}={\mathcal{A}}({w}_{(t-m):(t-1)},{\mathtt{Key}}), the number of distinct text windows w(t−m):(t−1){w}_{(t-m):(t-1)} is bounded by |𝒲|m|{{\mathcal{W}}}|^{m}. Consequently, the total number of possible pseudorandom variables ζt\zeta_{t} is also bounded by |𝒲|m|{{\mathcal{W}}}|^{m}. Since different minimal units must correspond to different pseudorandom variables, the number of minimal units satisfies |Π|≤|𝒲|m|\Pi|\leq|{{\mathcal{W}}}|^{m}, which does not grow with the text length nn. This boundedness necessitates a non-asymptotic efficiency notion, as |Π||\Pi| cannot diverge with nn when |𝒲||{{\mathcal{W}}}| and mm are fixed.
There are two key differences between our efficiency notion and that of [27, Theorem 2.1].
First, Rn,𝒫​(𝒉)R_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}) is defined for finite nn and uses minimal units as the basic building blocks.
In contrast, the earlier notion is defined at the token level and only in the asymptotic regime as n→∞n\to\infty.
That special case corresponds to our framework when the partition is Π={{1},{2},…,{n}}\Pi=\{\{1\},\{2\},\dots,\{n\}\}, that is, one token per unit.
Second, our formulation allows different belief classes to be assigned to different minimal units, and each minimal unit can have its own score function.
This flexibility enables us to evaluate a broader range of detection rules and better reflect practical scenarios.
By contrast, the efficiency notion of [27] requires a single belief class and a single score function across all tokens, which is less expressive.
Assumption 3.3.
We assume that
(i)
(Independence structure)
Either Assumption 3.1 or 3.2 holds.
(ii)
(Bounded variance)
Let 𝒉={h𝒱}𝒱∈Π{\bm{h}}=\{h_{{\mathcal{V}}}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi} be the score functions, with each assigned to a minimal unit. We assume that the variances of {h𝒱​(Y𝒱)}𝒱∈Π\{h_{{\mathcal{V}}}(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}})\}_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi} are uniformly bounded under H0H_{0}.
(iii)
(Well posedness)
Let Bn,𝒫​(𝒉)B_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}) denote the non-asymptotic quantity defined in (9).
There exists a minimizer of the infimum over θ\theta that is bounded by a positive constant independent of both the partition Π\Pi and nn.
We pose a mild Assumption 3.3 to simplify the efficiency notion Rn,𝒫R_{n,\mathscr{P}}. The first condition of independence structure reflects the repetition-induced partition and has been discussed in Section 3.1: although dependence may persist within a block, some independence still holds across different blocks or sub-blocks. The second condition of bounded variance rules out pathological score functions with unbounded variability, and is satisfied in practice since the score functions we study even admit finite MGFs. The last condition of well-posedness ensures that the minimization problem in Bn,𝒫​(𝒉)B_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}) has stable solutions: the minimizer over θ\theta is uniformly bounded. Together, these assumptions require only mild regularity and do not limit the practical applicability of our framework.
Theorem 3.1 (Explicit lower bound for detection efficiency).
Let 𝒫={𝒫𝒱}𝒱∈Π\mathscr{P}=\{{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi} denote the family of belief classes, with one belief class assigned to each minimal unit.
Let ϕ𝐏𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta) denote the moment generating function (MGF) under the alternative H1H_{1} in (4), defined for any θ≥0\theta\geq 0 as
ϕ𝑷𝒱,h𝒱​(θ):=𝔼1,𝐏𝒱⁡[exp⁡(−θ​h𝒱​(Y𝒱))].\displaystyle\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta):=\EB_{1,\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}}[\exp(-\theta\,h_{{\mathcal{V}}}(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}}))].
(8)
Under Assumption 3.3, the non-asymptotic 𝒫\mathscr{P}-efficiency of the score functions 𝐡={h𝒱}𝒱∈Π{\bm{h}}=\{h_{{\mathcal{V}}}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi} is lower bounded by
Rn,𝒫​(𝒉)≥Bn,𝒫​(𝒉)−ωNn,R_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}})\geq B_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}})-\omega_{N_{n}},
where
Bn,𝒫(𝒉):=−infθ≥01Nn∑𝒱∈Π(θ𝔼0[h𝒱(Y𝒱)]+sup𝐏𝒱⊆𝒫𝒱logϕ𝐏𝒱,h𝒱(θ)),\displaystyle B_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}):=-\inf_{\theta\geq 0}\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\left(\theta\,\EB_{0}[h_{{{\mathcal{V}}}}(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}})]+\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{V}}}\log\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{{\mathcal{V}}}}}(\theta)\right),
(9)
and ωNn\omega_{N_{n}} is a deterministic function of NnN_{n} satisfying ωNn→0\omega_{N_{n}}\to 0 as Nn→∞N_{n}\to\infty.
Remark 3.3 (Asymptotic tightness).
Under further regularity conditions, the lower bound Bn,𝒫​(𝒉)B_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}) is asymptotically tight in the sense that |Rn,𝒫​(𝒉)−Bn,𝒫​(𝒉)|≤ωNn\left|R_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}})-B_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}})\right|\leq\omega_{N_{n}} for the same sequence ωNn\omega_{N_{n}} introduced in Theorem 3.1.
To prove this tightness, we develop a novel non-i.i.d. large-deviation bound.
See Theorem A.2 in the Supplementary Material for more details.
In Theorem 3.1, we lower bound Rn,𝒫​(𝒉)R_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}) by a more explicit quantity Bn,𝒫​(𝒉)B_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}), using the classical Chernoff bound. Setting θ=0\theta=0 further shows that Bn,𝒫​(𝒉)B_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}) is always non-negative.
Optimality via minimax optimization.
The lower bound Bn,𝒫​(𝒉)B_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}) provides a tractable approximation to the efficiency notion Rn,𝒫​(𝒉)R_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}) and admits an explicit form suitable for analysis. In particular, identifying the optimal score functions reduces to solving the minimax optimization problem that max𝒉⁡Bn,𝒫​(𝒉)\max_{\bm{h}}B_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}).
Since the expression of Bn,𝒫​(𝒉)B_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}) decomposes over minimal units, the overall optimization problem naturally separates into independent subproblems. Viewing each scaled score function θ​h𝒱\theta h_{{\mathcal{V}}} as a reparameterization, finding the optimal collection 𝒉={h𝒱}𝒱∈Π{\bm{h}}=\{h_{{{\mathcal{V}}}}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi} reduces to solving the following minimax problem for each minimal unit 𝒱{\mathcal{V}}:
h𝒱=argminhmax𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫𝒱L(h,𝑷𝒱),whereL(h,𝑷𝒱)=𝔼0[h(Y𝒱)]+log𝔼1,𝐏𝒱[exp(−h(Y𝒱))].h_{{{\mathcal{V}}}}=\arg\min_{h}\max_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}}L(h,\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}),\quad\text{where}\quad L(h,\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}})=\EB_{0}[h(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}})]+\log\EB_{1,\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}}[\exp(-h(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}}))].
(10)
The key difference from the previous formulation in [27, Equation 14] is that we now optimize over all NTP distributions 𝑷𝒱\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}} within each minimal unit 𝒱{\mathcal{V}}, which is necessary to capture the dependence induced by repetition in the two-level partition. In contrast, the previous setting corresponds to the non-repetition case where |𝒱|=1|{\mathcal{V}}|=1, which results in a significantly simpler minimax problem.
Following prior work, we adopt the Δ\Delta-regular class as our belief set for simplicity:
𝒫Δ={𝑷:maxw⁡Pw≤1−Δ}.\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{\Delta}=\left\{\bm{P}:\max_{w}P_{w}\leq 1-\Delta\right\}.
(11)
4 Application to the Gumbel-max Watermark
In this section, we apply our framework to the Gumbel-max watermarking scheme [1]. Recall that the Gumbel-max decoder can be equivalently written as
wt=𝒮gum​(𝑷t,ζt):=arg⁡maxw∈𝒲⁡log⁡Ut,wPt,w,{w}_{t}={\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{gum}}(\bm{P}_{t},\zeta_{t}):=\arg\max_{{w}\in{{\mathcal{W}}}}\frac{\log U_{t,{w}}}{P_{t,{w}}},
(12)
where {ζt}t=1n={(Ut,w)w∈𝒲}t=1n\{\zeta_{t}\}_{t=1}^{n}=\{(U_{t,{w}})_{{w}\in{{\mathcal{W}}}}\}_{t=1}^{n} denotes n×|𝒲|n\times|{{\mathcal{W}}}| i.i.d. replicates of standard uniform random variables U⁡(0,1)U(0,1). As shown in (12), the Gumbel-max trick ensures that the decoder samples exactly from the intended NTP distribution 𝑷t\bm{P}_{t}.
The pivotal statistic in this setting is given by Yt=Ygum​(wt,ζt)=Ut,wtY_{t}=Y^{\mathrm{gum}}(w_{t},\zeta_{t})=U_{t,w_{t}}, namely the coordinate of ζt=(Ut,w)w∈𝒲\zeta_{t}=(U_{t,{w}})_{{w}\in{{\mathcal{W}}}} corresponding to the chosen token wt{w}_{t}.
This choice satisfies the refined Assumption 3.2, implying that each minimal unit coincides with a sub-block.
Consequently, for a minimal unit 𝒱={t1,…,tk}{\mathcal{V}}=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\}, all pivotal statistics Yt1,…,YtkY_{t_{1}},\dots,Y_{t_{k}} collapse to the same value, so it is sufficient to consider only the unique representative, say Yt1Y_{t_{1}}.
Under the null H0H_{0}, this statistic still follows Unif⁡(0,1)\mathrm{Unif}(0,1), since repetition does not alter its marginal distribution.
We next derive its alternative distribution in the following lemma.
Lemma 4.1.
For the minimal unit 𝒱={t1,…,tk}{\mathcal{V}}=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\}, all pivotal statistics within the unit share the same value, that is, Yt1=⋯=YtkY_{t_{1}}=\cdots=Y_{t_{k}}.
Let 𝐏𝒱=(𝐏t)t∈𝒱\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}=(\bm{P}_{t})_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}} denote their corresponding NTP distributions. Then, the alternative distribution of the shared pivotal statistic is given by
ℙ1,𝑷𝒱​(Yt1≤y|Yt1=⋯=Ytk)=∑w∈𝒲Sw​y1/Sw∑w∈𝒲Sw​where​Sw=(∑w′≠wmaxt∈𝒱⁡Pt,w′Pt,w+1)−1.{\mathbb{P}}_{1,\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(Y_{t_{1}}\leq y\,|\,Y_{t_{1}}=\cdots=Y_{t_{k}})=\frac{\sum_{w\in{{\mathcal{W}}}}S_{w}y^{1/S_{w}}}{\sum_{w\in{{\mathcal{W}}}}S_{w}}~\text{where}~S_{w}=\left(\sum_{w^{\prime}\neq w}\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}\frac{P_{t,w^{\prime}}}{P_{t,w}}+1\right)^{-1}.
(13)
The alternative distribution of Yt1Y_{t_{1}} is considerably more complex, as it depends on the NTP distributions of all tokens wt1,…,wtkw_{t_{1}},\ldots,w_{t_{k}} within the minimal unit 𝒱{\mathcal{V}}. This added dependence makes the analysis far more difficult than in [27]. In their case, with |𝒱|=1|{\mathcal{V}}|=1, the alternative CDF 𝑷↦ℙ1,𝑷​(Yt1≤y)\bm{P}\mapsto{\mathbb{P}}_{1,\bm{P}}(Y_{t_{1}}\leq y) is convex for every y∈[0,1]y\in[0,1], a property central to their analysis. By contrast, in our setting the mapping (𝑷t1,…,𝑷tk)↦ℙ1,𝑷𝒱​(Yt1≤y∣Yt1=⋯=Ytk)(\bm{P}_{t_{1}},\ldots,\bm{P}_{t_{k}})\mapsto{\mathbb{P}}_{1,\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(Y_{t_{1}}\leq y\mid Y_{t_{1}}=\cdots=Y_{t_{k}}) is highly non-convex, introducing a unique challenge that requires new analytical tools. We will show how we address this difficulty in Section 7.1.
To apply our framework, we evaluate the detection performance of score functions 𝒉={h𝒱}𝒱∈Π{\bm{h}}=\{h_{{\mathcal{V}}}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi} using the non-asymptotic Rn,𝒫R_{n,\mathscr{P}}-efficiency defined in Definition 3.3. Here, 𝒫\mathscr{P} assigns to each minimal unit a (potentially different) Δ\Delta-regular class 𝒫Δ{\mathcal{P}}_{\Delta} for the prior belief, as introduced in (11). To identify the optimal score functions, we focus on saddle point solutions of the minimax problem (10). For a minimal unit 𝒱{\mathcal{V}}, a pair (h⋆,𝑷𝒱⋆)(h^{\star},\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\star}) is called a saddle point solution of the minimax problem minh⁡max𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫Δ⁡L⁡(h,𝑷𝒱)\min_{h}\max_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{\Delta}}L(h,\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}) if and only if L⁡(h⋆,𝑷𝒱)≤L⁡(h⋆,𝑷𝒱⋆)≤L⁡(h,𝑷𝒱⋆)L(h^{\star},\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}})\leq L(h^{\star},\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\star})\leq L(h,\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\star}) holds for any score hh and 𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫Δ\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{\Delta}, where 𝑷𝒱⋆\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\star} is the set of least-favorable NTP distributions and h⋆h^{\star} is the corresponding optimal score function. We adopt this notion of optimality in line with the robust hypothesis testing literature [19, 49, 12], where saddle point solutions often provide both interpretability and explicit analytical forms. The following theorem specifies when such saddle point solutions exist and gives their explicit forms when they do.
Theorem 4.1 (Trichotomy of the saddle point solution).
Fix a sub-block 𝒱{\mathcal{V}}. There exist constants 0<Δ1⋆≤Δ2⋆<120<\Delta_{1}^{\star}\leq\Delta_{2}^{\star}<\tfrac{1}{2}, depending only on 𝒱{\mathcal{V}}, such that the minimax problem in (10) with belief class 𝒫Δ{\mathcal{P}}_{\Delta} admits a saddle point solution that falls into one of the following three regimes.
1.
Low-regularity regime (Δ∈[0,Δ1⋆])(\Delta\in[0,\Delta_{1}^{\star}])
A unique saddle point solution exists. The optimal score function is the weighted-log rule:
h𝒱gum​(y)=(|𝒱|∧|𝒲|)​Δ(|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1)​(1−Δ)​log⁡y.h_{{{\mathcal{V}}}}^{\mathrm{gum}}(y)=\frac{(|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|)\,\Delta}{(|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1)(1-\Delta)}\,\log y.
(14)
2.
Intermediate regime (Δ∈(Δ1⋆,Δ2⋆))(\Delta\in(\Delta_{1}^{\star},\Delta_{2}^{\star}))
In this range, the minimax problem in (10) does not admit a saddle point solution.
3.
High-regularity regime (Δ∈[Δ2⋆,12))(\Delta\in[\Delta_{2}^{\star},\tfrac{1}{2}))
A unique saddle point solution exists. The optimal score function takes the least-favorable form:
h𝒱gum​(y)=log⁡(yΔ1−Δ+y1−ΔΔ).h_{{{\mathcal{V}}}}^{\mathrm{gum}}(y)=\log\left(y^{\frac{\Delta}{1-\Delta}}+y^{\frac{1-\Delta}{\Delta}}\right).
(15)
Remark 4.1 (Beyond saddle point solutions).
Saddle point solutions are a strong form of optimality, offering both interpretability and explicit analytical forms. If we relax this requirement and do not insist that the optimal score function be part of a saddle point pair, then a solution always exists in the intermediate regime. However, this solution is not associated with any least-favorable NTP distribution and does not admit a closed-form expression. A detailed discussion is provided in Supplementary B.8.
Discussion of the trichotomy.
Theorem 4.1 reveals a trichotomy that reflects a transition in the existence and form of saddle point solutions as the regularity level Δ\Delta varies. In the low- and high-regularity regimes (Δ∉(Δ1⋆,Δ2⋆)\Delta\notin(\Delta_{1}^{\star},\Delta_{2}^{\star})), a saddle point solution exists and yields closed-form optimal score functions. Specifically, the expression in (15) coincides with the least-favorable solution identified in [27, Theorem 3.2], which is designed to perform optimally against the least-favorable NTP distribution in 𝒫Δ{\mathcal{P}}_{\Delta}. Meanwhile, the form in (14) resembles a weighted-log score and arises in the low-regularity regime, where the alternative distribution remains close to the null. In contrast, the intermediate regime Δ∈(Δ1⋆,Δ2⋆)\Delta\in(\Delta_{1}^{\star},\Delta_{2}^{\star}) admits no saddle point solution, as the minimax problem is not convex–concave and the stability conditions required for a solution break down. A more detailed discussion is provided later.
Figure 3: Left: Transaction thresholds Δ1⋆\Delta_{1}^{\star} (in red) and Δ2⋆\Delta_{2}^{\star} (in blue) as functions of |𝒱|∧|𝒲||{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|. The gray region marks the intermediate regime, where no saddle point solution exists. Right: Illustration of why no saddle point solution exists when |𝒱|=3≤|𝒲||{\mathcal{V}}|=3\leq|{{\mathcal{W}}}|.
For Δ\Delta in the low- and high-regularity regimes, each optimal score (h𝑺Δ⋆h_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}} or h𝑷Δ⋆h_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}}) corresponds to a specific distribution vector (𝑺Δ⋆{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star} or 𝑷Δ⋆\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}).
In the intermediate regime, no distribution aligns with either score, so no saddle point solution arises.
Effects of |𝒱||{\mathcal{V}}|.
When |𝒱|=1|{\mathcal{V}}|=1, no repetition occurs, and our optimal score function reduces to the rule in [28], which is exactly the least-favorable rule in (15). When |𝒱|≥2|{\mathcal{V}}|\geq 2, the role of |𝒱||{\mathcal{V}}| becomes more nuanced. While the least-favorable rule in (15) remains unaffected by |𝒱||{\mathcal{V}}|, the weighted-log rule in (14) incorporates |𝒱||{\mathcal{V}}| through the factor |𝒱|∧|𝒲||{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|, which weakly determines the effective regularity level assigned to each sub-block. In addition, |𝒱||{\mathcal{V}}| influences the transition thresholds Δ1⋆\Delta_{1}^{\star} and Δ2⋆\Delta_{2}^{\star} that govern the trichotomy. As illustrated in the left panel of Figure 3, increasing |𝒱|∧|𝒲||{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}| increases both Δ1⋆\Delta_{1}^{\star} and Δ2⋆\Delta_{2}^{\star}, thereby shrinking the intermediate regime that lacks a saddle point solution. This “gray region” eventually vanishes as the informativeness of each block grows.
Justification for discarding repeated pivotal statistics.
Theorem 4.1 shows that for the Gumbel-max watermark, the optimal score function for each minimal unit depends only on its unique pivotal statistic. This makes sense since all pivotal statistics within a minimal unit 𝒱{\mathcal{V}} take the same value, with only the size |𝒱||{\mathcal{V}}| contributing limited additional information. A key implication is that practical heuristics [14, 50, 9] that discard repeated pivotal statistics incur little information loss, as the optimal rule itself follows this principle. Furthermore, once repetitions are removed, the remaining pivotal statistics can be safely treated as i.i.d., which helps improve the alignment between empirical and theoretical Type I errors [14]. Our analysis thus offers a theoretical justification for this widely used practice.
Practical suggestion.
Since no saddle point solution exists when Δ∈(Δ1⋆,Δ2⋆)\Delta\in(\Delta_{1}^{\star},\Delta_{2}^{\star}), one may choose any preferred score function in practice. When |𝒱|=1|{\mathcal{V}}|=1, the thresholds collapse to Δ1⋆=Δ2⋆=0\Delta_{1}^{\star}=\Delta_{2}^{\star}=0, so the least-favorable rule in (15) applies directly. Empirical evidence [27, Figure 1] suggests that many practical scenarios fall into small-Δ\Delta regimes. Consequently, when |𝒱|≥2|{\mathcal{V}}|\geq 2, Theorem 4.1 often recommends the weighted-log rule.
A practical benefit of our framework over previous one [27] is its separation across minimal units, which allows different regularity levels to be assigned to different units. In our LLM experiments, we find that choosing Δ\Delta carefully—for example, setting Δ=1−maxw⁡Pw\Delta=1-\max_{{w}}P_{w}, where PwP_{w} is the underlying NTP distribution—often improves performance. In practice, however, 1−maxw⁡Pw1-\max_{{w}}P_{w} is typically unknown and must be estimated from related models or tasks. Such estimation can introduce inaccuracies and, in turn, reduce detection efficiency.
Why the saddle point solution does not exist.
We now briefly explain why no saddle point solution exists in the intermediate regime. To formalize this, we reparameterize the minimax problem in (10) as min⁡sup𝑺∈𝒟Δh⁡L⁡(h,𝑺)\min_{h}\sup_{{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}}L(h,{\bm{S}}), where 𝑺{\bm{S}} denotes the reparameterized distribution vector and 𝒟Δ{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta} its domain. If a saddle point solution existed, there would be a pair (h⋆,𝑺⋆)(h^{\star},{\bm{S}}^{\star}) such that L⁡(h⋆,𝑺)≤L⁡(h⋆,𝑺⋆)≤L⁡(h,𝑺⋆)L(h^{\star},{\bm{S}})\leq L(h^{\star},{\bm{S}}^{\star})\leq L(h,{\bm{S}}^{\star}) holds for all hh and 𝑺∈𝒟Δ{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}, where 𝑺⋆{\bm{S}}^{\star} is the least-favorable distribution vector and h⋆h^{\star} the corresponding optimal score function. Our analysis in Section 7.1 establishes two key facts. First, h⋆h^{\star} must be the log-likelihood ratio score associated with 𝑺⋆{\bm{S}}^{\star}. Second, 𝑺⋆{\bm{S}}^{\star} must be either 𝑺Δ⋆{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star} or 𝑷Δ⋆\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star} (see Lemma 7.5 for their closed forms). However, when Δ∈(Δ1⋆,Δ2⋆)\Delta\in(\Delta_{1}^{\star},\Delta_{2}^{\star}), 𝑺Δ⋆{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star} fails to maximize the loss for its own log-likelihood ratio score, while 𝑷Δ⋆\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star} fails for the same reason, so neither candidate consistently dominates the other. As a result, no saddle point solution exists in this regime. See the right panel of Figure 3 for an illustration and Section 7.1 for a proof sketch.
5 Application to the Inverse Transform Watermark
In this section, we apply the framework to the
inverse transform watermark [24].
Recall that its decoder is defined as
wt=𝒮inv​(𝑷t,ζt):=πt−1​(F−1​(Ut,πt)),{w}_{t}={\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{inv}}(\bm{P}_{t},\zeta_{t}):=\pi_{t}^{-1}(F^{-1}(U_{t};\pi_{t})),
where the pseudorandom number ζt=(πt,Ut)\zeta_{t}=(\pi_{t},U_{t}) with Ut∼U⁡(0,1)U_{t}\sim U(0,1) and πt\pi_{t} being sampled uniformly at random from all permutations on 𝒲{{\mathcal{W}}}. Its pivotal statistic is defined as
Ytinv=|Ut−η⁡(πt​(wt))|,whereη⁡(w):=w−1|𝒲|−1,Y^{\mathrm{inv}}_{t}=|U_{t}-\eta(\pi_{t}({w}_{t}))|,\quad\text{where}\quad\eta(w):=\frac{w-1}{|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1},
maps a discrete token index to the interval [0,1][0,1] to enable direct comparison with Ut∼U⁡(0,1)U_{t}\sim U(0,1).
The problem is inherently intricate, shown in prior work [27], because the combinatorial structure introduced by the permutation πt\pi_{t} significantly complicates the analysis. In our setting, this challenge is further intensified by the fact that we only have block-level independence rather than the stronger sub-block independence.
Indeed, under the pivotal function rule Yinv​(w,ζ)=|U−η⁡(π⁡(w))|Y^{\mathrm{inv}}(w,\zeta)=|U-\eta(\pi({w}))|, if ζ=(U,π)\zeta=(U,\pi) is shared within a block, then the pivotal statistics computed across different tokens ww in the block remain dependent. This violates the sub-block independence in Assumption 3.2.
As a result, the minimal units are entire blocks for the inverse transform watermark, not sub-blocks as in the Gumbel-max watermark. These introduce two layers of complexity: the same combinatorial challenges from πt\pi_{t}, and the potentially arbitrary dependence within each block. Together, these make the analysis substantially more challenging than in the Gumbel-max case.
To address these challenges, we slightly modify the efficiency measure by adopting an asymptotic perspective in which the vocabulary size tends to infinity. This adjustment leads to a significantly simpler characterization of both the null and alternative distributions, as shown in Theorem 5.1. It also enables us to manage within-block dependence more effectively: in the asymptotic regime, the joint distribution of pivotal statistics within a block is governed by a set of independent latent variables. As a result, the within-block dependence structure becomes much more tractable, allowing for a straightforward derivation of the optimal score function. See Theorem 5.2 for details.
5.1 Asymptotic Distributions
In the following, we focus our analysis on a minimal unit (or a block) ℐkζ{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta} for some index kk, which consists of mkm_{k} sub-blocks denoted by {ℐk,ℓY}ℓ=1mk\{{\mathcal{I}}_{k,\ell}^{Y}\}_{\ell=1}^{m_{k}}. By definition, we have ℐkζ=⋃ℓ=1mkℐk,ℓY.{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta}=\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{m_{k}}{\mathcal{I}}_{k,\ell}^{Y}.
Our results are asymptotic in nature and follow the convention in prior work [27], which studies an asymptotic efficiency by letting the vocabulary size |𝒲||{{\mathcal{W}}}| tend to infinity. To enable this analysis, we introduce a comparable set of regularity conditions on the NTP distributions.
Assumption 5.1 (Asymptotic NTP conditions).
Let Pt,(i)P_{t,(i)} denote the ii-th largest probability in the NTP distribution 𝐏t\bm{P}_{t}. We assume that
(i)
Regular NTP distributions There exists a universal constant δ>0\delta>0 and a sequence {Δt}t≥1⊆(0,1)\{\Delta_{t}\}_{t\geq 1}\subseteq(0,1) such that for all t≥1t\geq 1,
𝑷t∈𝒫¯Δt,where𝒫¯Δ:={𝑷:δ≤P(1)≤1−Δ,P(2)≤ε|𝒲|},\bm{P}_{t}\in\overline{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Delta_{t}},\quad\text{where}\quad{\overline{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Delta}}:=\left\{\bm{P}:\delta\leq P_{(1)}\leq 1-\Delta,\quad P_{(2)}\leq{\varepsilon}_{|{{\mathcal{W}}}|}\right\},
(16)
and ε|𝒲|{\varepsilon}_{|{{\mathcal{W}}}|} satisfies log⁡|𝒲|⋅ε|𝒲|→0\log|{{\mathcal{W}}}|\cdot{\varepsilon}_{|{{\mathcal{W}}}|}\to 0 as |𝒲|→∞|{{\mathcal{W}}}|\to\infty.
(ii)
Heavy repeated tokens All tokens in non-singleton minimal units are heavy, meaning that each token has the largest probability in its corresponding NTP distribution. That is, for any t∈ℐℓYt\in{\mathcal{I}}_{\ell}^{Y} (for some ℓ\ell) and mk>1m_{k}>1, we have Pt,wℓ=Pt,(1)P_{t,w_{\ell}}=P_{t,(1)}.
We briefly elaborate on Assumption 5.1.
Condition (i) extends the Δ\Delta-regular class defined in (11), and a similar condition is adopted by [27, Equation (24)]. As |𝒲|→∞|{{\mathcal{W}}}|\to\infty, the second-largest probabilities Pt,(2)P_{t,(2)} vanish uniformly, implying that each 𝑷t\bm{P}_{t} becomes asymptotically concentrated on a single token. This assumption simplifies the theoretical analysis while remaining realistic; [27, Figure 1] finds that practical NTP distributions are typically dominated by a single token.
Condition (ii) follows naturally from (i). Since 𝑷t\bm{P}_{t} asymptotically assigns non-negligible probability to a single token, that token is almost surely the one generated by the LLM, and thus must be the so-called heavy token. Importantly, this condition also aids the dependence analysis within a block: because the verifier lacks access to the NTP distributions during detection, and the same tokens in the same sub-block may come from distinct NTP distributions, assuming a heavy token allows us to use a single index Δt\Delta_{t} to represent 𝑷t\bm{P}_{t} in the asymptotic regime. This strategy—also employed by [27]—substantially simplifies the analysis while preserving essential asymptotic behavior.
Since tokens are identical within each sub-block and distinct across different sub-blocks, we let w1,…,wmkw_{1},\ldots,w_{m_{k}} denote the unique tokens corresponding to each sub-block.
With Assumption 5.1 in place, the following lemma establishes the asymptotic joint distribution of (U,η⁡(π⁡(w1)),…,η⁡(π⁡(wmk)))(U,\eta(\pi(w_{1})),\ldots,\eta(\pi(w_{m_{k}}))) under both H0H_{0} and H1H_{1}.
Lemma 5.1 (Asymptotic joint distribution of pseudorandomness and tokens).
Suppose Assumptions 3.1 and 5.1 hold. Fix a minimal unit ℐkζ{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta} from the partition Πζ\Pi_{\zeta} (Definition 3.1) with mkm_{k} sub-blocks {ℐk,ℓY}ℓ=1mk\{{\mathcal{I}}^{Y}_{k,\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{m_{k}}. Define the block-wise regularity vector as
𝚫¯k:=(Δ¯k,1,…,Δ¯k,mk),whereΔ¯k,ℓ:=maxt∈ℐk,ℓY⁡Δt.\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}}_{k}:=({\bar{\Delta}}_{k,1},\ldots,{\bar{\Delta}}_{k,m_{k}}),\quad\text{where}\quad{\bar{\Delta}}_{k,\ell}:=\max_{t\in{\mathcal{I}}^{Y}_{k,\ell}}\Delta_{t}.
(17)
As |𝒲|→∞|{{\mathcal{W}}}|\to\infty, the joint distribution of (U,η⁡(π⁡(w1)),…,η⁡(π⁡(wmk)))(U,\eta(\pi(w_{1})),\ldots,\eta(\pi(w_{m_{k}}))) converges weakly as follows.
•
Under H0H_{0}, (U,η⁡(π⁡(w1)),…,η⁡(π⁡(wmk)))→𝑑(U,X1,…,Xmk)(U,\eta(\pi(w_{1})),\ldots,\eta(\pi(w_{m_{k}})))\xrightarrow{d}(U,X_{1},\ldots,X_{m_{k}}), where U,X1,…,XmkU,X_{1},\ldots,X_{m_{k}} are i.i.d. Unif⁡(0,1)\mathrm{Unif}(0,1).
•
Under H1H_{1}, if Pt,(1)=1−ΔtP_{t,(1)}=1-\Delta_{t} for all t∈ℐkζt\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta}, (U,η⁡(π⁡(w1)),…,η⁡(π⁡(wmk)))→𝑑(U,X1,…,Xmk)(U,\eta(\pi(w_{1})),\ldots,\eta(\pi(w_{m_{k}})))\xrightarrow{d}(U,X_{1},\ldots,X_{m_{k}}), where X1,…,XmkX_{1},\ldots,X_{m_{k}} are i.i.d. Unif⁡(0,1)\mathrm{Unif}(0,1), and UU is independent and uniformly distributed on
[maxℓ∈[mk]Δ¯k,ℓXℓ,minℓ∈[mk](1−Δ¯k,ℓ+Δ¯k,ℓXℓ)],\left[\max_{\ell\in[m_{k}]}{\bar{\Delta}}_{k,\ell}X_{\ell},\quad\min_{\ell\in[m_{k}]}(1-{\bar{\Delta}}_{k,\ell}+{\bar{\Delta}}_{k,\ell}X_{\ell})\right],
(18)
conditioned on this interval being non-empty.
Surprisingly, the asymptotic distributions of (U,η⁡(π⁡(w1)),…,η⁡(π⁡(wmk)))(U,\eta(\pi(w_{1})),\ldots,\eta(\pi(w_{m_{k}}))) take simple forms under both H0H_{0} and H1H_{1}. Under H0H_{0}, the pseudorandom variable UU is independent of the normalized token vector (η⁡(π⁡(w1)),…,η⁡(π⁡(wmk)))(\eta(\pi(w_{1})),\ldots,\eta(\pi(w_{m_{k}}))), whose entries are all i.i.d. Unif⁡(0,1)\mathrm{Unif}(0,1). In contrast, under H1H_{1}, the pseudorandom value UU becomes dependent on the token vector due to the block structure specified by ℐkζ={ℐk,ℓY}ℓ=1mk{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta}=\{{\mathcal{I}}_{k,\ell}^{Y}\}_{\ell=1}^{m_{k}}. Specifically, UU is independently drawn from the interval in (18), which itself depends on the token vector, provided the interval is non-empty. This conditional dependence reflects the watermark signal embedded in the generation process.
Recall that for each sub-block ℐk,ℓY{\mathcal{I}}_{k,\ell}^{Y}, the corresponding pivotal statistic is defined as Yk,ℓ:=|U−η⁡(π⁡(wℓ))|Y_{k,\ell}:=|U-\eta(\pi(w_{\ell}))| for ℓ=1,…,mk\ell=1,\ldots,m_{k}. By applying a careful change-of-variable argument, we can then characterize the asymptotic joint distribution of the vector 𝒀k=(Yk,1,…,Yk,mk)\bm{Y}_{k}=(Y_{k,1},\ldots,Y_{k,m_{k}}) under both hypotheses, as stated in the following theorem.
Theorem 5.1 (Asymptotic joint distribution of pivotal statistics).
Under the same notions and assumptions of Lemma 5.1, let 𝐘k=(Yk,1,…,Yk,mk)\bm{Y}_{k}=(Y_{k,1},\ldots,Y_{k,m_{k}}) denote the vector of unique pivotal statistics within the block ℐkζ{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta}, where Yk,ℓY_{k,\ell} represents the pivotal statistic within the sub-block ℐk,ℓY{\mathcal{I}}^{Y}_{k,\ell}.
Then, as |𝒲|→∞|{{\mathcal{W}}}|\to\infty, the joint PDF of 𝐘k\bm{Y}_{k} converges as follows.
•
Under H0H_{0}, the limiting null PDF is
f0​(𝒚)=∫012|I1​(u)|​𝟏I2​(u)=∅​𝑑u,\displaystyle f_{0}(\bm{y})=\int_{0}^{1}2^{|I_{1}(u)|}{\bf 1}_{I_{2}(u)=\emptyset}\,{\mathrm{d}}u,
(19)
where for a fixed vector 𝒚=(y1,…,ymk)\bm{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m_{k}}) and u∈[0,1]u\in[0,1],
I1​(u):={ℓ∈[mk]:0<yℓ<min⁡(u,1−u)},I2​(u):={ℓ∈[mk]:yℓ≥max⁡(u,1−u)}.\displaystyle I_{1}(u):=\left\{\ell\in[m_{k}]:0<y_{\ell}<\min(u,1-u)\right\},\quad I_{2}(u):=\left\{\ell\in[m_{k}]:y_{\ell}\geq\max(u,1-u)\right\}.
•
Under H1H_{1}, the limiting alternative PDF is
f𝚫¯k​(𝒚)=1Imk​(𝚫¯k)​∑𝝈∈{−1,1}mk(B𝝈𝚫¯k​(𝒚)−A𝝈𝚫¯k​(𝒚))+,\displaystyle f_{\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}}_{k}}(\bm{y})=\frac{1}{I_{m_{k}}(\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}}_{k})}\sum_{\bm{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{m_{k}}}\left(B_{\bm{\sigma}}^{\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}}_{k}}(\bm{y})-A_{\bm{\sigma}}^{\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}}_{k}}(\bm{y})\right)_{+},
(20)
where for each sign vector 𝝈=(σ1,…,σmk)∈{−1,1}mk\bm{\sigma}=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{m_{k}})\in\{-1,1\}^{m_{k}} and input 𝒚=(y1,…,ymk)\bm{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m_{k}}),
L𝝈​(𝒚)\displaystyle L_{\bm{\sigma}}(\bm{y})
:=maxℓ∈[mk]⁡(−σℓ​yℓ),\displaystyle:=\max_{\ell\in[m_{k}]}(-\sigma_{\ell}y_{\ell}),
U𝝈​(𝒚)\displaystyle U_{\bm{\sigma}}(\bm{y})
:=minℓ∈[mk]⁡(1−σℓ​yℓ),\displaystyle:=\min_{\ell\in[m_{k}]}(1-\sigma_{\ell}y_{\ell}),
Y𝝈+​(𝒚)\displaystyle Y^{+}_{\bm{\sigma}}(\bm{y})
:=(maxℓ:σℓ=1Δ¯k,ℓ1−Δ¯k,ℓ⋅yℓ)+,\displaystyle:=\left(\max_{\ell:\sigma_{\ell}=1}\frac{{\bar{\Delta}}_{k,\ell}}{1-{\bar{\Delta}}_{k,\ell}}\cdot y_{\ell}\right)_{+},

with (x)+:=max⁡(x,0)(x)_{+}:=\max(x,0), and the normalization constant Imk​(𝚫¯k)I_{m_{k}}(\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}}_{k}) is given by
Imk(𝚫¯k):=∫[0,1]mk(minℓ∈[mk]{1−Δ¯k,ℓ+Δ¯k,ℓxℓ}−maxℓ∈[mk]{Δ¯k,ℓxℓ})+dx1⋯dxmk.I_{m_{k}}(\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}}_{k}):=\int_{[0,1]^{m_{k}}}\left(\min_{\ell\in[m_{k}]}\{1-{\bar{\Delta}}_{k,\ell}+{\bar{\Delta}}_{k,\ell}x_{\ell}\}-\max_{\ell\in[m_{k}]}\{{\bar{\Delta}}_{k,\ell}x_{\ell}\}\right)_{+}{\mathrm{d}}x_{1}\cdots{\mathrm{d}}x_{m_{k}}.
As a special case, when mk=1m_{k}=1, the block ℐkζ{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta} contains no repeated tokens. In this setting, the PDFs in Theorem 5.1 simplify significantly and recover the previous non-repetitive results in [27, Theorem 4.1], as shown in the following corollary.
Corollary 5.1 (Case mk=1m_{k}=1).
Consider a minimal unit ℐkζ{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta} consisting of a single sub-block. In this case, the parameter vector 𝚫¯k\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}}_{k} reduces to a scalar Δk,1\Delta_{k,1}.
The normalization constant from Theorem 5.1 simplifies to I1​(𝚫¯k)=1−Δk,1I_{1}(\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}}_{k})=1-\Delta_{k,1}. The asymptotic PDF for the single pivotal statistic Yk,1Y_{k,1} reduce to:
•
Under H0H_{0}, the PDF of Yk,1Y_{k,1} is a triangular distribution on [0,1][0,1]:
f0​(y1)=2​(1−y1)​𝟏0≤y1≤1\displaystyle f_{0}(y_{1})=2(1-y_{1}){\bf 1}_{0\leq y_{1}\leq 1}
•
