={21−Δk,1−2​y1(1−Δk,1)2,if ​0<y1<1−Δk,1,0,otherwise.\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\dfrac{2}{1-\Delta_{k,1}}-\dfrac{2y_{1}}{(1-\Delta_{k,1})^{2}},&\text{if }0<y_{1}<1-\Delta_{k,1},\\[5.0pt]
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}
(21)
5.2 Optimal Score Function
As shown in Theorem 5.1, when the vocabulary size |𝒲||{{\mathcal{W}}}| tends to infinity, the joint distributions of the unique pivotal statistics within each minimal unit simplify significantly under both H0H_{0} and H1H_{1}. To incorporate this effect in our framework, we replace the original class-dependent efficiency from Definition 3.3 with its asymptotic counterpart, defined over the new class 𝒫¯Δ{\overline{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Delta}} introduced in (16). Specifically, we consider the following asymptotic efficiency, denoted by R¯n,𝒫\bar{R}_{n,\mathscr{P}} and defined by
R¯n,𝒫​(𝒉):=lim inf|𝒲|→∞Rn,𝒫​(𝒉)≥lim inf|𝒲|→∞Bn,𝒫​(𝒉)−ωNn,\bar{R}_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}):=\liminf_{|{{\mathcal{W}}}|\to\infty}R_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}})\geq\liminf_{|{{\mathcal{W}}}|\to\infty}B_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}})-\omega_{N_{n}},
(22)
where 𝒫\mathscr{P} assigns the new class 𝒫¯Δ{\overline{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Delta}} (with potentially different values of Δ\Delta) to the minimal units, and ωNn\omega_{N_{n}} is a vanishing term tending to zero as the number of minimal units Nn→∞N_{n}\to\infty (by Theorem 3.1).
To identify the optimal score functions, we adopt the same strategy as in the analysis of the Gumbel-max watermark. The quantity lim inf|𝒲|→∞Bn,𝒫​(𝒉)\liminf\limits_{|{{\mathcal{W}}}|\to\infty}B_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}), introduced in (22) and defined in (9), retains its additive structure across minimal units. The main difference is that the null and alternative distributions are now replaced by their asymptotic limits, as established in Theorem 5.1. Thus, the problem reduces to optimizing the score function for each minimal unit individually. If we assign 𝒫¯Δ𝒱\overline{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Delta_{{\mathcal{V}}}} to a minimal unit 𝒱{\mathcal{V}}, we obtain the following minimax optimization problem, which parallels the structure of (10),
h𝒱=argminhsupΔ𝒱≤𝚫¯≤1−δL′(h,𝚫¯),whereL′(h,𝚫¯)=𝔼f0[h(𝐘k)]+log𝔼f𝚫¯[exp(−h(𝐘k))],h_{{{\mathcal{V}}}}=\arg\min_{h}\sup_{\Delta_{{\mathcal{V}}}\leq\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}}\leq 1-\delta}L^{\prime}(h,\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}}),\quad\text{where}\quad L^{\prime}(h,\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}})=\EB_{f_{0}}[h(\bm{Y}_{k})]+\log\EB_{f_{\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}}}}[\exp(-h(\bm{Y}_{k}))],
(23)
where f0f_{0} and f𝚫¯f_{\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}}} denote the asymptotic PDFs of the vector of pivotal statistics 𝒀k=(Yk,1,…,Yk,mk)\bm{Y}_{k}=(Y_{k,1},\ldots,Y_{k,m_{k}}) under the null and alternative, respectively, as given in Theorem 5.1. Here, 𝚫¯≥Δ𝒱\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}}\geq\Delta_{{\mathcal{V}}} means that every entry of 𝚫¯\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}} is at least Δ𝒱\Delta_{{\mathcal{V}}}, and the notation 𝚫¯≤1−δ\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}}\leq 1-\delta is defined analogously.
We then characterize the optimal score functions that maximize R¯n,𝒫\bar{R}_{n,\mathscr{P}}-efficiency (up to the infinitesimal error ωNn\omega_{N_{n}}), as stated in the following theorem.
Theorem 5.2.
Suppose Assumptions 3.1, 3.3 (ii), and 5.1 hold.
Fix a block 𝒱=ℐkζ{\mathcal{V}}={\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta} consisting of mkm_{k} minimal units, and assume that 𝒫\mathscr{P} assigns the class 𝒫¯Δ𝒱\overline{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Delta_{{\mathcal{V}}}} with Δ𝒱∈(0,1)\Delta_{{\mathcal{V}}}\in(0,1) to 𝒱{\mathcal{V}}.
Define
h𝒱inv​(𝒚):=log⁡f𝚫¯𝒱​(𝒚)f0​(𝒚)with𝒚=(y1,…,ymk),𝚫¯𝒱=(Δ𝒱,…,Δ𝒱)∈ℝmk,\displaystyle h_{{{\mathcal{V}}}}^{\mathrm{inv}}(\bm{y}):=\log\frac{f_{\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\bm{y})}{f_{0}(\bm{y})}\quad\text{with}\quad\bm{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m_{k}}),\quad\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}=(\Delta_{{\mathcal{V}}},\ldots,\Delta_{{\mathcal{V}}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m_{k}},
(24)
where f0f_{0} and f𝚫¯𝒱f_{\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}} denote the asymptotic null and alternative PDFs, respectively, as given in Theorem 5.1.
The score functions {h𝒱inv}𝒱∈Π\{h_{{{\mathcal{V}}}}^{\mathrm{inv}}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi} maximizes the R¯n,𝒫\bar{R}_{n,\mathscr{P}}-efficiency defined in (22), in the sense that
limM→∞R¯n,𝒫​({[h𝒱inv][−M,M]}𝒱∈Π)=∞,\lim_{M\to\infty}\bar{R}_{n,\mathscr{P}}\left(\{[h_{{{\mathcal{V}}}}^{\mathrm{inv}}]_{[-M,M]}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\right)=\infty,
where [⋅][−M,M][\cdot]_{[-M,M]} denotes the clipping operator onto the interval [−M,M][-M,M].
As shown in Theorem 5.2, the optimal score function takes the form of a log-likelihood ratio between the asymptotic null and alternative PDFs. This result generalizes the previous result of [27, Theorem 4.2], which corresponds to the special case mk=1m_{k}=1, where each block consists of only a single sub-block.
Notably, the efficiency at the rule h𝒱invh_{{{\mathcal{V}}}}^{\mathrm{inv}} diverges to infinity. This arises because the null and alternative PDFs f0f_{0} and f𝚫¯f_{\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}}} differ on their supports, causing the KL divergence (which is essentially the optimal efficiency) to diverge.
In practice, although the asymptotic regime |𝒲|→∞|{{\mathcal{W}}}|\to\infty only holds approximately, the score function h𝒱invh_{{{\mathcal{V}}}}^{\mathrm{inv}} still performs well—particularly when the regularity level Δ𝒱\Delta_{{\mathcal{V}}} is adaptively selected.
6 Experiments
This section highlights the effectiveness of our framework through synthetic and real-data experiments and shows the practical utility of our proposed methods under pseudorandom collision. All the experiment codes are at https://github.com/lx10077/WatermarkCollision.
6.1 Synthetic Studies
Experimental setup.
We deliberately introduce repetition to evaluate Type I and Type II errors under pseudorandom collisions.
We set the vocabulary size to |𝒲|=103|{{\mathcal{W}}}|=10^{3}. At each step tt, with probability 0.90.9, a new token is generated according to the considered watermarking scheme. Specifically, we first sample Δt∼Unif⁡(10−3,Δmax)\Delta_{t}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(10^{-3},\Delta_{\max}) for a prespecified Δmax∈(0,1)\Delta_{\max}\in(0,1), and then independently construct an NTP distribution 𝑷t\bm{P}_{t} satisfying maxw∈𝒲⁡𝑷t,w=1−Δt\max_{{w}\in{{\mathcal{W}}}}\bm{P}_{t,{w}}=1-\Delta_{t}. The NTP distribution interpolates between a Zipf law [54] and the uniform distribution,55
5
See Algorithm 1 in the appendix of [28] for details. with Δmax\Delta_{\max} controlling its degree of randomness or entropy.
With the remaining probability 0.10.1, we introduce repetition through two independent mechanisms. With probability 0.050.05, we insert a segment sampled from a growing pool of previously used segments, which is updated whenever a new segment is generated or observed. With another 0.050.05, we copy a contiguous block from the generated prefix: draw a length L∈{1,…,Lmax}L\in\{1,\ldots,L_{\max}\} with Lmax=5L_{\max}=5, select a valid start uniformly, and replicate the block as the next output. Since the repeat decision is independent of the mechanism, this yields a decoupled corruption setup, enabling direct comparisons of Type I and Type II errors across score functions. The simulation results for Δmax=0.7\Delta_{\max}=0.7 are shown in Figure 4, while further implementation details and additional results for other values of Δmax\Delta_{\max} are provided in Supplementary D.
Figure 4: Type I errors (left) and Type II errors (right, log scale) on synthetic datasets for the Gumbel-max watermark (top) and the inverse-transform watermark (bottom).
Our new detection rules are denoted by hnew,Δh_{\mathrm{new},\Delta}. Here, “raw” or “deduped” indicates that the detection rule is applied to raw or unique pivotal statistics.
Type I error.
From the first column of Figure 4, existing rules, when directly applied to raw data, fail to control Type I error: at α=0.01\alpha=0.01, their empirical errors (light curves) hover around 0.030.03, well above the nominal level. This inflation arises because repeated pivotal statistics are double-counted as independent evidence, inflating the effective sample size and understating variance. In our setup, about 15%–20% of the data is repeated on average. We then evaluate the same detection rules, together with our proposed rule in Theorem 4.1 and the new rule in Theorem 5.2, after removing repetitions while regenerating until the sequence length is maintained. In contrast, these methods (darker curves) control Type I error well, with only natural random fluctuation. These results show that treating minimal units as the basic unit is effective for controlling Type I errors.
Type II error.
From the second column of Figure 4, we find that detection rules, when applied to deduplicated data, achieve Type II errors that are comparable to, and sometimes smaller than, those on raw data. For example, for the Gumbel-max watermark, harsh_{\mathrm{ars}} performs slightly better once repetitions are removed. Both of our proposed detection rules also perform on par with existing state-of-the-art methods: for Gumbel-max, hnew,0.005h_{\mathrm{new},0.005} behaves similarly to harsh_{\mathrm{ars}}, while for the inverse-transform watermark, hnew,0.01h_{\mathrm{new},0.01} matches the performance of hdif,0.01h_{\mathrm{dif},0.01} from [27]. These findings indicate that modest levels of repetition do not substantially degrade Type II errors or the detection power, as the watermark signal embedded in unique data is already strong. Another reason is that we set a uniform Δ\Delta across all minimal units, which may limit potential gains from explicitly modeling repetition.
Figure 5: Type I errors (left) and Type II errors (right, log scale) on C4 dataset for the Gumbel-max watermark (top) and the inverse-transform watermark (bottom).
Our new detection rules are denoted by hnewh_{\mathrm{new}}. Here, “sur” and “oracle” indicate that the Δ\Delta-values are approximated or computed using the ground truth.
6.2 Real-World Examples
Next, we conduct an empirical analysis of the detection performance of different watermark detection methods on text sequences generated by the language model, OPT-1.3B [52]. We evaluate Type I errors using 2000 human-written samples from the C4 news-like dataset [42]. To assess Type II errors, we randomly sample prompts from the same dataset, feed them to the model, and let it generate continuations. To ensure a fair evaluation based on unique pivotal statistics, we continue generating until each generated sentence contains at least 300 unique pivotal statistics (or minimal units). This approach guarantees a sufficient number of valid statistics, regardless of the total sequence length. The remaining experimental setup follows [27] and is detailed in Supplementary Material E for completeness.
The empirical Type I (left) and Type II errors (right) are presented in Figure 5.
The score functions hgum,0.05h_{\mathrm{gum},0.05} and hdif,0.1h_{\mathrm{dif},0.1} are the two methods proposed in [27], while harsh_{\mathrm{ars}} and hnegh_{\mathrm{neg}} serve as baseline scores introduced in their original works [1, 24].
Across most scenarios, all detection methods maintain Type I errors between 0.006 and 0.014, closely aligning with the nominal 0.01 level.
This result is consistent with expectations, as the deduplicated pivotal statistics can be regarded as i.i.d., allowing conventional detection methods to remain effective — a phenomenon also observed in [14, 50].
To further demonstrate the advantage of our new framework, we consider two approaches for computing the Δ\Delta-values for each minimal unit.
The first, denoted as “oracle,” uses the ground-truth NTP distributions to compute the regularity level Δ𝒱=1−maxt∈𝒱⁡maxw​Pt,w\Delta_{{\mathcal{V}}}=1-\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}\max_{{w}}P_{t,{w}} for the minimal unit 𝒱{\mathcal{V}}.
Since the ground-truth NTP distributions are inaccessible in practice, we introduce a practical surrogate: for a given text, we feed it directly into the detection model (OPT-1.3B in our setup) and autoregressively estimate the NTP distributions.
Although this surrogate approximation omits the preceding context and initial prompt, it still yields a reasonably accurate estimate of Δ\Delta. See the red solid curve for hnewh_{\mathrm{new}} “sur”.
Remarkably, even with this rough approximation, our proposed methods consistently outperform previous state-of-the-art approaches.
Furthermore, when oracle Δ\Delta-values are available, our methods demonstrate a clear and substantial advantage, underscoring the effectiveness of our framework in adaptively selecting Δ\Delta for each minimal unit.
7 Proof of Main Results
In this section, we provide proof sketches for Theorems 4.1 and 5.2, with the proofs of technical lemmas deferred to the Supplementary Material.
7.1 Proof of Theorem 4.1
Fix a minimal unit 𝒱={t1,…,tk}{\mathcal{V}}=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\} and, without loss of generality, let Y𝒱:=Yt1Y_{{\mathcal{V}}}:=Y_{t_{1}}. To facilitate analysis, we reparameterize the alternative distribution F𝑺F_{{\bm{S}}} of Y𝒱Y_{{\mathcal{V}}} in terms of a vector 𝑺{\bm{S}} rather than the original NTP distributions 𝑷𝒱\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}. This step is motivated by the fact that, as shown in Lemma 4.1, the mapping 𝑷𝒱↦F𝑺\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\mapsto F_{{\bm{S}}} is non-convex, making direct optimization over 𝑷𝒱\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}} intractable. In contrast, the mapping 𝑺↦F𝑺{\bm{S}}\mapsto F_{{\bm{S}}} yields a much simpler structure that is more amenable to analysis.
Specifically, under this parameterization, the alternative distribution takes the form F𝑺​(y)=∑wSw​y1/Sw∑w′Sw′F_{{\bm{S}}}(y)=\sum_{w}\frac{S_{w}y^{1/S_{w}}}{\sum_{w^{\prime}}S_{w^{\prime}}}, where each SwS_{w} is a nonlinear transformation of 𝑷𝒱\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}} (see (13)).
With this reparameterization, we revisit the minimax problem in (10), which now is expressed as
L(h,𝑺)=𝔼0[h(Yt1)]+log𝔼F𝐒[e−h⁡(Yt1)]=∫h(y)F0(dy)+log∫e−h⁡(y)F𝐒(dy),L(h,{\bm{S}})=\EB_{0}[h(Y_{t_{1}})]+\log\EB_{F_{\bm{S}}}[{\mathrm{e}}^{-h(Y_{t_{1}})}]=\int h(y)F_{0}({\mathrm{d}}y)+\log\int{\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}F_{{\bm{S}}}({\mathrm{d}}y),
(25)
where F0F_{0} and F𝑺F_{{\bm{S}}} denote the null and alternative distributions of Yt1Y_{t_{1}}, respectively.
Let 𝒟Δ{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta} denote the set of all feasible 𝑺{\bm{S}} vectors induced by 𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫Δ\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{\Delta}. Our goal is reduced to identify a saddle point pair (h⋆,𝑺⋆)(h^{\star},{\bm{S}}^{\star}) that solves the following minimax problem:
minhmax𝑺∈𝒟ΔL(h,𝑺)=∫h(y)F0(dy)+log∫e−h⁡(y)F𝑺(dy).\displaystyle\min_{h}\max_{{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}}L(h,{\bm{S}})=\int h(y)F_{0}({\mathrm{d}}y)+\log\int{\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}F_{{\bm{S}}}({\mathrm{d}}y).
(26)
The function L⁡(h,𝑺)L(h,{\bm{S}}) is convex in the score function hh for a fixed 𝑺{\bm{S}}, but generally not concave or convex in 𝑺{\bm{S}} when hh is fixed and |𝒱|>1|{\mathcal{V}}|>1. As a result, this renders standard minimax tools unusable, and so even the existence of a solution is not guaranteed. We begin by characterizing when a saddle point solution exists in Lemma 7.1.
Lemma 7.1 (Necessity of optimal score functions).
Let h𝐒=log⁡(d​F𝐒/d​y)h_{\bm{S}}=\log({\mathrm{d}}F_{\bm{S}}/{\mathrm{d}}y) denote the loglikelihood ratio with respect to the alternative distribution F𝐒F_{\bm{S}}.
The saddle point pair (h⋆,𝐒⋆)(h^{\star},{\bm{S}}^{\star}) solves the minimax problem (26) if and only if there exists a vector 𝐒⋆∈𝒟Δ{\bm{S}}^{\star}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta} such that h⋆=h𝐒⋆h^{\star}=h_{{\bm{S}}^{\star}} and
max𝑺∈𝒟Δ⁡L⁡(h𝑺⋆,𝑺)=L⁡(h𝑺⋆,𝑺⋆).\max_{{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}}L(h_{{\bm{S}}^{\star}},{\bm{S}})=L(h_{{\bm{S}}^{\star}},{\bm{S}}^{\star}).
(27)
The optimal objective value is −KL(F0∥F𝐒⋆)-\mathrm{KL}(F_{0}\|F_{{\bm{S}}^{\star}}) where F0=U⁡(0,1)F_{0}={U}(0,1) for the Gumbel-max watermark.
Lemma 7.1 implies that any optimal score function corresponding to a saddle point pair must be of the log-likelihood ratio form h𝑺h_{{\bm{S}}} for some 𝑺∈𝒟Δ{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}, and that such functions are always non-decreasing from Lemma 4.1. Hence, it suffices to restrict our attention to non-decreasing hh. A similar approach is used in [27], but while their feasible domain 𝒫Δ{\mathcal{P}}_{\Delta} is straightforward, our domain 𝒟Δ{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta} is substantially more complex, as shown in Lemma 7.2.
Lemma 7.2 (Properties of the domain 𝒟Δ{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}).
𝒟Δ{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta} is a permutation-invariant set.66
6
It means that for any permutation π∈Perm⁡(𝒲)\pi\in\mathrm{Perm}({{\mathcal{W}}}), the permuted vector π⁡(𝐒):=(Sπ⁡(w))w∈𝒲\pi({\bm{S}}):=(S_{\pi(w)})_{w\in{{\mathcal{W}}}} also belongs to 𝒟Δ{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}.
For any 𝐒=(Sw)w∈𝒲{\bm{S}}=(S_{w})_{w\in{{\mathcal{W}}}} in 𝒟Δ{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}, it follows that (i) 0≤Sw≤1−Δ0\leq S_{w}\leq 1-\Delta for any ww, (ii) ∑wSw≤1\sum_{w}S_{w}\leq 1, (iii) maxw⁡Sw1−Δ≤1−1−∑wSw|𝒱|∧|𝒲|\frac{\max_{w}S_{w}}{1-\Delta}\leq 1-\frac{1-\sum_{w}S_{w}}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|}, and (iv) 𝐒Δ⋆:=(1−Δ1+Δ|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1,0,⋯,0)∈𝒟Δ{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}:=(\frac{1-\Delta}{1+\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1}},0,\cdots,0)\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}.
Now, solving the minimax problem (26) reduces to identifying a feasible vector 𝑺⋆{\bm{S}}^{\star} that satisfies condition (27). This requires understanding both (i) the geometry of the feasible domain 𝒟Δ{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta} and (ii) how to achieve the maximum in the mapping 𝑺↦𝔼F𝐒⁡[e−h⁡(Yt1)]=∫e−h⁡(y)​F𝐒​(dy){\bm{S}}\mapsto\EB_{F_{{\bm{S}}}}[{\mathrm{e}}^{-h(Y_{t_{1}})}]=\int{\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}F_{\bm{S}}({\mathrm{d}}y) for any fixed y∈[0,1]y\in[0,1] and a given function hh.
The first issue is addressed in detail in Lemma 7.2, while the second issue can be approached by noting that the mapping is Schur-convex in 𝑺{\bm{S}}. In principle, the maximum of a Schur-convex function over a permutation-invariant domain typically occurs at its boundary.
Hence, both Lemmas 7.2 and 7.3 assist in solving the inner maximization problem in (26).
Definition 7.1 (Schur-convexity).
A function FF is Schur-convex if it is isotonic and preserves order. Specifically, if 𝐱{\bm{x}} is majorized by 𝐲{\bm{y}}, denoted by, 𝐱≤m𝐲{\bm{x}}\leq_{m}{\bm{y}}, then it must satisfy F⁡(𝐱)≤F⁡(𝐲)F({\bm{x}})\leq F({\bm{y}}).
For two vectors 𝐱,𝐲∈ℝd{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}, 𝐱≤m𝐲{\bm{x}}\leq_{m}{\bm{y}} if and only if (i) ∑i=1ky(i)≥∑i=1kx(i)\sum_{i=1}^{k}y_{(i)}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{k}x_{(i)} for all k=1,2,…,dk=1,2,\ldots,d with y(1)≥…≥y(d)y_{(1)}\geq\ldots\geq y_{(d)} and x(1)≥…≥x(d)x_{(1)}\geq\ldots\geq x_{(d)} the ordered entries and (ii) ∑i=1dxi=∑i=1dyi\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}y_{i}.
Lemma 7.3 (Schur-convexity).
For any non-decresing function hh, the map 𝐒↦∫e−h⁡(y)​F𝐒​(𝑑y){\bm{S}}\mapsto\int{\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}F_{\bm{S}}({\mathrm{d}}y) is Schur-convex in 𝐒{\bm{S}}.
Lemma 7.4 (Reduced domain).
Let ℋΔ={𝐒:1−Δ1+Δ|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1≤∑wSw}{\mathcal{H}}_{\Delta}=\{{\bm{S}}:\frac{1-\Delta}{1+\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1}}\leq\sum_{w}S_{w}\} be a half-space.
For any non-decreasing function hh,
max⁡∫𝑺∈𝒟Δ⁡e−h⁡(y)​F𝑺​(𝑑y)=max⁡∫𝑺∈𝒟Δ∩ℋΔ⁡e−h⁡(y)​F𝑺​(𝑑y).\max_{{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}}\int{\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}F_{\bm{S}}({\mathrm{d}}y)=\max_{{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{\Delta}}\int{\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}F_{\bm{S}}({\mathrm{d}}y).
Moreover, Lemma 7.4 shows that the maximum of the objective over the domain 𝒟Δ{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta} always lies within the half-space ℋΔ{\mathcal{H}}_{\Delta}. Consequently, any points in 𝒟Δ∖ℋΔ{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}_{\Delta} are suboptimal and can be safely excluded from consideration.
This reduction allows us to focus on the reduced domain 𝒟Δ∩ℋΔ{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{\Delta}. To proceed with the proof, we aim to characterize its convex envelope, which provides a tractable outer approximation while preserving all potential maximizers of the objective.
Lemma 7.5 (Convex envelope of 𝒟Δ∩ℋΔ{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{\Delta}).
We define new sets 𝒦Δ{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta} and ℰΔ{\mathcal{E}}_{\Delta} by
𝒦Δ={𝑺:∀w,0≤Sw≤1−Δ,1−Δ1+Δ|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1≤∑wSw≤1andmaxw⁡Sw1−Δ≤1−1−∑wSw|𝒱|∧|𝒲|},\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta}=\left\{{\bm{S}}:\forall w,0\leq S_{w}\leq 1-\Delta,~\frac{1-\Delta}{1+\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1}}\leq\sum_{w}S_{w}\leq 1~\text{and}~\frac{\max_{w}S_{w}}{1-\Delta}\leq 1-\frac{1-\sum_{w}S_{w}}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|}\right\},
ℰΔ:={π(𝑷Δ⋆),π(𝑺Δ⋆),∀π∈Perm(𝒲)},\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\Delta}:=\{\pi(\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}),\pi({\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}),~\forall\pi\in\mathrm{Perm}({{\mathcal{W}}})\},
where 𝐏Δ⋆:=(1−Δ,Δ,0,⋯,0)\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}:=\left(1-\Delta,\Delta,0,\cdots,0\right) and 𝐒Δ⋆:=(1−Δ1+Δ|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1,0,⋯,0){\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}:=(\frac{1-\Delta}{1+\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1}},0,\cdots,0).
With Δ∈(0,0.5]\Delta\in(0,0.5], then
1.
𝒦Δ{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta} is a convex polyhedron with extreme points given by ℰΔ{\mathcal{E}}_{\Delta}, that is, 𝒦Δ=conv⁡(ℰΔ){\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta}=\mathrm{conv}({\mathcal{E}}_{\Delta}).
2.
ℰΔ⊆𝒟Δ∩ℋΔ{\mathcal{E}}_{\Delta}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{\Delta}.
3.
𝒦Δ{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta} is the convex envelop of 𝒟Δ∩ℋΔ{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{\Delta}, that is, 𝒦Δ=conv⁡(𝒟Δ∩ℋΔ){\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta}=\mathrm{conv}({\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{\Delta}).
By Lemma 7.5, the convex envelope of 𝒟Δ∩ℋΔ{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{\Delta} is characterized by 𝒦Δ{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta}, which forms a convex polyhedron whose extreme points are explicitly known. This structure enables the inner maximization to be reduced to a binary comparison, leveraging permutation invariance and Schur-convexity. In particular, Lemma 7.6 shows that maximizing over this relaxed domain 𝒦Δ{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta} is straightforward.
Lemma 7.6 (Maximum over polyhedron).
Let the points 𝐏Δ⋆\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}, 𝐒Δ⋆{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}, and the set 𝒦Δ{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta} be as defined in Lemma 7.5. When Δ∈(0,0.5)\Delta\in(0,0.5), it follows that for any non-decreasing function hh,
max⁡∫𝑺∈𝒦Δ⁡e−h⁡(y)​F𝑺​(𝑑y)=max⁡{∫e−h⁡(y)​F𝑷Δ⋆​(𝑑y),∫e−h⁡(y)​F𝑺Δ⋆​(𝑑y)}.\max_{{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta}}\int{\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}F_{\bm{S}}({\mathrm{d}}y)=\max\left\{\int{\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}F_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}}({\mathrm{d}}y),\int{\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}F_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}}({\mathrm{d}}y)\right\}.
With all supporting lemmas established, we are ready to prove Theorem 4.1.
Proof of Theorem 4.1.
By Lemma 7.1, solving the minimax problem (26) by a saddle point reduces to obtaining a feasible solution 𝑺∈𝒟Δ{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta} that satisfies the optimality condition (27).
By Lemmas 7.4, 7.5, and 7.6, for any non-decreasing function hh,
max⁡∫𝑺∈𝒟Δ⁡e−h⁡(y)​F𝑺​(𝑑y)=max⁡{∫e−h⁡(y)​F𝑷Δ⋆​(𝑑y),∫e−h⁡(y)​F𝑺Δ⋆​(𝑑y)}.\max_{{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}}\int{\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}F_{\bm{S}}({\mathrm{d}}y)=\max\left\{\int{\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}F_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}}({\mathrm{d}}y),\int{\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}F_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}}({\mathrm{d}}y)\right\}.
This implies that the maximum is achieved by either 𝑺Δ⋆{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star} or 𝑷Δ⋆\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}. According to Lemma 7.1, if an optimal score function exists, it must be either h𝑺Δ⋆h_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}} or h𝑷Δ⋆h_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}}. Therefore, we verify whether either pair—(h𝑺Δ⋆,𝑺Δ⋆)(h_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}},{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}) or (h𝑷Δ⋆,𝑷Δ⋆)(h_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}},\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star})—solves the minimax problem (26).
•
If h𝑺Δ⋆h_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}} is the optimal score function, then it must satisfy L⁡(h𝑺Δ⋆,𝑺Δ⋆)≥L⁡(h𝑺Δ⋆,𝑷Δ⋆)L(h_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}},{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star})\geq L(h_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}},\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}).
This condition is equivalent to the inequality
1≥∫e−h𝑺Δ⋆​(y)​F𝑷Δ⋆​(𝑑y)=∫d​F𝑷Δ⋆d​F𝑺Δ⋆​d​F0,1\geq\int{\mathrm{e}}^{-h_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}}(y)}F_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}}({\mathrm{d}}y)=\int\frac{{\mathrm{d}}F_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}}}{{\mathrm{d}}F_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}}}{\mathrm{d}}F_{0},
(28)
which leads to an algebraic constraint. By numerically solving this condition, we identify the first valid parameter range: Δ∈[0,Δ1⋆)\Delta\in[0,\Delta_{1}^{\star}).
•
If h𝑷Δ⋆h_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}} is the optimal score function, it must satisfy L⁡(h𝑷Δ⋆,𝑷Δ⋆)≥L⁡(h𝑷Δ⋆,𝑺Δ⋆).L(h_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}},\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star})\geq L(h_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}},{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}).
This is equivalent to the inequality
1≥∫e−h𝑷Δ⋆​(y)​F𝑺Δ⋆​(𝑑y)=∫d​F𝑺Δ⋆d​F𝑷Δ⋆​d​F0.1\geq\int{\mathrm{e}}^{-h_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}}(y)}F_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}}({\mathrm{d}}y)=\int\frac{{\mathrm{d}}F_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}}}{{\mathrm{d}}F_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}}}{\mathrm{d}}F_{0}.
(29)
Numerically solving this condition yields the second valid range: Δ∈(Δ2⋆,0.5]\Delta\in(\Delta_{2}^{\star},0.5].
•
We always have Δ1⋆≤Δ2⋆\Delta_{1}^{\star}\leq\Delta_{2}^{\star} because the Chebyshev inequality ensures that the sum of the right-hand sides of both (28) and (29) is at least 22. This implies that the intervals [0,Δ1⋆)[0,\Delta_{1}^{\star}) and (Δ2⋆,0.5](\Delta_{2}^{\star},0.5] are disjoint, and hence Δ1⋆≤Δ2⋆\Delta_{1}^{\star}\leq\Delta_{2}^{\star}. By Lemma 7.1, no optimal score function exists when Δ∈(Δ1⋆,Δ2⋆)\Delta\in(\Delta_{1}^{\star},\Delta_{2}^{\star}). The gray region in Figure 3 highlights where this breakdown occurs.
∎
7.2 Proof of Theorem 5.2
Recall that 𝒫={𝒫¯Δ𝒱}𝒱∈Π\mathscr{P}=\{\overline{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Delta_{{\mathcal{V}}}}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}.
For the score functions 𝒉={h𝒱}𝒱∈Π{\bm{h}}=\{h_{{\mathcal{V}}}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}, it follows that
R¯n,𝒫​(𝒉)\displaystyle\bar{R}_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}})
≥(a)​lim inf|𝒲|→∞Bn,𝒫​(𝒉)−ωNn,\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\geq}\liminf_{|{{\mathcal{W}}}|\to\infty}B_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}})-\omega_{N_{n}},
≥(b)−infθ≥0lim sup|𝒲|→∞1Nn​∑𝒱∈Π(θ​𝔼0​[h𝒱​(Y𝒱)]+sup𝐏𝒱⊆𝒫¯Δ𝒱log⁡ϕ𝐏𝒱,h𝒱​(θ))−ωNn\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\geq}-\inf_{\theta\geq 0}\limsup_{|{{\mathcal{W}}}|\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\left(\theta\,\EB_{0}[h_{{\mathcal{V}}}(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}})]+\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq\overline{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Delta_{{\mathcal{V}}}}}\log\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)\right)-\omega_{N_{n}}

The last lower bound (30) separates over the scores of each sub-block, so it suffices to consider each subproblem individually.
Lemma 7.7 shows that, as |𝒲|→∞|{{\mathcal{W}}}|\to\infty, the objective function for each subproblem simplifies exactly to (23).
Its proof essentially exchanges the order of lim sup\limsup and sup\sup, and then applies the weak convergence result in Theorem 5.1.
Lemma 7.7 (Simplified limits).
For a minimal unit 𝒱=ℐkζ{\mathcal{V}}={\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta} containing mkm_{k} sub-blocks, we represent its associated pivotal statistics Y𝒱Y_{{\mathcal{V}}} as the vector 𝐘k=(Yk,1,…,Yk,mk)\bm{Y}_{k}=(Y_{k,1},\ldots,Y_{k,m_{k}}), where each component corresponds to a distinct sub-block. lim sup|𝒲|→∞(𝔼0⁡[h⁡(𝐘k)]+sup𝐏𝒱⊆𝒫¯Δ𝒱log⁡𝔼1,𝐏𝒱​[exp⁡(−h⁡(𝐘k))])=supΔ𝒱≤𝚫¯′≤1−δL′​(h,𝚫¯′),\limsup_{|{{\mathcal{W}}}|\to\infty}\left(\EB_{0}[h(\bm{Y}_{k})]+\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq\overline{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Delta_{{\mathcal{V}}}}}\log\EB_{1,\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}}[\exp(-h(\bm{Y}_{k}))]\right)=\sup_{\Delta_{{\mathcal{V}}}\leq\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}}^{\prime}\leq 1-\delta}L^{\prime}(h,\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}}^{\prime}),
where 𝚫¯′=(Δ1′,…,Δmk′)\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}}^{\prime}=(\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\Delta_{m_{k}}^{\prime}) is a regularity-level vector and L′L^{\prime} is given in (23).
Lemma 7.8.
Let h𝒱inv​(𝐲)=log⁡f𝚫¯𝒱​(𝐲)f0​(𝐲)h_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathrm{inv}}(\bm{y})=\log\frac{f_{\bm{{\bar{\Delta}}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\bm{y})}{f_{0}(\bm{y})} be defined as in (24). For any Δ𝒱∈(0,1)\Delta_{{\mathcal{V}}}\in(0,1), it follows that

where [⋅][−M,M][\cdot]_{[-M,M]} denotes the clipping operator onto the interval [−M,M][-M,M].
Finally, Theorem 5.2 is obtained by combining the lower bound (30) with Lemmas 7.7 and 7.8:
limM→∞R¯n,𝒫​({[h𝒱inv][−M,M]}𝒱∈Π)=∞.\lim_{M\to\infty}\bar{R}_{n,\mathscr{P}}(\{[h_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathrm{inv}}]_{[-M,M]}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi})=\infty.
8 Discussion
In this paper, we study how to optimally perform watermark detection under pseudorandomness collisions, a phenomenon arising from text repetition in both human-written and low-random LLM outputs. Our central idea is to capture the repetition structure through a hierarchical two-layer partition, identifying minimal units within which strong dependence exists but across which independence is preserved. Using these minimal units as basic components, we develop a new non-asymptotic efficiency measure for evaluating detection rules that take the form of sum-based scores over the minimal units. This formulation naturally casts the search for optimal detection rules as a minimax problem. We then apply our framework to two watermarking schemes—the Gumbel-max watermark and the inverse-transform watermark. For both schemes, we derive the corresponding optimal detection rules and show, both theoretically and empirically, that our rules enable valid Type I error control while achieving comparable or even higher detection power. Moreover, our framework provides a theoretical justification for the widely used heuristic of discarding repeated statistics. At a broader level, our contribution of incorporating pseudorandomness collisions into watermark analysis advances the development of statistical foundations for LLMs [48].
Building on this foundation, our work opens several promising directions for future research.
First, our framework empirically demonstrates the benefit of assigning different regularity levels Δ\Delta to different minimal units. Further efforts could focus on more accurately approximating the NTP distribution for a given text [25].
Second, our current analysis adopts a Δ\Delta-regular belief class of NTP distributions to represent the least favorable case. Exploring alternative or more refined belief classes may sharpen efficiency guarantees and yield stronger detection rules, particularly when the existing worst-case formulation is overly conservative.
Last, many downstream statistical tasks merit reexamination under pseudorandomness collisions. Examples include detection under human edits [26] and estimation of the proportion of watermarked tokens in AI-mixed text [28]. Both problems can be reformulated with minimal units as the basic component, offering a principled alternative to methods that still assume perfect pseudorandomness.
Beyond methodological development, our study also connects to a classical statistical problem called content authenticity.
Traditional approaches such as stylometry and authorship attribution identify an author’s linguistic fingerprints from stylistic patterns like word-length distributions or function-word usage [32, 34, 20, 45].
Plagiarism detection represents another related line, leveraging information-retrieval techniques to identify surface-level overlaps with existing corpora [31].
Watermark detection, however, is fundamentally distinct, as its objective is not to detect unconscious stylistic features or verbatim copies, but to verify the presence of a deliberately embedded statistical signal with explicitly specified properties [27].
This distinction makes the reliability of the underlying statistical dependence crucial—precisely the aspect that pseudorandomness collisions undermine. At the same time, our framework may inspire new revisitations of these classical authenticity problems, where one could deliberately embed structured dependence or repeated linguistic patterns via watermarking to enhance detectability and robustness in the era of generative AI.

Supplementary Material
This Supplementary Material contains the remaining proofs and technical details.
The proof that supports the general framework is collected in Section A.
The proofs about the Gumbel-max watermark are presented in Section B.
Section C includes the proofs of results for the inverse transform watermark.
Sections D and E contain experiment details for simulation and real-world examples, respectively.
Appendix A Proof for the General Framework
A.1 Proof of Theorem 3.1
Proof of Theorem 3.1.
By Markov’s inequality, it follows that for any θ≥0\theta\geq 0,
ℙ1​(Sn<γn,α)=ℙ1​(e−θ​Sn>e−θ​γn,α)≤eθ​γn,α​𝔼1​[e−θ​Sn].{\mathbb{P}}_{1}(S_{n}<\gamma_{n,\alpha})={\mathbb{P}}_{1}(e^{-\theta S_{n}}>e^{-\theta\gamma_{n,\alpha}})\leq e^{\theta\gamma_{n,\alpha}}\,\EB_{1}[{\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta S_{n}}].
Recall that Sn=∑𝒱∈Πh𝒱​(Y𝒱)S_{n}=\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}h_{{\mathcal{V}}}(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}}) and scores for each minimal unit h𝒱​(Y𝒱)h_{{\mathcal{V}}}(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}}) are independent.
It then follows that
𝔼1⁡[e−θ​Sn]=∏𝒱∈Πϕ𝐏𝒱,h𝒱​(θ).\EB_{1}[{\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta S_{n}}]=\prod_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta).
Taking logarithms yields
log⁡𝔼1​[e−θ​Sn]=∑𝒱∈Πlog⁡ϕ𝐏𝒱,h𝒱​(θ).\log\EB_{1}[{\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta S_{n}}]=\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\log\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta).
Thus, the Type II error satisfies
1−𝔼1⁡[Tn]≤exp⁡(θ​γn,α+∑𝒱∈Πlog⁡ϕ𝐏𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)).1-\EB_{1}[T_{n}]\leq\exp\!\left(\theta\gamma_{n,\alpha}+\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\log\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)\right).
Dividing by NnN_{n} (the total number of minimal units |Π||\Pi|), we have for any θ≥0\theta\geq 0,
(1−𝔼1⁡[Tn])1/Nn\displaystyle\left(1-\EB_{1}[T_{n}]\right)^{1/N_{n}}
≤exp⁡(θ​γn,αNn+1Nn​∑𝒱∈Πlog⁡ϕ𝑷𝒱,h𝒱​(θ))\displaystyle\leq\exp\!\left(\frac{\theta\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}+\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\log\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)\right)
≤exp⁡(θ​γn,αNn+1Nn​∑𝒱∈Πsup𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫𝒱log⁡ϕ𝑷𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)).\displaystyle\leq\exp\!\left(\frac{\theta\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}+\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}}\log\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)\right).
(31)
To proceed with the proof, we introduce a new quantity, denoted by Dn,𝒫​(𝒉)D_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}):
Dn,𝒫(𝒉):=−infθ≥0{θ⋅γn,αNn+1Nn∑𝒱∈Πsup𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫𝒱logϕ𝑷𝒱,h𝒱(θ)}.\displaystyle D_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}):=-\inf_{\theta\geq 0}\left\{\theta\cdot\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}+\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}}\log\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)\right\}.
(32)
Therefore, by taking the minimum with respect to θ≥0\theta\geq 0 in (31), we have that
exp⁡(−Rn,𝒫​(𝒉))=sup𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫𝒱,∀𝒱(1−𝔼1⁡[Tn])1/Nn\displaystyle\exp(-R_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}))=\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}},\forall{\mathcal{V}}}\left(1-\EB_{1}[T_{n}]\right)^{1/N_{n}}
≤exp⁡(infθ≥0{θ⋅γn,αNn+1Nn​∑𝒱∈Πsup𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫𝒱log⁡ϕ𝑷𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)})=exp⁡(−Dn,𝒫​(𝒉)),\displaystyle\quad\leq\exp\!\left(\inf_{\theta\geq 0}\left\{\theta\cdot\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}+\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}}\log\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)\right\}\right)=\exp(-D_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}})),
which implies that we have Rn,𝒫​(𝒉)≥Dn,𝒫​(𝒉)R_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}})\geq D_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}) for any scores 𝒉{\bm{h}}.
Lemma A.1.
Let Assumptions 3.3 (i) and (ii) hold with 0<Cvar<∞0<C_{\mathrm{var}}<\infty the uniform variance bound for each h𝒱​(Y𝒱)h_{{\mathcal{V}}}(Y_{\mathcal{V}}), that is, Var0​(h𝒱​(Y𝒱))≤Cvar\mathrm{Var}_{0}(h_{{\mathcal{V}}}(Y_{\mathcal{V}}))\leq C_{\mathrm{var}} for any minimal unit 𝒱{\mathcal{V}}.
It follows that for any α∈(0,1)\alpha\in(0,1),
|γn,αNn−μn|≤ε0=CvarNn⋅min⁡(α,1−α)whereμn=1Nn​∑𝒱∈Π𝔼0⁡[h𝒱].\displaystyle\left|\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}-\mu_{n}\right|\leq\varepsilon_{0}=\sqrt{\frac{C_{\mathrm{var}}}{N_{n}\cdot\min(\alpha,1-\alpha)}}\quad\text{where}\quad\mu_{n}=\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\EB_{0}[h_{{\mathcal{V}}}].
(33)
Lemma A.2.
Under Assumption 3.3, there exists a universal constant M¯>0\overline{M}>0, independent of nn and the partition Π\Pi, such that for any family of belief classes 𝒫={𝒫𝒱}𝒱∈Π\mathscr{P}=\{{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}, the optimal value of θ\theta in the definitions of both Dn,𝒫D_{n,\mathscr{P}} and Bn,𝒫B_{n,\mathscr{P}} lies within the interval [0,M¯][0,\overline{M}].
Recall that
Bn,𝒫(𝒉):=−infθ≥01Nn∑𝒱∈Π{θ𝔼0[h𝒱]+sup𝐏𝒱⊆𝒫𝒱logϕ𝐏𝒱,h𝒱(θ)}.B_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}):=-\inf_{\theta\geq 0}\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\left\{\theta\,\EB_{0}[h_{{\mathcal{V}}}]\,+\,\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}}\log\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)\right\}.
By Lemma A.2, there exists a universal constant M¯>0\overline{M}>0 that doesn’t depend on nn and Π\Pi such that the optimal θ\theta in the definition of both Dn,𝒫​(𝒉)D_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}) and Bn,𝒫​(𝒉)B_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}) are uniformly bounded above by M¯\overline{M}.
Combining this with Lemma A.1, we obtain the approximation bound
|Bn,𝒫​(𝒉)−Dn,𝒫​(𝒉)|≤ε0⋅M¯=Θ⁡(1Nn),\bigl|B_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}})-D_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}})\bigr|\,\leq\,{\varepsilon}_{0}\cdot\overline{M}\,=\,\Theta\!\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{n}}}\right),
(34)
where ε0{\varepsilon}_{0} is the approximation error from Lemma A.1,
and M¯\overline{M} is the bound from Lemma A.2.
∎
Finally, we provide the proofs of Lemma A.1 and Lemma A.2.
Proof of Lemma A.1.
Let μn=𝔼0⁡[Sn/Nn]\mu_{n}=\EB_{0}[S_{n}/N_{n}] denote the expectation of the score SnS_{n} under the null. By the definition of SnS_{n}, we have
μn=𝔼0⁡[1Nn​∑𝒱∈Πh𝒱​(Y𝒱)]=1Nn​∑𝒱∈Π𝔼0⁡[h𝒱​(Y𝒱)].\mu_{n}=\EB_{0}\left[\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}h_{{\mathcal{V}}}(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}})\right]=\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\EB_{0}[h_{{\mathcal{V}}}(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}})].
Since the minimal units are independent under H0H_{0}, the variance of Sn/NnS_{n}/N_{n} can be bounded as
Var0​(SnNn)=1Nn2​Var0​(Sn)=1Nn2​∑𝒱∈ΠVar0​(h𝒱​(Y𝒱)).\mathrm{Var}_{0}\left(\frac{S_{n}}{N_{n}}\right)=\frac{1}{N_{n}^{2}}\mathrm{Var}_{0}(S_{n})=\frac{1}{N_{n}^{2}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\mathrm{Var}_{0}(h_{{\mathcal{V}}}(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}})).
Using the uniform variance bound Var0​(h𝒱​(Y𝒱))≤Cvar\mathrm{Var}_{0}(h_{{\mathcal{V}}}(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}}))\leq C_{\mathrm{var}}, we have Var0​(SnNn)≤1Nn2​∑𝒱∈ΠCvar=CvarNn.\mathrm{Var}_{0}\left(\frac{S_{n}}{N_{n}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{N_{n}^{2}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}C_{\mathrm{var}}=\frac{C_{\mathrm{var}}}{N_{n}}.
Hence, by Chebyshev’s inequality, it follows that for any ε>0\varepsilon>0
ℙ0​(|SnNn−μn|≥ε)≤Var0​(Sn/Nn)ε2≤CvarNn​ε2.{\mathbb{P}}_{0}\left(\left|\frac{S_{n}}{N_{n}}-\mu_{n}\right|\geq\varepsilon\right)\leq\frac{\mathrm{Var}_{0}(S_{n}/N_{n})}{\varepsilon^{2}}\leq\frac{C_{\mathrm{var}}}{N_{n}\varepsilon^{2}}.
When we set
ε=CvarNn⋅min⁡(α,1−α).\varepsilon=\sqrt{\frac{C_{\mathrm{var}}}{N_{n}\cdot\min(\alpha,1-\alpha)}}.
This choice implies that CvarNn​ε02=min⁡(α,1−α)\frac{C_{\mathrm{var}}}{N_{n}\varepsilon_{0}^{2}}=\min(\alpha,1-\alpha). Therefore, we have:
•
ℙ0​(Sn/Nn≥μn+ε0)≤ℙ0​(|Sn/Nn−μn|≥ε0)≤min⁡(α,1−α)≤α{\mathbb{P}}_{0}(S_{n}/N_{n}\geq\mu_{n}+\varepsilon_{0})\leq{\mathbb{P}}_{0}(|S_{n}/N_{n}-\mu_{n}|\geq\varepsilon_{0})\leq\min(\alpha,1-\alpha)\leq\alpha.
•
ℙ0​(Sn/Nn≤μn−ε0)≤ℙ0​(|Sn/Nn−μn|≥ε0)≤min⁡(α,1−α)≤1−α{\mathbb{P}}_{0}(S_{n}/N_{n}\leq\mu_{n}-\varepsilon_{0})\leq{\mathbb{P}}_{0}(|S_{n}/N_{n}-\mu_{n}|\geq\varepsilon_{0})\leq\min(\alpha,1-\alpha)\leq 1-\alpha.
We now use these bounds to constrain γn,α\gamma_{n,\alpha}. By definition, ℙ0​(Sn≥γn,α)=α{\mathbb{P}}_{0}(S_{n}\geq\gamma_{n,\alpha})=\alpha.
For the upper bound, since ℙ0​(Sn≥(μn+ε0)​Nn)<α{\mathbb{P}}_{0}(S_{n}\geq(\mu_{n}+\varepsilon_{0})N_{n})<\alpha, it must be that the threshold γn,α\gamma_{n,\alpha} is smaller than (μn+ε0)​Nn(\mu_{n}+\varepsilon_{0})N_{n}. Thus,
γn,α≤(μn+ε0)​Nn⟹γn,αNn≤μn+ε0.\gamma_{n,\alpha}\leq(\mu_{n}+\varepsilon_{0})N_{n}\implies\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}\leq\mu_{n}+\varepsilon_{0}.
For the lower bound, by definition ℙ0​(Sn<γn,α)=1−α{\mathbb{P}}_{0}(S_{n}<\gamma_{n,\alpha})=1-\alpha.
Since ℙ0​(Sn<(μn−ε0)​Nn)<1−α{\mathbb{P}}_{0}(S_{n}<(\mu_{n}-\varepsilon_{0})N_{n})<1-\alpha, it must be that the threshold γn,α\gamma_{n,\alpha} is larger than (μn−ε0)​Nn(\mu_{n}-\varepsilon_{0})N_{n}. Thus,
γn,α≥(μn−ε0)​Nn⟹γn,αNn≥μn−ε0.\gamma_{n,\alpha}\geq(\mu_{n}-\varepsilon_{0})N_{n}\implies\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}\geq\mu_{n}-\varepsilon_{0}.
Combining the upper and lower bounds, we have:
μn−ε0≤γn,αNn≤μn+ε0,\mu_{n}-\varepsilon_{0}\leq\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}\leq\mu_{n}+\varepsilon_{0},
which is equivalent to:
|γn,αNn−μn|≤ε0=CvarNn⋅min⁡(α,1−α).\left|\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}-\mu_{n}\right|\leq\varepsilon_{0}=\sqrt{\frac{C_{\mathrm{var}}}{N_{n}\cdot\min(\alpha,1-\alpha)}}.
This completes the proof. The second part can be proved similarly.
∎
Proof of Lemma A.2.
The claim for Bn,𝒫B_{n,\mathscr{P}} follows directly from Assumption 3.3 (iii). We now prove the result for Dn,𝒫D_{n,\mathscr{P}}.
Let θB⋆\theta_{B}^{\star} and θD⋆\theta_{D}^{\star} denote the optimal values of θ\theta for Bn,𝒫B_{n,\mathscr{P}} and Dn,𝒫D_{n,\mathscr{P}}, respectively.
For any fixed θ≥0\theta\geq 0, define
bn,𝒫,𝒉​(θ)\displaystyle b_{n,\mathscr{P},{\bm{h}}}(\theta)
:=θ​𝔼0​[h𝒱]+1Nn​∑𝒱∈Πsup𝐏𝒱⊆𝒫𝒱log⁡ϕ𝐏𝒱,h𝒱​(θ),\displaystyle:=\theta\,\EB_{0}[h_{{\mathcal{V}}}]+\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}}\log\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta),
(35)
dn,𝒫,𝒉​(θ)\displaystyle d_{n,\mathscr{P},{\bm{h}}}(\theta)
:=θ​γn,αNn+1Nn​∑𝒱∈Πsup𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫𝒱log⁡ϕ𝑷𝒱,h𝒱​(θ).\displaystyle:=\frac{\theta\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}+\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}}\log\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta).
(36)
Both functions are convex in θ\theta and we have bn,𝒫,𝒉′​(θB⋆)=0b^{\prime}_{n,\mathscr{P},{\bm{h}}}(\theta_{B}^{\star})=0 and dn,𝒫,𝒉′​(θD⋆)=0d^{\prime}_{n,\mathscr{P},{\bm{h}}}(\theta_{D}^{\star})=0.
By Assumption 3.3 (iii), there exists a universal constant M¯>0\overline{M}>0, independent of Π\Pi, such that θB⋆<M¯\theta_{B}^{\star}<\overline{M} and bn,𝒫,𝒉′​(M¯)>c>0b^{\prime}_{n,\mathscr{P},{\bm{h}}}(\overline{M})>c>0 for some constant cc. Moreover, Lemma A.1 implies that when NnN_{n} is sufficiently large, we also have dn,𝒫,𝒉′​(M¯)>c/2>0d^{\prime}_{n,\mathscr{P},{\bm{h}}}(\overline{M})>c/2>0.
Therefore, for large enough NnN_{n}, the minimizer θD⋆\theta_{D}^{\star} must also satisfy θD⋆<M¯\theta_{D}^{\star}<\overline{M}, completing the proof.
∎
A.2 Asymptotic Tightness
In this subsection, we show that the lower bound in Theorem 3.1 is asymptotically tight under a set of standard regularity conditions. We first introduce the assumptions required for this result. The interpretation and justification of Assumption A.1 are in Section A.8.
Assumption A.1 (Regularity conditions for lower bound tightness).
We assume that
1.
(Finite maximizers) For each minimal unit 𝒱{\mathcal{V}} and all θ≥0\theta\geq 0, the supremum of the MGF over the belief class 𝒫𝒱{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}} is achieved on a finite subset 𝒫𝒱⋆⊆𝒫𝒱{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\star}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}:
sup𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫𝒱ϕ𝑷𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)=sup𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫𝒱⋆ϕ𝑷𝒱,h𝒱​(θ).\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}}\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)=\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\star}}\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta).
2.
(Informative scores) The score functions 𝒉={h𝒱}𝒱∈Π{\bm{h}}=\{h_{{\mathcal{V}}}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi} are informative in the sense that, for every minimal unit 𝒱{\mathcal{V}}, 𝔼0⁡[h𝒱​(Y𝒱)]<𝔼1,𝐏𝒱⁡[h𝒱​(Y𝒱)]\EB_{0}[h_{{\mathcal{V}}}(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}})]<\EB_{1,\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}}[h_{{\mathcal{V}}}(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}})] for all 𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫𝒱\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}.
3.
(CGF regularity) For all 𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫𝒱⋆\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\star}, the cumulant generating function log⁡ϕ𝑷𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)\log\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta) is well-defined and smooth on its domain. Moreover, for any compact set KK inside its domain, there exist constants 0<σmin2​(K),Ck​(K)<∞0<\sigma^{2}_{\min}(K),C_{k}(K)<\infty such that for all θ∈K\theta\in K:
(i)
σmin2​(K)≤d2d​θ2​log⁡ϕ𝑷𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)≤C2​(K)\sigma^{2}_{\min}(K)\leq\displaystyle\frac{{\mathrm{d}}^{2}}{{\mathrm{d}}\theta^{2}}\log\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)\leq C_{2}(K).
(ii)
|dkd​θk​log⁡ϕ𝑷𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)|≤Ck​(K)\displaystyle\left|\frac{{\mathrm{d}}^{k}}{{\mathrm{d}}\theta^{k}}\log\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)\right|\leq C_{k}(K) for k=3,4k=3,4.
4.
(Score density regularity)
The set of size-one minimal units is Π1:={𝒱∈Π:|𝒱|=1}\Pi_{1}:=\{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi:|{\mathcal{V}}|=1\}, representing all non-repetitive tokens. We assume that these units are sufficiently large and regular. In particular, there exist universal constants c,λ>0c,\lambda>0 and CB​V<∞C_{BV}<\infty such that, for all Nn>0N_{n}>0, the size of Π1\Pi_{1} satisfies |Π1|≥c​Nnλ|\Pi_{1}|\geq cN_{n}^{\lambda}. Furthermore, for any 𝒱∈Π1{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi_{1}, the score density has uniformly bounded total variation:
sup𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫𝒱⋆TV⁡(ρ𝑷𝒱)≤CB​V,\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\star}}\mathrm{TV}(\rho_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}})\leq C_{BV},
where ρ𝑷𝒱\rho_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}} denotes the alternative PDF of h𝒱​(Y𝒱)h_{{\mathcal{V}}}(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}}) under NTP distributions 𝑷𝒱\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}, and TV⁡(ρ):=∫−∞∞|ρ′​(x)|,𝑑x\mathrm{TV}(\rho):=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\rho^{\prime}(x)|,{\mathrm{d}}x is the total variation of ρ\rho.
The assumptions in Assumption A.1 are mild and largely standard in statistical analysis. The finite maximizer condition simply restricts attention to a finite representative subset of distributions without narrowing the generality of the belief class. The informativeness requirement ensures that the scores meaningfully distinguish between null and alternative distributions, which is fundamental for any detection framework. The regularity of the cumulant generating function (CGF) is a standard smoothness condition, guaranteeing that variance and higher-order moments remain controlled on compact sets. Finally, the score density regularity condition leverages the abundance of non-repetitive tokens in typical texts, making the growth and bounded-variation requirements natural and broadly satisfied in practice. Together, these conditions provide technical tractability while remaining weak enough to encompass a wide range of realistic scenarios. The verification of those assumptions in our case is in Section A.8.
Theorem A.1 (Formal version of Remark 3.3).
Suppose Assumptions 3.3 and A.1 hold. Then, the lower bound Bn,𝒫​(𝐡)B_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}), defined in (9), is asymptotically tight, in the sense that
|Rn,𝒫​(𝒉)−Bn,𝒫​(𝒉)|≤ωNn,\left|R_{n,{\mathcal{P}}}({\bm{h}})-B_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}})\right|\leq\omega_{N_{n}},
where ωNn\omega_{N_{n}} is a deterministic function of NnN_{n} satisfying ωNn→0\omega_{N_{n}}\to 0 as Nn→∞N_{n}\to\infty.
Proof of Theorem A.1.
Under Assumption 3.3, Theorem 3.1 guarantees that
Rn,𝒫​(𝒉)≥Bn,𝒫​(𝒉)−ωNn.R_{n,{\mathcal{P}}}({\bm{h}})\geq B_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}})-\omega_{N_{n}}.
To establish asymptotic tightness, it remains to prove the upper bound:
Rn,𝒫​(𝒉)≤Bn,𝒫​(𝒉)+ωNn,R_{n,{\mathcal{P}}}({\bm{h}})\leq B_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}})+\omega_{N_{n}},
under the additional Assumption A.1.
To this end, it suffices to show that
Rn,𝒫​(𝒉)≤Dn,𝒫​(𝒉)+ωNn,R_{n,{\mathcal{P}}}({\bm{h}})\leq D_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}})+\omega_{N_{n}},
(37)
where Dn,𝒫​(𝒉)D_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}) is the intermediate quantity defined in (32). Once this is shown, the result follows from the bound
|Dn,𝒫​(𝒉)−Bn,𝒫​(𝒉)|=Θ⁡(1Nn)|D_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}})-B_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}})|=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{n}}}\right)
established in (34), completing the proof.
To proceed with the proof, we then introduce some notations.
For each minimal unit 𝒱{\mathcal{V}}, the number of possible assignments 𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫𝒱⋆\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\star} is finite, given that |𝒫𝒱⋆||{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\star}| is finite.
We denote this finite collection of structured assignments by 𝒬⋆{\mathcal{Q}}^{\star}, defined as
𝒬⋆={{𝑷𝒱}𝒱∈Π:𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫𝒱⋆}.{\mathcal{Q}}^{\star}=\left\{\{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}:\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\star}\right\}.
Let 𝒬⋆={𝒬1⋆,…,𝒬K⋆}{\mathcal{Q}}^{\star}=\{{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}^{\star},\dots,{\mathcal{Q}}_{K}^{\star}\} be an enumeration of all such combinations, and define the probability simplex over {1,2,…,K}\{1,2,\dots,K\} by
Λ:={λ=(λ1,…,λK)∈ℝK:λi≥0,∑i=1Kλi=1}.\Lambda:=\left\{\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{K})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}:\lambda_{i}\geq 0,\ \sum_{i=1}^{K}\lambda_{i}=1\right\}.
Each element 𝒬i⋆∈𝒬⋆{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{\star}\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{\star} corresponds to a particular assignment of NTP distributions that potentially attains the supremum in sup𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫𝒱ϕ𝑷𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}}\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta). Specifically, we write 𝒬i⋆=(𝑸i,𝒱)𝒱∈Π{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{\star}=(\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}})_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}, where each minimal unit 𝒱{\mathcal{V}} is assigned the distributions 𝑸i,𝒱\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}}. Thus, the index i∈[K]i\in[K] indexes KK distinct type-wise configurations of NTP distributions across the entire partition Π\Pi.
Now, we are ready to prove this upper bound (37). It follows that
−Rn,𝒫​(𝒉)\displaystyle-R_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}})
=1Nn​sup𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫𝒱,∀𝒱log⁡𝔼1⁡(1−𝔼1⁡[Tn|{𝐏𝒱}𝒱]),\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}},\forall{\mathcal{V}}}\log\EB_{1}(1-\EB_{1}[T_{n}|\{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}}]),
≥(a)​maxλ∈Λ​1Nn​∑i=1Kλi​log⁡(1−𝔼1⁡[Tn∣{𝐏𝒱}𝒱=𝒬i⋆])\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\geq}\max_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{K}\lambda_{i}\log\left(1-\EB_{1}[T_{n}\mid\{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}}={\mathcal{Q}}^{\star}_{i}]\right)
(38)
≥(b)​maxλ∈Λ​1Nn​∑i=1Kλi​[minθ≥0⁡(θ⋅γn,αNn+1Nn​∑𝒱∈Πlog⁡ϕ𝑸i,𝒱,h𝒱​(θ))−αNn]\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\geq}\max_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{K}\lambda_{i}\left[\min_{\theta\geq 0}\left(\theta\cdot\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}+\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\log\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)\right)-\alpha_{N_{n}}\right]
(39)
=maxλ∈Λ⁡minθi≥0,∀i​1Nn​∑i=1Kλi​[(θi⋅γn,αNn+1Nn​∑𝒱∈Πlog⁡ϕ𝑸i,𝒱,h𝒱​(θi))−αNn]\displaystyle=\max_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\min_{\theta_{i}\geq 0,\forall i}\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{K}\lambda_{i}\left[\left(\theta_{i}\cdot\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}+\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\log\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta_{i})\right)-\alpha_{N_{n}}\right]
(40)
=(c)​minθi≥0,∀i​maxλ∈Λ​1Nn​∑i=1Kλi​[(θi⋅γn,αNn+1Nn​∑𝒱∈Πlog⁡ϕ𝑸i,𝒱,h𝒱​(θi))−αNn]\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{=}\min_{\theta_{i}\geq 0,\forall i}\max_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{K}\lambda_{i}\left[\left(\theta_{i}\cdot\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}+\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\log\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta_{i})\right)-\alpha_{N_{n}}\right]
(41)
Here, (a) follows from the fact that 𝒫𝒱⋆⊆𝒫𝒱{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\star}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}} and each 𝒬i⋆{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{\star} specifies a valid instance of {𝑷𝒱}𝒱∈Π\{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}; (b) applies Lemma A.3, which provides a non-asymptotic large deviation bound for independent but non-identically distributed random variables; and (c) uses the minimax theorem in Lemma A.4 to exchange the order of the maximum and the infimum (where we view (θ1,…,θK)(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{K}) as a new θ\theta to apply this lemma). The term αNn\alpha_{N_{n}} denotes a positive deterministic function of NnN_{n} that converges to zero as Nn→∞N_{n}\to\infty.
Consequently, we have
−Rn,𝒫​(𝒉)\displaystyle-R_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}})
≥(a)​minθ≥0​{θ⋅γn,αNn+1Nn​∑𝒱∈Πsup𝑷𝒱⋆⊆𝒫⋆log⁡ϕ𝑷𝒱⋆,h𝒱​(θ)}−αNn\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\geq}\min_{\theta\geq 0}\left\{\theta\cdot\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}+\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\star}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}^{\star}}\log\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\star},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)\right\}-\alpha_{N_{n}}
=(b)​minθ≥0​{θ⋅γn,αNn+1Nn​∑𝒱∈Πsup𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫𝒱log⁡ϕ𝑷𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)}−αNn.\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\min_{\theta\geq 0}\left\{\theta\cdot\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}+\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}}\log\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)\right\}-\alpha_{N_{n}}.
where (a) follows from the fact that the maximum over the simplex is attained at an extreme point, that is, there exists some i∈[K]i\in[K] such that each 𝑸i,𝒱\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}} in 𝒬i⋆=(𝑸i,𝒱)𝒱∈Π{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{\star}=(\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}})_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi} achieves the supremum sup𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫⋆log⁡ϕ𝑷𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}^{\star}}\log\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta); and (b) uses the condition that sup𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫𝒱ϕ𝑷𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)=sup𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫⋆ϕ𝑷𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}}\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)=\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}^{\star}}\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta) for all θ≥0\theta\geq 0.
As a result, we obtain the bound
−Rn,𝒫​(𝒉)\displaystyle-R_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}})
≥minθ≥0⁡{θ⋅γn,αNn+1Nn​∑𝒱∈Πsup𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫𝒱log⁡ϕ𝑷𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)}−αNn,\displaystyle\geq\min_{\theta\geq 0}\left\{\theta\cdot\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}+\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}}\log\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)\right\}-\alpha_{N_{n}},
(42)
which implies the upper bound
Rn,𝒫​(𝒉)≤Dn,𝒫​(𝒉)+αNn.R_{n,{\mathcal{P}}}({\bm{h}})\leq D_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}})+\alpha_{N_{n}}.
This completes the proof.
Lemma A.3 (Non-i.i.d. large deviation lower bound).
Let Assumptions 3.3 and A.1 hold, and let 𝒬i∗={𝐐i,𝒱}𝒱∈Π{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{*}=\{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi} denote a given assignment of NTP distributions. Then, we have
1Nn​log⁡(1−𝔼1⁡[Tn|{𝐏𝒱}𝒱=𝒬i⋆])≥minθ≥0⁡{θ⋅γn,αNn+∑𝒱∈Π1Nn​log⁡ϕ𝑸i,𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)}−αNn,\frac{1}{N_{n}}\log\left(1-\EB_{1}\left[T_{n}~|~\{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}}={\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{\star}\right]\right)\geq\min_{\theta\geq 0}\left\{\theta\cdot\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}+\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\frac{1}{N_{n}}\log\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)\right\}-\alpha_{N_{n}},
where αNn\alpha_{N_{n}} is a non-negative function of NnN_{n} that converges to zero as Nn→∞N_{n}\to\infty, and is independent of the choice of 𝒬i⋆{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{\star}.
Lemma A.4 (Minimax theorem).
Let 𝒫⋆={𝐏1⋆,…,𝐏K⋆}{\mathcal{P}}^{\star}=\{\bm{P}_{1}^{\star},\dots,\bm{P}_{K}^{\star}\} be a finite set, and let L⁡(𝐏⋆,θ)L(\bm{P}^{\star},\theta) be a function defined on 𝒫⋆×Θ{\mathcal{P}}^{\star}\times\Theta, where Θ⊂ℝd\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{d} is a convex set. Assume that for each fixed 𝐏⋆∈𝒫⋆\bm{P}^{\star}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\star}, the function L⁡(𝐏⋆,⋅)L(\bm{P}^{\star},\cdot) is continuous and convex in θ\theta.
Let Λ:={λ∈ℝK:λi≥0,∑i=1Kλi=1}\Lambda:=\left\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{K}:\lambda_{i}\geq 0,\ \sum_{i=1}^{K}\lambda_{i}=1\right\} denote the probability simplex over 𝒫⋆{\mathcal{P}}^{\star}, and define
F⁡(λ,θ):=∑i=1Kλi​L​(𝑷i⋆,θ).F(\lambda,\theta):=\sum_{i=1}^{K}\lambda_{i}L(\bm{P}_{i}^{\star},\theta).
Then,
maxλ∈Λ⁡minθ∈Θ⁡F⁡(λ,θ)=minθ∈Θ⁡maxλ∈Λ⁡F⁡(λ,θ)=min⁡sup𝑷⋆∈𝒫⋆θ∈Θ⁡L⁡(𝑷⋆,θ).\max_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\min_{\theta\in\Theta}F(\lambda,\theta)=\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\max_{\lambda\in\Lambda}F(\lambda,\theta)=\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{\bm{P}^{\star}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\star}}L(\bm{P}^{\star},\theta).
∎
We provide the proof of Lemma A.4 below. The proof of Lemma A.3 is deferred to Section A.3, as it is more technical and lengthy.
Proof of Lemma A.4.
Note that the function F⁡(λ,θ)F(\lambda,\theta) is convex in θ\theta for each fixed λ\lambda, and linear (hence concave) in λ\lambda for each fixed θ\theta.
By assumption, L⁡(𝑷i⋆,⋅)L(\bm{P}_{i}^{\star},\cdot) is continuous and convex on the convex domain Θ\Theta, so FF is concave-convex and jointly continuous on the product space Λ×Θ\Lambda\times\Theta. Since Δ\Delta is convex and compact, and Θ\Theta is convex, we may apply Sion’s minimax theorem to exchange the order of min\min and max\max:
minθ∈Θ⁡maxλ∈Λ⁡F⁡(λ,θ)=maxλ∈Λ⁡minθ∈Θ⁡F⁡(λ,θ).\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\max_{\lambda\in\Lambda}F(\lambda,\theta)=\max_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\min_{\theta\in\Theta}F(\lambda,\theta).
Because the maximum over λ∈Λ\lambda\in\Lambda of a convex combination ∑iλi​L​(𝑷i⋆,θ)\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}L(\bm{P}_{i}^{\star},\theta) is achieved at a vertex of the simplex, we observe:
minθ∈Θ⁡maxλ∈Λ⁡F⁡(λ,θ)=minθ∈Θ⁡max𝑷⋆∈𝒫⋆⁡L⁡(𝑷⋆,θ).\displaystyle\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\max_{\lambda\in\Lambda}F(\lambda,\theta)=\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\max_{\bm{P}^{\star}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\star}}L(\bm{P}^{\star},\theta).
∎
A.3 Proof of Lemma A.3
Proof of Lemma A.3.
At a high level, we analyze the quantity
1−𝔼1⁡[Tn|{𝐏𝒱}𝒱=𝒬i⋆]=ℙ1​(Sn≤γn,α)1-\EB_{1}\left[T_{n}~|~\{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}}={\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{\star}\right]={\mathbb{P}}_{1}(S_{n}\leq\gamma_{n,\alpha})
by isolating its dominant term and deriving the lower bound stated in the lemma.
Since all NTP distributions are fixed by the assignment 𝒬i⋆={𝑸i,𝒱}𝒱∈Π{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{\star}=\{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}, we omit this dependence from the notation for clarity.
Step 1: Define tilted random variables.
We begin by defining the random variables:
X𝒱:=−h𝒱​(Y𝒱).X_{{\mathcal{V}}}:=-h_{{\mathcal{V}}}(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}}).
for each minimal unit 𝒱{\mathcal{V}}. Let F𝒱F_{{\mathcal{V}}} denote the alternative CDF of X𝒱X_{{\mathcal{V}}}.
Next, for any θ≥0\theta\geq 0, we define the tilted random variable X¯𝒱\bar{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}} with its CDF given by
F¯𝒱​(x):=1ϕ𝑸i,𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)​∫−∞xeθ​y​d​F𝒱​(y),\bar{F}_{{\mathcal{V}}}(x):=\frac{1}{\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)}\int_{-\infty}^{x}{\mathrm{e}}^{\theta y}{\mathrm{d}}F_{{\mathcal{V}}}(y),
where the normalizing constant
ϕ𝑸i,𝒱,h𝒱​(θ):=𝔼1,𝐐i,𝒱⁡[e−θ​h𝒱​(Y𝒱)]=∫−∞∞eθ​y​dF𝒱​(y)\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta):=\EB_{1,\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}}}[{\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta h_{{\mathcal{V}}}(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}})}]=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{\theta y}{\mathrm{d}}F_{{\mathcal{V}}}(y)
is the MGF of X𝒱X_{{\mathcal{V}}}.
The mean and variance of the tilted random variable X¯𝒱\bar{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}} are given by:
𝔼1⁡[X¯𝒱]=1ϕ𝑸i,𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)​∫−∞∞y​eθ​y​d​F𝒱​(y)=dd​θ​log⁡ϕ𝑸i,𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)=:m𝒱​(θ),Var1​(X¯𝒱)=1ϕ𝑸i,𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)​∫−∞∞(y−𝔼1⁡[X¯𝒱])2​eθ​y​dF𝒱​(y)=d2d​θ2​log⁡ϕ𝐐i,𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)=:σ𝒱2​(θ).\displaystyle\begin{split}\EB_{1}[\bar{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}}]&=\frac{1}{\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}y\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\theta y}{\mathrm{d}}F_{{\mathcal{V}}}(y)=\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\theta}\log\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)=:m_{{\mathcal{V}}}(\theta),\\
\mathrm{Var}_{1}(\bar{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}})&=\frac{1}{\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(y-\EB_{1}[\bar{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}}])^{2}{\mathrm{e}}^{\theta y}{\mathrm{d}}F_{{\mathcal{V}}}(y)=\frac{{\mathrm{d}}^{2}}{{\mathrm{d}}\theta^{2}}\log\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)=:\sigma^{2}_{{\mathcal{V}}}(\theta).\end{split}
(43)
Step 2: Reformulate the tail probability.
Recall the test statistic Sn=∑𝒱∈Πh𝒱(Y𝒱)=−∑𝒱∈ΠX𝒱S_{n}=\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}h_{{\mathcal{V}}}(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}})=-\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}X_{{\mathcal{V}}}. Thus, for any x∈ℝx\in{\mathbb{R}}, we can write ℙ1​(Sn≤−Nn​x)=ℙ1​(∑𝒱∈ΠX𝒱≥Nn​x){\mathbb{P}}_{1}(S_{n}\leq-N_{n}x)={\mathbb{P}}_{1}\left(\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}X_{{\mathcal{V}}}\geq N_{n}x\right). We then express the tail probability ℙ1​(∑𝒱∈ΠX𝒱≥Nn​x){\mathbb{P}}_{1}\left(\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}X_{{\mathcal{V}}}\geq N_{n}x\right) in terms of the CDF of tilted random variables, as stated in the following lemma, whose proof can be found in Section A.4.
Lemma A.5.
Let H¯​(t)\bar{H}(t) be the CDF of the standardized sum ∑𝒱∈ΠX¯𝒱−Nn​m​(θ)Nn​σ2​(θ)\frac{\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\bar{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}}-N_{n}m(\theta)}{\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}}. Then we have
ℙ1​(∑𝒱∈ΠX𝒱≥Nn​x)=∏𝒱∈Πϕ𝑸i,𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)​e−θ​Nn​m​(θ)​∫Nn​x−Nn​m​(θ)Nn​σ2​(θ)∞e−θ​Nn​σ2​(θ)​t​d​H¯n​(t),{\mathbb{P}}_{1}\left(\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}X_{{\mathcal{V}}}\geq N_{n}x\right)=\prod_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta){\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta N_{n}m(\theta)}\int_{\frac{N_{n}x-N_{n}m(\theta)}{\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}}}^{\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}t}{\mathrm{d}}\bar{H}_{n}(t),
where the mixture mean and variance are defined as
m⁡(θ):=1Nn​∑𝒱∈Πm𝒱​(θ),σ2​(θ):=1Nn​∑𝒱∈Πσ𝒱2​(θ).m(\theta):=\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}m_{{\mathcal{V}}}(\theta),\qquad\sigma^{2}(\theta):=\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\sigma^{2}_{{\mathcal{V}}}(\theta).
with each m𝒱​(θ)m_{{\mathcal{V}}}(\theta) and σ𝒱2​(θ)\sigma^{2}_{{\mathcal{V}}}(\theta) given in (43).
Step 3: Decompose the tail integral via Edgeworth expansion.
We next apply Edgeworth expansion to approximate the PDF of H¯n​(t)\bar{H}_{n}(t).
Lemma A.6.
Let φ\varphi denote the PDF of the standard normal distribution 𝒩⁡(0,1){\mathcal{N}}(0,1). Under Assumptions 3.3 and A.1, for any x∈ℝx\in{\mathbb{R}}, we have:
d​H¯n​(x)d​x=φ⁡(x)+λ3,Nn6​Nn​(x3−3​x)​φ​(x)+RNn​(x),\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\bar{H}_{n}(x)}{{\mathrm{d}}x}=\varphi(x)+\frac{\lambda_{3,N_{n}}}{6\sqrt{N_{n}}}(x^{3}-3x)\varphi(x)+R_{N_{n}}(x),

λ3,Nn=1Nn​∑𝒱∈Π𝔼⁡[|X𝒱−𝔼1,𝐐𝒱τ⁡[X𝒱]|3]σ3​(θ).\lambda_{3,N_{n}}=\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\frac{\EB[|X_{\mathcal{V}}-\EB_{1,\bm{Q}_{{\mathcal{V}}_{\tau}}}[X_{\mathcal{V}}]|^{3}]}{\sigma^{3}(\theta)}.
The proof of Lemma A.6 can be found in Section A.5.
Using the above expansion, we evaluate the integral in the tail expression whose proof can be found in Section A.6
Lemma A.7.
Under Assumption A.1, for any θ\theta in the fixed interval (e.g., from Lemma A.8), then
∫0∞e−θ​Nn​σ2​(θ)​t​d​H¯n​(t)=Θ⁡(1Nn).\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}t}{\mathrm{d}}\bar{H}_{n}(t)=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{n}}}\right).
where Θ⁡(⋅)\Theta(\cdot) denote asymptotic equivalence up to constant factors.77
