That is, for two positive sequences ana_{n} and bnb_{n}, we write an=Θ⁡(bn)a_{n}=\Theta(b_{n}) if there exist constants 0<c<C<∞0<c<C<\infty such that c⋅bn≤an≤C⋅bnc\cdot b_{n}\leq a_{n}\leq C\cdot b_{n} for all sufficiently large nn.
Step 4: Putting the pieces together.
Combining Lemmas A.5, A.6, and A.7, and setting x=m⁡(θ)x=m(\theta), we obtain that
ℙ1​(Sn≤−Nn​x)\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{1}(S_{n}\leq-N_{n}x)
=∏𝒱∈Πϕ𝑸i,𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)​e−θ​Nn​m​(θ)⋅Θ⁡(1Nn).\displaystyle=\prod_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta){\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta N_{n}m(\theta)}\cdot\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{n}}}\right).
(44)
Taking logarithms and normalizing, we complete the proof by noting that
1Nn​log⁡ℙ1​(Sn≤−Nn​x)\displaystyle\frac{1}{N_{n}}\log{\mathbb{P}}_{1}(S_{n}\leq-N_{n}x)
=1Nn​log​∏𝒱∈Π(ϕ𝑸i,𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)​e−θ​Nn​m​(θ)⋅Θ⁡(1Nn))\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{n}}\log\prod_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\left(\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta){\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta N_{n}m(\theta)}\cdot\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{n}}}\right)\right)
(45)
=−θ​x+1Nn​∑𝒱∈Πlog⁡(ϕ𝑸i,𝒱,h𝒱​(θ))−Θ⁡(log⁡NnNn),\displaystyle=-\theta x+\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\log\left(\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)\right)-\Theta\left(\frac{\log N_{n}}{N_{n}}\right),
(46)
=(a)​θ​γn,αNn+1Nn​∑𝒱∈Π1​log⁡(ϕ𝑸i,𝒱,h𝒱​(θ))−Θ⁡(log⁡NnNn),\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\theta\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}+\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}1\log\left(\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)\right)-\Theta\left(\frac{\log N_{n}}{N_{n}}\right),
(47)
≥(b)infθ≥0{θγn,αNn+1Nn∑𝒱∈Πlog(ϕ𝑸i,𝒱,h𝒱(θ)))}−Θ(log⁡NnNn)\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\geq}\inf_{\theta\geq 0}\left\{\theta\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}+\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\log\left(\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)\right))\right\}-\Theta\left(\frac{\log N_{n}}{{N_{n}}}\right)
(48)
where (a) follows by setting x=−γn,αNnx=-\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}, and (b) uses the conclusion that the solution to m⁡(θ)=−γn,αNnm(\theta)=-\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}} satisfies θ≥0\theta\geq 0 from Lemma A.8. The proof of Lemma A.8 is in Section A.7.
Lemma A.8 (Stability of roots for mixture equations).
Let Assumptions 3.3 and A.1 hold. Consider the equation m⁡(θ)=−γn,αNnm(\theta)=-\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}, where m⁡(θ)m(\theta) is given in Lemma A.5.
Then the root of this equation is well-defined and lies within a fixed interval [M¯,M¯][\underline{M},\overline{M}], where the constants M¯,M¯>0\underline{M},\overline{M}>0 are independent of NnN_{n} and the specific choice of NTP assignment 𝒬i⋆=(𝐐i,𝒱)𝒱∈Π{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{\star}=(\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}})_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}.
∎
A.4 Proof of Lemma A.5
Proof of Lemma A.5.
Let Wn​(x)W_{n}(x), W¯n​(x)\bar{W}_{n}(x), and H¯n​(x)\bar{H}_{n}(x) denote the CDFs of the random variables ∑𝒱∈ΠX𝒱\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}X_{{\mathcal{V}}}, ∑𝒱∈ΠX¯𝒱\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\bar{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}}, and the standardized sum ∑𝒱∈ΠX¯𝒱−Nn​m​(θ)Nn​σ2​(θ)\frac{\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\bar{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}}-N_{n}m(\theta)}{\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}}, respectively.
By definition, it follows that
H¯n​(x)=W¯n​(Nn​σ2​(θ)​x+Nn​m​(θ)).\displaystyle\bar{H}_{n}(x)=\bar{W}_{n}\left(\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}x+N_{n}m(\theta)\right).
(49)
Let i denote the imaginary unit. The characteristic function of Wn​(x)W_{n}(x) is given by
wn​(z):=𝔼1,𝒬i⋆⁡[ei​z​∑𝒱∈ΠX𝒱]=∏𝒱∈Πϕ𝐐i,𝒱,h𝒱​(i​z).\displaystyle w_{n}(z):=\EB_{1,{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{\star}}[{\mathrm{e}}^{{\textnormal{i}}z\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}X_{{\mathcal{V}}}}]=\prod_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}({\textnormal{i}}z).
(50)
Similarly, the characteristic function of W¯n​(x)\bar{W}_{n}(x) is
w¯n​(z):=𝔼1,𝒬i⋆⁡[ei​z​∑𝒱∈ΠX¯𝒱]=∏𝒱∈Πϕ¯𝐐i,𝒱,h𝒱​(i​z),\displaystyle\bar{w}_{n}(z):=\EB_{1,{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{\star}}[{\mathrm{e}}^{{\textnormal{i}}z\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\bar{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}}}]=\prod_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\bar{\phi}_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}({\textnormal{i}}z),
(51)
where by definition, we have
ϕ¯𝑸i,𝒱,h𝒱​(i​z)=ϕ𝑸i,𝒱,h𝒱​(i​(z−i​θ))/ϕ𝑸i,𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)\bar{\phi}_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}({\textnormal{i}}z)=\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}({\textnormal{i}}(z-{\textnormal{i}}\theta))/\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)
is the MGF of the centered variable X¯𝒱\bar{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}} under the NTP distribution 𝑸i,𝒱\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}}. Using this relation, we obtain the identity:
wn​(z)=w¯n​(z+i​θ)⋅∏𝒱∈Πϕ𝑸i,𝒱,h𝒱​(θ).\displaystyle w_{n}(z)=\bar{w}_{n}(z+{\textnormal{i}}\theta)\cdot\prod_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta).
(52)
If the imaginary part of zz is zero, the left side of the last equation is the characteristic function of Wn​(x)W_{n}(x), while the right side is the characteristic function of
∏𝒱∈Πϕ𝑸i,𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)​∫−∞xe−θ​y​d​W¯n​(y).\prod_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)\int_{-\infty}^{x}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta y}{\mathrm{d}}\bar{W}_{n}(y).
Thus, for all x∈ℝx\in\mathbb{R}, we have
Wn​(x)=∏𝒱∈Πϕ𝑸i,𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)​∫−∞xe−θ​y​d​W¯n​(y).\displaystyle W_{n}(x)=\prod_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)\int_{-\infty}^{x}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta y}{\mathrm{d}}\bar{W}_{n}(y).
(53)
Now, make the change of variables y=Nn​m​(θ)+Nn​σ2​(θ)​ty=N_{n}m(\theta)+\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}t, which yields
Wn​(x)\displaystyle W_{n}\left(x\right)
=∏𝒱∈Πϕ𝑸i,𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)​∫−∞x−Nn​m​(θ)Nn​σ2​(θ)e−θ⁡(Nn​m​(θ)+Nn​σ2​(θ)​t)​d​H¯n​(t)\displaystyle=\prod_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta)\int_{-\infty}^{\frac{x-N_{n}m(\theta)}{\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}}}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta(N_{n}m(\theta)+\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}t)}{\mathrm{d}}\bar{H}_{n}(t)
(54)
=∏𝒱∈Πϕ𝑸i,𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)​e−θ​Nn​m​(θ)​∫−∞x−Nn​m​(θ)Nn​σ2​(θ)e−θ​Nn​σ2​(θ)​t​d​H¯n​(t).\displaystyle=\prod_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta){\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta N_{n}m(\theta)}\int_{-\infty}^{\frac{x-N_{n}m(\theta)}{\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}}}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}t}{\mathrm{d}}\bar{H}_{n}(t).
(55)
Therefore, by substituting x←Nn​xx\leftarrow N_{n}x, we obtain
1−Wn​(Nn​x)=∏𝒱∈Πϕ𝑸i,𝒱,h𝒱​(θ)​e−θ​Nn​m​(θ)​∫Nn​x−Nn​m​(θ)Nn​σ2​(θ)∞e−θ​Nn​σ2​(θ)​t​d​H¯n​(t),1-W_{n}(N_{n}x)=\prod_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\phi_{\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(\theta){\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta N_{n}m(\theta)}\int_{\frac{N_{n}x-N_{n}m(\theta)}{\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}}}^{\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}t}{\mathrm{d}}\bar{H}_{n}(t),
which concludes the proof.
∎
A.5 Proof of Lemma A.6
Proof of Lemma A.6.
At a high level, we apply the Edgeworth expansion to approximate the PDF of H¯n\bar{H}_{n} using functionals of the standard Gaussian distribution. We will make use of the following lemma and verify that its conditions are satisfied in our setting.
Lemma A.9 (Classical Edgeworth expansion).
Let X1,…,XnX_{1},\dots,X_{n} be independent, zero-mean real-valued random variables with variances σi2=Var⁡(Xi)\sigma_{i}^{2}=\mathrm{Var}(X_{i}) and finite third moments. Define
Sn:=∑i=1nXi,Bn:=1n​∑i=1nσi2,Zn:=Snn​Bn,andλ3,n:=1n∑i=1n𝔼⁡[Xi3]Bn3.S_{n}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i},\quad B_{n}:=\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}^{2}},\quad Z_{n}:=\frac{S_{n}}{\sqrt{n}B_{n}},\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda_{3,n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\EB[X_{i}^{3}]}{B_{n}^{3}}.
Suppose the following conditions hold:
1.
lim infn→∞Bn>0\liminf\limits_{n\to\infty}B_{n}>0 and lim supn→∞1n​𝔼⁡[|Xj|3]<∞\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\EB[|X_{j}|^{3}]<\infty.
2.
For some positive τ<1/2\tau<1/2, 1n​∑j=1n𝔼⁡[|Xj|3​𝟏|Xj|>nτ]→0\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\EB\left[|X_{j}|^{3}\mathbf{1}_{|X_{j}|>n^{\tau}}\right]\to 0 as n→∞n\to\infty.
3.
(Cramér’s condition) For every fixed ε>0\varepsilon>0, n​∫|t|>ε∏j=1n|vj​(t)|​𝑑t→0{n}\int_{|t|>\varepsilon}\prod_{j=1}^{n}|v_{j}(t)|dt\to 0 as n→∞n\to\infty where vj​(t)=𝔼⁡[exp⁡(t​i​Xj)]v_{j}(t)=\EB[\exp(t{\textnormal{i}}X_{j})] is the characteristic function of XjX_{j}.
Then, the Edgeworth expansion satisfies
supx∈ℝ|pZn​(x)−[φ⁡(x)+λ3,n6​n​(x3−3​x)​φ​(x)]|=o⁡(1n),\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}\left|p_{Z_{n}}(x)-\left[\varphi(x)+\frac{\lambda_{3,n}}{6\sqrt{n}}(x^{3}-3x)\varphi(x)\right]\right|=o\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right),
where φ⁡(x)\varphi(x) and pZn​(x)p_{Z_{n}}(x) are the PDFs of the standard normal distribution 𝒩⁡(0,1){\mathcal{N}}(0,1) and ZnZ_{n}, respectively.
Proof of Lemma A.9.
The result follows directly from Theorem 7 in Chapter VI, §4 of [40]. Its proof, which we omit, relies on the analysis of the class of random variables denoted by S⁡(3,1,1)S(3,1,1), as defined in the same reference.
∎
To apply Lemma A.9, we define the mean-zero, independent variables {X~𝒱}𝒱∈Π\{\widetilde{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi} by centering the tilted variables:
X~𝒱=X¯𝒱−𝔼1⁡[X¯𝒱]=X¯𝒱−m𝒱​(θ)\widetilde{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}}=\bar{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}}-\EB_{1}[\bar{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}}]=\bar{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}}-m_{{\mathcal{V}}}(\theta)
where m𝒱​(θ)m_{{\mathcal{V}}}(\theta) is defined in (43). These variables remain independent because they preserve the dependence structure of the original variables {X𝒱}𝒱∈Π\{X_{{\mathcal{V}}}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}. The required regularity conditions for applying the Edgeworth expansion are ensured by Assumption A.1, as we now formalize.
Fact A.1 (Facts about centered tilted distributions).
Let [M¯,M¯][\underline{M},\overline{M}] be the interval defined in Lemma A.8 and fix any θ∈[M¯,M¯]\theta\in[\underline{M},\overline{M}]. Under Assumption A.1, the centered tilted variables X~𝒱\widetilde{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}} satisfy:
1.
Uniformly bounded moments: There exists a constant Cmax>0C_{\max}>0 such that for all 𝒱∈Π{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi and all θ∈[M¯,M¯]\theta\in[\underline{M},\overline{M}],
𝔼1⁡[X~𝒱4]≤Cmax.\EB_{1}[\widetilde{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{4}]\leq C_{\max}.
In particular, this also implies uniform bounds on third moments, that is, 𝔼⁡[|X~𝒱|3]≤Cmax′\EB[|\widetilde{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}}|^{3}]\leq C^{\prime}_{\max}.
2.
(Uniform Non-degeneracy of Variance) Uniformly bounded variance away from zero: There exists a constant σmin2>0\sigma_{\min}^{2}>0 such that for all 𝒱∈Π{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi and all θ∈[M¯,M¯]\theta\in[\underline{M},\overline{M}],
Var⁡(X¯𝒱)=𝔼⁡[(X~𝒱)2]=σ𝒱2​(θ)≥σmin2.\mathrm{Var}(\bar{X}_{\mathcal{V}})=\EB[(\widetilde{X}_{\mathcal{V}})^{2}]=\sigma_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{2}(\theta)\geq\sigma^{2}_{\min}.
We now verify the conditions in Lemma A.9 using the above properties:
•
Condition (i): The term
1Nn​∑𝒱∈Π𝔼⁡[|X~𝒱|3]≤Cmax′<∞,\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\EB[|\widetilde{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}}|^{3}]\leq C^{\prime}_{\max}<\infty,
is uniformly bounded. Moreover,
1Nn​∑𝒱∈Π𝔼⁡[|X~𝒱|2]=1Nn​∑𝒱∈Πσ𝒱2​(θ)≥σmin2>0.\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\EB[|\widetilde{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}}|^{2}]=\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\sigma_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{2}(\theta)\geq\sigma_{\min}^{2}>0.
•
Condition (ii): Since we have uniform bounds on the fourth moments, for any τ<1/2\tau<1/2, when n→∞n\to\infty, we have
1Nn​∑𝒱∈Π𝔼⁡[|X~𝒱|3​𝟏|X~𝒱|>Nnτ]≤1Nn1+τ​∑𝒱∈Π𝔼⁡[|X~𝒱|4​𝟏|X~𝒱|>Nnτ]≤CmaxNnτ→0.\displaystyle\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\EB[|\widetilde{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}}|^{3}\mathbf{1}_{|\widetilde{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}}|>N_{n}^{\tau}}]\leq\frac{1}{N_{n}^{1+\tau}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\EB[|\widetilde{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}}|^{4}\mathbf{1}_{|\widetilde{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}}|>N_{n}^{\tau}}]\leq\frac{C_{\max}}{N_{n}^{\tau}}\to 0.
(56)
•
Condition (iii): Cramér’s condition is satisfied as a consequence of the results presented in [40] (Chapter VI, §4, Lemma 10 and the subsequent discussion), combined with our assumptions on score density regularity in Assumption A.1.
Therefore, all conditions in Lemma A.9 are satisfied for the centered tilted variables {X~𝒱}𝒱∈Π\{\widetilde{X}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}. Applying the lemma yields
d​H¯n​(x)d​x=φ⁡(x)+λ3,Nn6​Nn​(x3−3​x)​φ​(x)+RNn​(x),\displaystyle\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\bar{H}_{n}(x)}{{\mathrm{d}}x}=\varphi(x)+\frac{\lambda_{3,N_{n}}}{6\sqrt{N_{n}}}(x^{3}-3x)\varphi(x)+R_{N_{n}}(x),
(57)
where the remainder satisfies
supx∈ℝ|RNn​(x)|=o⁡(1Nn),\displaystyle\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}|R_{N_{n}}(x)|=o\left(\frac{1}{{\sqrt{N_{n}}}}\right),
(58)
concluding the proof.
∎
A.6 Proof of Lemma A.7
Proof of Lemma A.7.
We begin by defining the integral of interest:
I:=∫0∞e−θ​Nn​σ2​(θ)​t​d​H¯n​(t).\displaystyle I:=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}t}{\mathrm{d}}\bar{H}_{n}(t).
Applying the Edgeworth expansion from Lemma A.6, we have
d​H¯n​(t)d​t=φ⁡(t)+λ3,Nn6​Nn​(t3−3​t)​φ​(t)+RNn​(t),\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\bar{H}_{n}(t)}{{\mathrm{d}}t}=\varphi(t)+\frac{\lambda_{3,N_{n}}}{6\sqrt{N_{n}}}(t^{3}-3t)\varphi(t)+R_{N_{n}}(t),
where φ⁡(x)\varphi(x) is the PDF of the standard normal distribution.
Substituting this expansion into the expression for II, we obtain
I\displaystyle I
=∫0∞e−θ​Nn​σ2​(θ)​t​φ​(t)​𝑑t+λ3,Nn6​Nn​∫0∞e−θ​Nn​σ2​(θ)​t​(t3−3​t)​φ​(t)​𝑑t\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}t}\varphi(t){\mathrm{d}}t+\frac{\lambda_{3,N_{n}}}{6\sqrt{N_{n}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}t}(t^{3}-3t)\varphi(t){\mathrm{d}}t
(59)
+∫0∞e−θ​Nn​σ2​(θ)​tRNn(t)dt.\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}t}R_{N_{n}}(t){\mathrm{d}}t.
(60)
We now analyze each term on the right-hand side:
•
First term: We compute the integral as follows:
∫0∞e−θ​Nn​σ2​(θ)​t​φ​(t)​𝑑t\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}t}\varphi(t){\mathrm{d}}t
=∫0∞12​π​e−12​(t2+2​θ​Nn​σ2​(θ)​t)​𝑑t\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(t^{2}+2\theta\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}t\right)}{\mathrm{d}}t
(61)
=∫0∞12​π​e−12​(t+θ​Nn​σ2​(θ))2+θ2​Nn​σ2​(θ)2​𝑑t\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(t+\theta\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}\right)^{2}+\frac{\theta^{2}N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}{2}}{\mathrm{d}}t
(62)
=eθ2​Nn​σ2​(θ)2​∫θ​Nn​σ2​(θ)∞12​π​e−u22​𝑑u\displaystyle={\mathrm{e}}^{\frac{\theta^{2}N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}{2}}\int_{\theta\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}}{\mathrm{d}}u
(63)
=12​π⋅1−Φ⁡(θ​Nn​σ2​(θ))φ⁡(θ​Nn​σ2​(θ)).\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\cdot\frac{1-\Phi(\theta\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)})}{\varphi(\theta\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)})}.
(64)
Lemma A.10 (Mill’s ratio [13]).
Let Φ⁡(x)\Phi(x) and φ⁡(x)\varphi(x) denote the CDF and PDF of the standard normal distribution 𝒩⁡(0,1){\mathcal{N}}(0,1), respectively. Then for all x>0x>0, it holds that
x1+x2<1−Φ⁡(x)φ⁡(x)<1x.\frac{x}{1+x^{2}}<\frac{1-\Phi(x)}{\varphi(x)}<\frac{1}{x}.
Using the classical Mill’s ratio bound in Lemma A.10, we obtain
∫0∞e−θ​Nn​σ2​(θ)​t​φ​(t)​𝑑t=Θ⁡(1θ​Nn​σ2​(θ))=Θ⁡(1Nn).\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}t}\varphi(t){\mathrm{d}}t=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\theta\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}}\right)=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{n}}}\right).
•
Second term: Since λ3,Nn≤C\lambda_{3,N_{n}}\leq C by assumption, we can bound this term as
|λ3,Nn6​2​π​Nn​∫0∞e−θ​Nn​σ2​(θ)​t​(t3−3​t)​e−t22​𝑑t|\displaystyle\left|\frac{\lambda_{3,N_{n}}}{6\sqrt{2\pi N_{n}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}t}(t^{3}-3t){\mathrm{e}}^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2}}{\mathrm{d}}t\right|
≤CNn​∫0∞e−θ​Nn​σ2​(θ)​t​(t3+3​t)​e−t22​𝑑t\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{N_{n}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}t}(t^{3}+3t){\mathrm{e}}^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2}}{\mathrm{d}}t
(65)
≤CNn​[O​(1Nn2)+O​(1Nn)]\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{N_{n}}}\left[O\left(\frac{1}{N_{n}^{2}}\right)+O\left(\frac{1}{N_{n}}\right)\right]
(66)
=O⁡(1Nn3/2).\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{1}{N_{n}^{3/2}}\right).
(67)
•
Third term: Using the bound supx∈ℝ|RNn​(x)|=o⁡(1/Nn)\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}|R_{N_{n}}(x)|=o(1/{\sqrt{N_{n}}}) from Lemma A.6, we have
∫0∞e−θ​Nn​σ2​(θ)​t​RNn​(t)​𝑑t=o⁡(1Nn).\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta\sqrt{N_{n}\sigma^{2}(\theta)}t}R_{N_{n}}(t){\mathrm{d}}t=o\left(\frac{1}{{N_{n}}}\right).
(68)
Putting all terms together, we conclude that
I\displaystyle I
=Θ⁡(1Nn)+O⁡(1Nn3/2)+o⁡(1Nn)=Θ⁡(1Nn).\displaystyle=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{n}}}\right)+O\left(\frac{1}{N_{n}^{3/2}}\right)+o\left(\frac{1}{{N_{n}}}\right)=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{n}}}\right).
(69)
∎
A.7 Proof of Lemma A.8
Proof of Lemma A.8.
Recall that the empirical mean and variance functions are defined by
m⁡(θ):=1Nn​∑𝒱∈Πm𝒱​(θ),σ2​(θ):=1Nn​∑𝒱∈Πσ𝒱2​(θ).m(\theta):=\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}m_{{\mathcal{V}}}(\theta),\qquad\sigma^{2}(\theta):=\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\sigma^{2}_{{\mathcal{V}}}(\theta).
We claim that there exists a unique non-negative solution to the equation m⁡(θ)=−γn,αNnm(\theta)=-\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}. This follows from the following facts:
•
The function mm is strictly increasing for sufficiently large NnN_{n}, since m′=σ2>0m^{\prime}=\sigma^{2}>0.
•
We have m(0)=−1Nn∑𝒱∈Π⋅𝔼1,𝐐i,𝒱[h𝒱]<−1Nn∑𝒱∈Π𝔼0[h𝒱]m(0)=-\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\cdot\EB_{1,\bm{Q}_{i,{\mathcal{V}}}}[h_{{\mathcal{V}}}]<-\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\EB_{0}[h_{{\mathcal{V}}}] due to informativeness.
•
Lemma A.11 implies that
limθ→∞m(θ)=1Nn∑𝒱∈Πesssup(−h𝒱)=−1Nn∑𝒱∈Πessinf(h𝒱)>−1Nn∑𝒱∈Π𝔼0[h𝒱].\lim_{\theta\to\infty}m(\theta)=\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\mathrm{esssup}(-h_{{\mathcal{V}}})=-\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\mathrm{essinf}(h_{{\mathcal{V}}})>-\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\EB_{0}[h_{{\mathcal{V}}}].
Lemma A.11 (Asymptotic behavior of the tilted mean).
Let XX be a real-valued random variable with CDF FF, and define its MGF by ϕ⁡(θ):=𝔼⁡[eθ​X]\phi(\theta):=\EB[{\mathrm{e}}^{\theta X}]. Assume that ϕτ​(θ)\phi_{\tau}(\theta) is finite for all θ∈[0,∞)\theta\in[0,\infty).
Let m⁡(θ):=dd​θ​log⁡ϕ​(θ)m(\theta):=\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\theta}\log\phi(\theta) denote the mean of the exponentially tilted distribution. Then,
limθ→∞m⁡(θ)=esssup⁡(X):=inf{x∈ℝ:ℙ⁡(X≤x)=1}.\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\to\infty}m(\theta)=\mathrm{esssup}(X):=\inf\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}:{\mathbb{P}}(X\leq x)=1\}.
We defer the proof of this lemma at the end of this section.
•
When NnN_{n} is sufficiently large, Lemma A.1 implies that γn,αNn\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}} concentrates to 1Nn​∑𝒱∈Π𝔼0⁡[h𝒱]\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\EB_{0}[h_{{\mathcal{V}}}], so that −γn,αNn∈[m⁡(0),limθ→∞m⁡(θ))-\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}\in[m(0),\lim\limits_{\theta\to\infty}m(\theta)).
We denote by θ⋆\theta^{\star} the unique solution to the equation m⁡(θ)=−γn,αNnm(\theta)=-\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}.
By Lemma A.2, we already know that θ⋆≤M¯\theta^{\star}\leq\overline{M}.
It therefore suffices to establish a lower bound M¯\underline{M} such that θ⋆≥M¯\theta^{\star}\geq\underline{M}.
Fix the interval K:=[0,M¯]K:=[0,\overline{M}].
By the CGF regularity in Assumption A.1, for any θ∈K\theta\in K we have
0<σmin2​(K)≤m′​(θ)≤C2​(K).0<\sigma^{2}_{\min}(K)\;\leq\;m^{\prime}(\theta)\;\leq\;C_{2}(K).
Consequently,
m⁡(0)+σmin2​(K)⋅θ⋆≤m⁡(θ⋆)=−γn,αNn≤m⁡(0)+C2​(K)⋅θ⋆.m(0)+\sigma^{2}_{\min}(K)\cdot\theta^{\star}\;\leq\;m(\theta^{\star})=-\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}\;\leq\;m(0)+C_{2}(K)\cdot\theta^{\star}.
By Lemma A.1, when NnN_{n} is sufficiently large, γn,αNn\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}} concentrates around 1Nn​∑𝒱∈Π𝔼1,𝒬1,𝒱⁡[h𝒱]\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\EB_{1,{\mathcal{Q}}_{1,{\mathcal{V}}}}[h_{{\mathcal{V}}}].
Hence, for large NnN_{n}, it follows that
1Nn​∑𝒱∈Π(𝔼1,𝒬1,𝒱⁡[h𝒱]−𝔼0⁡[h𝒱])C2​(K)≤θ⋆≤1Nn​∑𝒱∈Π(𝔼1,𝒬1,𝒱⁡[h𝒱]−𝔼0⁡[h𝒱])σmin2​(K).\frac{\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\bigl(\EB_{1,{\mathcal{Q}}_{1,{\mathcal{V}}}}[h_{{\mathcal{V}}}]-\EB_{0}[h_{{\mathcal{V}}}]\bigr)}{C_{2}(K)}\;\leq\;\theta^{\star}\;\leq\;\frac{\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\bigl(\EB_{1,{\mathcal{Q}}_{1,{\mathcal{V}}}}[h_{{\mathcal{V}}}]-\EB_{0}[h_{{\mathcal{V}}}]\bigr)}{\sigma^{2}_{\min}(K)}.
We complete the proof by setting
M¯=1C2​([0,M¯])​inf𝒱∈Πinf𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫𝒱⋆[𝔼1,𝐏𝒱⁡[h𝒱]−𝔼0⁡[h𝒱]],\underline{M}\;=\;\frac{1}{C_{2}([0,\overline{M}])}\inf_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\;\inf_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\star}}\Bigl[\EB_{1,\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}}[h_{{\mathcal{V}}}]-\EB_{0}[h_{{\mathcal{V}}}]\Bigr],
which is positive by the informativeness condition in Assumption A.1.
∎
At the end, we provide the proof of Lemma A.11 below.
Proof of Lemma A.11.
The function m⁡(θ)m(\theta) is the expected value of XX under the exponentially tilted probability measure:
m⁡(θ)=∫−∞∞x​eθ​x​𝑑F​(x)∫−∞∞eθ​x​𝑑F​(x).m(\theta)=\frac{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}x{\mathrm{e}}^{\theta x}\,{\mathrm{d}}F(x)}{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{\theta x}\,{\mathrm{d}}F(x)}.
Let x⋆:=esssup⁡(X)=inf{x∈ℝ:ℙ⁡(X≤x)=1}x^{\star}:=\mathrm{esssup}(X)=\inf\{x\in\mathbb{R}:{\mathbb{P}}(X\leq x)=1\}. We aim to show that limθ→∞(θ)=x⋆.\lim_{\theta\to\infty}(\theta)=x^{\star}.
Step 1: Upper bound.
For any ε>0\varepsilon>0, define B:=x⋆+εB:=x^{\star}+\varepsilon. Since ℙ⁡(X>B)=0{\mathbb{P}}(X>B)=0, the tilted mean satisfies
m⁡(θ)≤∫−∞Bx​eθ​x​𝑑F​(x)∫−∞Beθ​x​𝑑F​(x)≤B.m(\theta)\leq\frac{\int_{-\infty}^{B}x{\mathrm{e}}^{\theta x}\,{\mathrm{d}}F(x)}{\int_{-\infty}^{B}{\mathrm{e}}^{\theta x}\,{\mathrm{d}}F(x)}\leq B.
Therefore, for all θ\theta, m⁡(θ)≤x⋆+εm(\theta)\leq x^{\star}+\varepsilon. Taking lim sup\limsup and then letting ε→0\varepsilon\to 0 gives
lim supθ→∞m⁡(θ)≤x⋆.\limsup_{\theta\to\infty}m(\theta)\leq x^{\star}.
Step 2: Lower bound.
Fix δ>0\delta>0 and let A:=x⋆−δA:=x^{\star}-\delta. By the definition of x⋆x^{\star}, we have ℙ⁡(X≥A)>0{\mathbb{P}}(X\geq A)>0. Decompose m⁡(θ)m(\theta) as
m⁡(θ)=∫x<Ax​eθ​x​𝑑F​(x)+∫x≥Ax​eθ​x​𝑑F​(x)∫x<Aeθ​x​𝑑F​(x)+∫x≥Aeθ​x​𝑑F​(x).m(\theta)=\frac{\int_{x<A}x{\mathrm{e}}^{\theta x}\,{\mathrm{d}}F(x)+\int_{x\geq A}x{\mathrm{e}}^{\theta x}\,{\mathrm{d}}F(x)}{\int_{x<A}{\mathrm{e}}^{\theta x}\,{\mathrm{d}}F(x)+\int_{x\geq A}{\mathrm{e}}^{\theta x}\,{\mathrm{d}}F(x)}.
Rewrite both numerator and denominator by factoring out eθ​A{\mathrm{e}}^{\theta A}:
m⁡(θ)=∫x<Ax​eθ⁡(x−A)​𝑑F​(x)+∫x≥Ax​eθ⁡(x−A)​𝑑F​(x)∫x<Aeθ⁡(x−A)​𝑑F​(x)+∫x≥Aeθ⁡(x−A)​𝑑F​(x).m(\theta)=\frac{\int_{x<A}x{\mathrm{e}}^{\theta(x-A)}\,{\mathrm{d}}F(x)+\int_{x\geq A}x{\mathrm{e}}^{\theta(x-A)}\,{\mathrm{d}}F(x)}{\int_{x<A}{\mathrm{e}}^{\theta(x-A)}\,{\mathrm{d}}F(x)+\int_{x\geq A}{\mathrm{e}}^{\theta(x-A)}\,{\mathrm{d}}F(x)}.
As θ→∞\theta\to\infty, the integrals over x<Ax<A vanish by the dominated convergence theorem, since x−A<0x-A<0 in this range and the MGF is finite. Thus,
limθ→∞m⁡(θ)=limθ→∞∫x≥Ax​eθ⁡(x−A)​𝑑F​(x)∫x≥Aeθ⁡(x−A)​𝑑F​(x).\lim_{\theta\to\infty}m(\theta)=\lim_{\theta\to\infty}\frac{\int_{x\geq A}x{\mathrm{e}}^{\theta(x-A)}\,{\mathrm{d}}F(x)}{\int_{x\geq A}{\mathrm{e}}^{\theta(x-A)}\,{\mathrm{d}}F(x)}.
Since x≥Ax\geq A on the support of both integrals, we have the pointwise bound
∫x≥Ax​eθ⁡(x−A)​𝑑F​(x)∫x≥Aeθ⁡(x−A)​𝑑F​(x)≥A=x⋆−δ.\frac{\int_{x\geq A}x{\mathrm{e}}^{\theta(x-A)}\,{\mathrm{d}}F(x)}{\int_{x\geq A}{\mathrm{e}}^{\theta(x-A)}\,{\mathrm{d}}F(x)}\geq A=x^{\star}-\delta.
Therefore, lim infθ→∞m⁡(θ)≥x⋆−δ\liminf_{\theta\to\infty}m(\theta)\geq x^{\star}-\delta. Since δ>0\delta>0 is arbitrary, we conclude
lim infθ→∞m⁡(θ)≥x⋆.\liminf_{\theta\to\infty}m(\theta)\geq x^{\star}.
Combining both steps, we have lim infθ→∞m⁡(θ)≥x⋆\liminf_{\theta\to\infty}m(\theta)\geq x^{\star} and complete the proof.
∎
A.8 Verification of Regularity Conditions for Considered Watermarks
In this section, we show that the required conditions are satisfied for the established optimal detection rules of the two watermarking schemes under study. The independence structure in Assumption 3.3 is already justified by the sensitivity of hash functions and therefore does not require further verification. We thus focus on the remaining conditions.
A.8.1 Inverse Transform Watermark
We begin with the inverse transform watermark, as its verification is more straightforward.
First, Assumption 5.1 cannot be verified in practice since it is introduced as a simplifying assumption for theoretical analysis. Thus, it suffices to check the remaining two conditions in Assumption 3.3.
On the one hand, the bounded variance condition in Assumption 3.3(ii) holds immediately because [h𝒱inv]​[−M,M][h_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathrm{inv}}]{[-M,M]} is uniformly bounded by MM.
On the other hand, the well-posedness condition in Assumption 3.3 (iii) is automatically satisfied because in deriving (30) we essentially set the minimizer to θ=1\theta=1, which is uniformly bounded.
To make this intuition more rigorous, we can instead argue more directly: for the score functions 𝒉={h𝒱}𝒱∈Π{\bm{h}}=\{h_{{\mathcal{V}}}\}_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}, we have
R¯n,𝒫​(𝒉)\displaystyle\bar{R}_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}})
≥(a)​lim inf|𝒲|→∞Dn,𝒫​(𝒉)\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\geq}\liminf_{|{{\mathcal{W}}}|\to\infty}D_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}})
≥(b)−lim sup|𝒲|→∞(γn,αNn+1Nn​∑𝒱∈Πsup𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫¯Δ𝒱log⁡ϕ𝑷𝒱,h𝒱​(1))\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\geq}-\limsup_{|{{\mathcal{W}}}|\to\infty}\left(\frac{\gamma_{n,\alpha}}{N_{n}}+\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq\overline{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Delta_{{\mathcal{V}}}}}\log\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(1)\right)
≥(c)−lim sup|𝒲|→∞1Nn​∑𝒱∈Π(𝔼0⁡[h𝒱​(Y𝒱)]+sup𝐏𝒱⊆𝒫¯Δ𝒱log⁡ϕ𝐏𝒱,h𝒱​(1))−ωNn\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\geq}-\limsup_{|{{\mathcal{W}}}|\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\left(\EB_{0}[h_{{\mathcal{V}}}(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}})]+\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq\overline{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Delta_{{\mathcal{V}}}}}\log\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(1)\right)-\omega_{N_{n}}
=−1Nn​∑𝒱∈Πlim sup|𝒲|→∞(𝔼0⁡[h𝒱​(Y𝒱)]+sup𝐏𝒱⊆𝒫¯Δ𝒱log⁡ϕ𝐏𝒱,h𝒱​(1))−ωNn.\displaystyle\overset{}{=}-\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{{\mathcal{V}}\in\Pi}\limsup_{|{{\mathcal{W}}}|\to\infty}\left(\EB_{0}[h_{{\mathcal{V}}}(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}})]+\sup_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq\overline{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Delta_{{\mathcal{V}}}}}\log\phi_{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}},h_{{\mathcal{V}}}}(1)\right)-\omega_{N_{n}}.
(30)
Here, (a)(a) follows from (31), (b)(b) from setting θ=1\theta=1 in the definition of Dn,𝒫​(𝒉)D_{n,\mathscr{P}}({\bm{h}}) in (32), and (c)(c) from Lemma A.1.
As a result, we still arrive at (30).
A.8.2 Gumbel-max Watermark
The main effort is devoted to the Gumbel-max watermark, as we need to verify both conditions in Assumption 3.3 as well as the additional Assumption A.1, which is required for establishing the asymptotic tightness in Remark 3.3.
For clarity, we fix a minimal unit 𝒱{\mathcal{V}} and denote the two proposed score functions as
h𝑺Δ⋆​(y):=(|𝒱|∧|𝒲|)​Δ(|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1)​(1−Δ)​log⁡yandh𝑷Δ⋆​(y):=log⁡(yΔ1−Δ+y1−ΔΔ).h_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}}(y):=\frac{(|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|)\,\Delta}{(|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1)(1-\Delta)}\,\log y\quad\text{and}\quad h_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}}(y):=\log\left(y^{\frac{\Delta}{1-\Delta}}+y^{\frac{1-\Delta}{\Delta}}\right).
(70)
Verification of Assumption 3.3.
Note that both optimal scores h𝑺Δ⋆h_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}} and h𝑷Δ⋆h_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}} are log-likelihood ratio functions corresponding to the least-favorable distribution vectors 𝑺Δ⋆{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star} and 𝑷Δ⋆\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}, respectively. By direct computation, their moment generating functions are finite and their variances are uniformly bounded. Under these optimal scores, the minimization in θ\theta is achieved at θ=1\theta=1, which is uniformly bounded. Hence, the well-posedness condition is satisfied.
Verification of Assumption A.1.
There are four conditions in Assumption A.1, and we verify them one by one.
1.
(Finite maximizers)
This condition follows from Lemma 7.6, together with the fact that both h𝑺Δ⋆h_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}} and h𝑷Δ⋆h_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}} are increasing. Hence, only 𝑺Δ⋆{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star} and 𝑷Δ⋆\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star} can serve as least-favorable distribution vectors.
2.
(Informative scores)
By Lemma 4.1, the null and alternative CDFs are given by F0​(y)=yF_{0}(y)=y and F𝑺​(y)=(∑wSw)−1​∑wSw​y1/SwF_{{\bm{S}}}(y)=(\sum_{w}S_{w})^{-1}\sum_{w}S_{w}y^{1/S_{w}} with 𝑺=(S1,…,S|𝒲|){\bm{S}}=(S_{1},\ldots,S_{|{{\mathcal{W}}}|}), respectively. From Lemma 7.2, we know that Sw∈[0,1−Δ)S_{w}\in[0,1-\Delta) for any ww, so Sw<1S_{w}<1 and thus y1/Sw<yy^{1/S_{w}}<y for any y∈(0,1)y\in(0,1). Consequently,
F𝑺​(y)=∑wSw​y1/Sw∑wSw<∑wSw​y∑wSw=y=F0​(y).\displaystyle F_{{\bm{S}}}(y)=\frac{\sum_{w}S_{w}y^{1/S_{w}}}{\sum_{w}S_{w}}<\frac{\sum_{w}S_{w}y}{\sum_{w}S_{w}}=y=F_{0}(y).
Thus, the alternative distribution of YY is stochastically dominated by the null distribution. Since both h𝑺Δ⋆h_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}} and h𝑷Δ⋆h_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}} are strictly increasing, integration by parts shows that 𝔼1,𝐏𝒱⁡[h⁡(Y)]>𝔼0⁡[h⁡(Y)]\EB_{1,\bm{P}_{\mathcal{V}}}[h(Y)]>\EB_{0}[h(Y)] for h∈{h𝑺Δ⋆,h𝑷Δ⋆}h\in\{h_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}},h_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}}\}.
3.
(CGF regularity)
Recall that the CGF is defined as the logarithm of the MGF. Formally, for a score function hh, the MGF is ϕ𝑺​(θ)=𝔼1,𝐒⁡[exp⁡(−θ​h​(Y𝒱))]\phi_{{\bm{S}}}(\theta)=\EB_{1,{\bm{S}}}[\exp(-\theta h(Y_{\mathcal{V}}))] and the CGF is given by K⁡(θ)=log⁡ϕ𝑺​(θ)K(\theta)=\log\phi_{{\bm{S}}}(\theta). For simplicity, we denote the alternative density by f𝑺​(y)=F𝑺′​(y)=(∑w′​Sw′)−1​∑wy1/Sw−1f_{{\bm{S}}}(y)=F^{\prime}_{{\bm{S}}}(y)=(\sum{w^{\prime}}S_{w^{\prime}})^{-1}\sum_{w}y^{1/S_{w}-1}.
•
For h𝑺Δ⋆h_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}}, the MGF takes the explicit form
ϕ𝑺​(θ)=∫01e−θ​c​log⁡y​f𝑺​(y)​𝑑y=∫01y−θ​c​∑wy1/Sw−1∑w′Sw′​𝑑y=1∑w′Sw′​∑wSw1−θ​c​Sw.\phi_{{\bm{S}}}(\theta)=\int_{0}^{1}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\theta c\log y}f_{{\bm{S}}}(y){\mathrm{d}}y=\int_{0}^{1}y^{-\theta c}\frac{\sum_{w}y^{1/S_{w}-1}}{\sum_{w^{\prime}}S_{w^{\prime}}}{\mathrm{d}}y=\frac{1}{\sum_{w^{\prime}}S_{w^{\prime}}}\sum_{w}\frac{S_{w}}{1-\theta cS_{w}}.
•
For h𝑷Δ⋆h_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}}, the MGF is
ϕ𝑺​(θ)=∫01(y1−ΔΔ+yΔ1−Δ)−θ​f𝑺​(y)​𝑑y.\phi_{{\bm{S}}}(\theta)=\int_{0}^{1}(y^{\frac{1-\Delta}{\Delta}}+y^{\frac{\Delta}{1-\Delta}})^{-\theta}f_{{\bm{S}}}(y)dy.
On any compact set [0,M¯][0,\overline{M}], the derivatives of log⁡ϕ𝑺​(θ)\log\phi_{{\bm{S}}}(\theta) are smooth and bounded, which yields uniform upper bounds. For the lower bound, note that K′′​(θ)K^{\prime\prime}(\theta) equals the variance of −h⁡(Y)-h(Y) under a tilted measure, which is strictly positive as long as h⁡(Y)h(Y) is not constant. By continuity, K′′​(θ)K^{\prime\prime}(\theta) is uniformly bounded below on [0,M¯][0,\overline{M}] by some constant σmin2​(K)>0\sigma_{\min}^{2}(K)>0. These constants can be chosen independently of any specific 𝑺∈𝒟Δ{\bm{S}}\in\mathcal{D}_{\Delta}, thanks to compactness of [0,M¯][0,\overline{M}] and smoothness of the CGF.
4.
(Score density regularity)
The last condition is verified directly by Lemma A.12.
Lemma A.12 (Bounded total variation).
Let TV⁡(ρ):=∫−∞∞|ρ′​(x)|​𝑑x\mathrm{TV}(\rho):=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\rho^{\prime}(x)|{\mathrm{d}}x denote the total variation of a PDF ρ\rho.
When |𝒱|=1|{\mathcal{V}}|=1 and Δ∈(0,1/2)\Delta\in(0,1/2), with h𝐒Δ⋆h_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}} and h𝐏Δ⋆h_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}} defined in (70), we have a universal constant CR>0C_{R}>0 such that
TV⁡(h𝑺Δ⋆)≤|𝒲|andTV⁡(h𝑷Δ⋆)≤|𝒲|+CR.\mathrm{TV}(h_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}})\leq|{{\mathcal{W}}}|\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{TV}(h_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}})\leq|{{\mathcal{W}}}|+C_{R}.
Proof of Lemma A.12.
Let Z=h⁡(Y)Z=h(Y) denote the score for a minimal unit 𝒱\mathcal{V}.
When |𝒱|=1|{\mathcal{V}}|=1, there is no repetition and each 𝑺{\bm{S}} reduces to 𝑷\bm{P}.
By Lemma 4.1, the alternative PDF of YY is
f𝑷​(y)=F𝑷′​(y)=∑wy1/Pw−1.f_{\bm{P}}(y)=F^{\prime}_{\bm{P}}(y)=\sum_{w}y^{1/P_{w}-1}.
By a change of variables, the PDF of ZZ, denoted by ρ𝑷\rho_{\bm{P}}, is
ρ𝑷​(z)=f𝑷​(g⁡(z))​|g′​(z)|\rho_{\bm{P}}(z)=f_{\bm{P}}(g(z))|g^{\prime}(z)|, where y=g⁡(z)y=g(z) is the inverse of z=h⁡(y)z=h(y).
Case 1: h𝑺Δ⋆​(y)=C​log⁡yh_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}}(y)=C\log y.
Here C=Δ1−ΔC=\frac{\Delta}{1-\Delta}, and the inverse function is y=g⁡(z)=ez/Cy=g(z)=e^{z/C} for z∈(−∞,0)z\in(-\infty,0) with derivative g′​(z)=1C​ez/Cg^{\prime}(z)=\tfrac{1}{C}e^{z/C}. The density of ZZ is
ρ𝑷​(z)=f𝑷​(ez/C)⋅1C​ez/C=1C​∑wez/(C​Pw).\rho_{\bm{P}}(z)=f_{\bm{P}}(e^{z/C})\cdot\frac{1}{C}e^{z/C}=\frac{1}{C}\sum_{w}e^{z/(CP_{w})}.
Since ρ𝑷′​(z)>0\rho_{\bm{P}}^{\prime}(z)>0 for z∈(−∞,0)z\in(-\infty,0), it follows that
TV⁡(ρ𝑷)=∫−∞0ρ𝑷′​(z)​𝑑z=ρ𝑷​(0)−limz→−∞ρ𝑷​(z)=|𝒲|C≤|𝒲|,\mathrm{TV}(\rho_{\bm{P}})=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\rho_{\bm{P}}^{\prime}(z)\,{\mathrm{d}}z=\rho_{\bm{P}}(0)-\lim_{z\to-\infty}\rho_{\bm{P}}(z)=\frac{|{{\mathcal{W}}}|}{C}\leq|{{\mathcal{W}}}|,
where the last inequality holds because C>1C>1 when Δ∈(0,1/2)\Delta\in(0,1/2).
Case 2: h𝑷Δ⋆​(y)=log⁡(yC+y1/C)h_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}}(y)=\log(y^{C}+y^{1/C}).
Here C=Δ1−Δ∈(0,1)C=\frac{\Delta}{1-\Delta}\in(0,1). By definition,
TV⁡(ρ𝑷)=∫|ρ𝑷′​(z)|​𝑑z=∫01|dd​y​(f𝑷​(y)h′​(y))|​𝑑y≤∫01|f𝑷′​(y)h′​(y)|​dy⏟(I)+∫01f𝑷​(y)​|h′′​(y)(h′​(y))2|​dy⏟(I​I).\mathrm{TV}(\rho_{\bm{P}})=\int|\rho_{\bm{P}}^{\prime}(z)|\,{\mathrm{d}}z=\int_{0}^{1}\left|\frac{d}{dy}\left(\frac{f_{\bm{P}}(y)}{h^{\prime}(y)}\right)\right|{\mathrm{d}}y\leq\underbrace{\int_{0}^{1}\left|\frac{f_{\bm{P}}^{\prime}(y)}{h^{\prime}(y)}\right|{\mathrm{d}}y}_{(I)}+\underbrace{\int_{0}^{1}f_{\bm{P}}(y)\left|\frac{h^{\prime\prime}(y)}{(h^{\prime}(y))^{2}}\right|{\mathrm{d}}y}_{(II)}.
We first analyze the term (II). For simplicity, we define R⁡(y):=|h′′​(y)/(h′​(y))2|R(y):=\big|h^{\prime\prime}(y)/(h^{\prime}(y))^{2}\big|, which is independent of 𝑷\bm{P}.
As y→1y\to 1, h′​(1)=(C+1/C)/2≠0h^{\prime}(1)=(C+1/C)/2\neq 0, so R⁡(1)<∞R(1)<\infty.
As y→0y\to 0, h′​(y)∼C/yh^{\prime}(y)\sim C/y and h′′(y)∼−C/y2h^{\prime\prime}(y)\sim-C/y^{2}, hence limy→0R⁡(y)=1/C\lim_{y\to 0}R(y)=1/C.
Since R⁡(y)R(y) is continuous on (0,1](0,1] with finite boundary limits, it is uniformly bounded by some constant CR<∞C_{R}<\infty. Thus,
(I​I)≤CR​∫01f𝑷​(y)​𝑑y=CR.(II)\leq C_{R}\int_{0}^{1}f_{\bm{P}}(y){\mathrm{d}}y=C_{R}.
Next, we analyze the term (I). Since Pw<1P_{w}<1 for any 𝑷∈𝒫Δ\bm{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Delta}, we have f𝑷′​(y)=∑w(1/Pw−1)​y1/Pw−2>0f_{\bm{P}}^{\prime}(y)=\sum_{w}(1/P_{w}-1)y^{1/P_{w}-2}>0, so the absolute value can be removed. Integration by parts gives
(I)=∫01f𝑷′​(y)h′​(y)​𝑑y=[f𝑷​(y)h′​(y)]01+∫01f𝑷​(y)​h′′​(y)(h′​(y))2​𝑑y.(I)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{f_{\bm{P}}^{\prime}(y)}{h^{\prime}(y)}{\mathrm{d}}y=\left[\frac{f_{\bm{P}}(y)}{h^{\prime}(y)}\right]_{0}^{1}+\int_{0}^{1}f_{\bm{P}}(y)\frac{h^{\prime\prime}(y)}{(h^{\prime}(y))^{2}}dy.
At y=1y=1, f𝑷​(1)=|𝒲|f_{\bm{P}}(1)=|{{\mathcal{W}}}|.
As y→0y\to 0, using h′​(y)∼C/yh^{\prime}(y)\sim C/y,
limy→0f𝑷​(y)h′​(y)=1C​limy→0∑wy1/Pw=0.\lim_{y\to 0}\frac{f_{\bm{P}}(y)}{h^{\prime}(y)}=\frac{1}{C}\lim_{y\to 0}\sum_{w}y^{1/P_{w}}=0.
The integral term is bounded in magnitude by (II). Hence,
(I)≤|𝒲|h′​(1)+CR=2​|𝒲|C+1/C+CR.(I)\leq\frac{|{{\mathcal{W}}}|}{h^{\prime}(1)}+C_{R}=\frac{2|{{\mathcal{W}}}|}{C+1/C}+C_{R}.
Combining the bounds for (I) and (II) yields
TV⁡(ρ𝑷)≤2​|𝒲|C+1/C+2​CR≤|𝒲|+2​CR\mathrm{TV}(\rho_{\bm{P}})\leq\frac{2|{{\mathcal{W}}}|}{C+1/C}+2C_{R}\leq|{{\mathcal{W}}}|+2C_{R}
which is finite and uniform over 𝑷∈𝒫Δ\bm{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Delta}.
In both cases, TV⁡(ρ𝑷)\mathrm{TV}(\rho_{\bm{P}}) is finite and bounded by a multiple of |𝒲||{{\mathcal{W}}}|, completing the proof.
∎
Appendix B Proof for Gumbel-max Watermarks in Section 4
B.1 Proof of Lemma 4.1
Proof of Lemma 4.1.
We assert that Yt1=⋯=YtkY_{t_{1}}=\cdots=Y_{t_{k}} if and only if wt1=⋯=wtkw_{t_{1}}=\cdots=w_{t_{k}}. This follows from the fact that if wt1≠wt2w_{t_{1}}\neq w_{t_{2}}, then Yt1Y_{t_{1}} and Yt2Y_{t_{2}} are independent by Assumption 3.2. Since each YtY_{t} has a smooth CDF, the probability ℙ1​(Yt1=Yt2∣wt1≠wt2)=0{\mathbb{P}}_{1}(Y_{t_{1}}=Y_{t_{2}}\mid w_{t_{1}}\neq w_{t_{2}})=0, making such an event almost surely impossible.
Recall that the NTP distribution for wtiw_{t_{i}} is given by 𝑷ti\bm{P}_{t_{i}}. Since the same pseudorandom variable is used to generate all wtiw_{t_{i}} for ti∈𝒱t_{i}\in{\mathcal{V}}, we denote it by ζ=(Uw)w∈𝒲\zeta=(U_{w})_{w\in{{\mathcal{W}}}}. Consequently, each token satisfies wti=𝒮⁡(𝑷ti,ζ)w_{t_{i}}={\mathcal{S}}(\bm{P}_{t_{i}},\zeta) for all ti∈𝒱t_{i}\in{\mathcal{V}}.
Under the event Yt1=⋯=YtkY_{t_{1}}=\cdots=Y_{t_{k}}, we define wt1=⋯=wtk=ww_{t_{1}}=\cdots=w_{t_{k}}=w. This implies that w=𝒮⁡(𝑷t,ζ)w={\mathcal{S}}(\bm{P}_{t},\zeta) for all t∈𝒱t\in{\mathcal{V}}.
Therefore, it follows that
ℙ1(Yt1≤y|Yt1=⋯=Ytk,𝑷𝒱)\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{1}(Y_{t_{1}}\leq y~|~Y_{t_{1}}=\cdots=Y_{t_{k}},\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}})
=ℙ1(Yt1≤y,wt1=⋯=wtk|Yt1=⋯=Ytk,𝑷𝒱)\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}_{1}(Y_{t_{1}}\leq y,~w_{t_{1}}=\cdots=w_{t_{k}}|\,Y_{t_{1}}=\cdots=Y_{t_{k}},\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}})
=∑w∈𝒲ℙ1(Yt1≤y,wt1=⋯=wtk=w|Yt1=⋯=Ytk,𝑷𝒱)\displaystyle=\sum_{w\in{{\mathcal{W}}}}{\mathbb{P}}_{1}(Y_{t_{1}}\leq y,~w_{t_{1}}=\cdots=w_{t_{k}}=w|\,Y_{t_{1}}=\cdots=Y_{t_{k}},\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}})
=∑w∈𝒲ℙ1(Y(w,ζ)≤y|w=𝒮(𝑷t,ζ),∀t∈𝒱)\displaystyle=\sum_{w\in{{\mathcal{W}}}}{\mathbb{P}}_{1}(Y(w,\zeta)\leq y~|~w={\mathcal{S}}(\bm{P}_{t},\zeta),~\forall t\in{\mathcal{V}})
=∑w∈𝒲ℙ1(Uw≤y|Uw′≤Uwmaxt∈𝒱⁡(Pt,w′Pt,w),∀w′≠w).\displaystyle=\sum_{w\in{{\mathcal{W}}}}{\mathbb{P}}_{1}\left(U_{w}\leq y~\Big|~U_{w^{\prime}}\leq U_{{w}}^{\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}\left(\frac{P_{t,w^{\prime}}}{P_{t,w}}\right)},\forall w^{\prime}\neq{w}\right).
Given that {Uw}w∈𝒲\{U_{w}\}_{w\in{{\mathcal{W}}}} are i.i.d. U⁡(0,1){U}(0,1), direct calculation yields that
ℙ1(Uw≤y,Uw′≤Uwmaxt∈𝒱⁡(Pt,w′Pt,w),∀w′≠w)=Swy1/Sw.\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{1}\left(U_{w}\leq y,U_{w^{\prime}}\leq U_{{w}}^{\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}\left(\frac{P_{t,w^{\prime}}}{P_{t,w}}\right)},\forall w^{\prime}\neq{w}\right)=S_{w}y^{1/S_{w}}.
As a result, it follows from Bayes’ theorem that
ℙ1(Uw≤y|Uw′≤Uwmaxt∈𝒱⁡(Pt,w′Pt,w),∀w′≠w)=Sw​y1/Sw∑w′∈𝒲Sw′.{\mathbb{P}}_{1}\left(U_{w}\leq y~\Big|~U_{w^{\prime}}\leq U_{{w}}^{\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}\left(\frac{P_{t,w^{\prime}}}{P_{t,w}}\right)},\forall w^{\prime}\neq{w}\right)=\frac{S_{w}y^{1/S_{w}}}{\sum_{w^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{W}}}}S_{w^{\prime}}}.
Summing the last probability over all w∈𝒲w\in{{\mathcal{W}}} completes the proof.
∎
B.2 Proof of Lemma 7.1
Proof of Lemma 7.1.
Proof of the “if” direction.
If there exists a vector 𝑺⋆∈𝒟Δ{\bm{S}}^{\star}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta} such that
max𝑺∈𝒟Δ⁡L⁡(h𝑺⋆,𝑺)=L⁡(h𝑺⋆,𝑺⋆),\max_{{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}}L(h_{{\bm{S}}^{\star}},{\bm{S}})=L(h_{{\bm{S}}^{\star}},{\bm{S}}^{\star}),
(27)
on the one hand, it follows from the Donsker–Varadhan representation that
minhmax𝑺∈𝒟ΔL(h,𝑺)≥minhL(h,𝑺⋆)=−KL(F0∥F𝑺⋆).\min_{h}\max_{{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}}L(h,{\bm{S}})\geq\min_{h}L(h,{\bm{S}}^{\star})=-\mathrm{KL}(F_{0}\|F_{{\bm{S}}^{\star}}).
On the other hand, by the condition (27), it follows that
minhmax𝑺∈𝒟ΔL(h,𝑺)≤max𝑺∈𝒟ΔL(h𝑺⋆,𝑺)=L(h𝑺⋆,𝑺⋆)=−KL(F0∥F𝑺⋆).\displaystyle\min_{h}\max_{{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}}L(h,{\bm{S}})\leq\max_{{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}}L(h_{{\bm{S}}^{\star}},{\bm{S}})=L(h_{{\bm{S}}^{\star}},{\bm{S}}^{\star})=-\mathrm{KL}(F_{0}\|F_{{\bm{S}}^{\star}}).
Combining the above two directions, we know that (h𝑺⋆,𝑺⋆)(h_{{\bm{S}}^{\star}},{\bm{S}}^{\star}) is a solution pair of the minimax problem (26).
Proof of the “only if” direction.
Suppose the pair (h⋆,𝑺⋆)(h^{\star},{\bm{S}}^{\star}) solves the minimax problem (26). By definition, we have
L⁡(h⋆,𝑺⋆)=minh⁡max𝑺∈𝒟Δ⁡L⁡(h,𝑺)=max𝑺∈𝒟Δ⁡L⁡(h⋆,𝑺)=minh⁡L⁡(h,𝑺⋆).\displaystyle L(h^{\star},{\bm{S}}^{\star})=\min_{h}\max_{{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}}L(h,{\bm{S}})=\max_{{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}}L(h^{\star},{\bm{S}})=\min_{h}L(h,{\bm{S}}^{\star}).
The last equality holds if and only if h⋆=h𝑺⋆h^{\star}=h_{{\bm{S}}^{\star}} (up to a constant shift), by the Donsker–Varadhan representation. The second equality corresponds exactly to the optimality condition (27).
∎
B.3 Proof of Lemma 7.2
Proof of Lemma 7.2.
Recall that for any 𝑺∈𝒟Δ{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta} there exists 𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫Δ\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{\Delta} such that for each w∈𝒲w\in{{\mathcal{W}}},
Sw=(∑w′∈𝒲maxt∈𝒱⁡Pt,w′Pt,w)−1.S_{w}=\left(\sum_{w^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{W}}}}\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}\frac{P_{t,w^{\prime}}}{P_{t,w}}\right)^{-1}.
(71)
According to this definition, the permutation invariance of 𝒟Δ{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta} follows directly by permuting the order of entries in each NTP distribution in 𝑷𝒱\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}.
We now turn to prove the remaining part.
Using the fact that Pt,w′Pt,w≤maxt∈𝒱⁡Pt,w′Pt,w≤∑t∈𝒱Pt,w′Pt,w\frac{P_{t,w^{\prime}}}{P_{t,w}}\leq\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}\frac{P_{t,w^{\prime}}}{P_{t,w}}\leq\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}\frac{P_{t,w^{\prime}}}{P_{t,w}} for any t∈𝒱t\in{\mathcal{V}}, we have that
(∑t∈𝒱1Pt,w−(|𝒱|−1))−1≤Sw≤mint∈𝒱⁡Pt,w.\left(\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}\frac{1}{P_{t,w}}-(|{\mathcal{V}}|-1)\right)^{-1}\leq S_{w}\leq\min_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,w}.
(72)
With this result, we are now ready to prove the three bullet points.
(i)
By the definition in (71), it is clear that 0≤Sw0\leq S_{w}. Using (72), it follows that Sw≤mint∈𝒱⁡Pt,w≤1−ΔS_{w}\leq\min_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,w}\leq 1-\Delta due to 𝑷𝒱⊆𝒫Δ\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}_{\Delta}.
(ii)
By (72), we have that ∑wSw≤∑wmint∈𝒱⁡Pt,w≤∑wPt,w=1\sum_{w}S_{w}\leq\sum_{w}\min_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,w}\leq\sum_{w}P_{t,w}=1.
(iii)
By some algebraic manipulation, the target inequality maxw⁡Sw1−Δ≤1−1−∑wSw|𝒱|∧|𝒲|\frac{\max_{w}S_{w}}{1-\Delta}\leq 1-\frac{1-\sum_{w}S_{w}}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|} is equivalent to
(1+Δ|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1)​Sw≤(1−Δ)⋅(∑w′≠wSw′|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1+1),∀w∈𝒲.\left(1+\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1}\right)S_{w}\leq(1-\Delta)\cdot\left(\frac{\sum_{w^{\prime}\neq w}S_{w^{\prime}}}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1}+1\right),~\forall~w\in{{\mathcal{W}}}.
(73)
We then turn to prove (73).
Fix any w∈𝒲w\in{{\mathcal{W}}}.
If Sw=0S_{w}=0, then (73) holds trivially. Otherwise, if Sw>0S_{w}>0, then by the relation (72), we have 0<Sw≤mint∈𝒱⁡Pt,w0<S_{w}\leq\min_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,w}. This implies that Pt,wP_{t,w} is strictly positive for all indices t∈𝒱t\in{\mathcal{V}}.
In this case, on the one hand, it follows that
Sw=(1+∑w′≠wmaxt∈𝒱⁡Pt,w′Pt,w)−1≤(1+∑w′≠wmaxt∈𝒱⁡Pt,w′1−Δ)−1\displaystyle S_{w}=\left(1+\sum_{w^{\prime}\neq w}\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}\frac{P_{t,w^{\prime}}}{P_{t,w}}\right)^{-1}\leq\left(1+\frac{\sum_{w^{\prime}\neq w}\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,w^{\prime}}}{1-\Delta}\right)^{-1}
(74)
where the inequality holds because Pt,w≤1−ΔP_{t,w}\leq 1-\Delta for all tt and ww.
On the other hand, we have that
∑w′≠wSw′\displaystyle\sum_{w^{\prime}\neq w}S_{w^{\prime}}
=∑w′≠w(∑j∈𝒲maxt∈𝒱⁡Pt,jPt,w′)−1\displaystyle=\sum_{w^{\prime}\neq w}\left(\sum_{j\in{{\mathcal{W}}}}\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}\frac{P_{t,j}}{P_{t,w^{\prime}}}\right)^{-1}
≥∑w′≠w(1+∑j≠w′maxt∈𝒱⁡Pt,jmint∈𝒱⁡Pt,w′)−1\displaystyle\geq\sum_{w^{\prime}\neq w}\left(1+\sum_{j\neq w^{\prime}}\frac{\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,j}}{\min_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,w^{\prime}}}\right)^{-1}
(75)
=∑w′≠wmint∈𝒱⁡Pt,w′⋅(mint∈𝒱⁡Pt,w′+∑j≠w′maxt∈𝒱⁡Pt,j)−1\displaystyle=\sum_{w^{\prime}\neq w}\min_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,w^{\prime}}\cdot\left(\min_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,w^{\prime}}+\sum_{j\neq w^{\prime}}\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,j}\right)^{-1}
≥∑w′≠wmint∈𝒱⁡Pt,w′1−Δ+∑w′≠wmaxt∈𝒱⁡Pt,w′,\displaystyle\geq\frac{\sum_{w^{\prime}\neq w}\min_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,w^{\prime}}}{1-\Delta+\sum_{w^{\prime}\neq w}\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,w^{\prime}}},
where the last inequality follows from the fact that, for any w′≠ww^{\prime}\neq w,
mint∈𝒱⁡Pt,w′+∑j≠w′maxt∈𝒱⁡Pt,j\displaystyle\min_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,w^{\prime}}+\sum_{j\neq w^{\prime}}\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,j}
≤mint∈𝒱⁡Pt,w′+maxt∈𝒱⁡Pt,w+∑j∉{w,w′}maxt∈𝒱⁡Pt,j\displaystyle\leq\min_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,w^{\prime}}+\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,w}+\sum_{j\notin\{w,w^{\prime}\}}\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,j}
≤maxt∈𝒱⁡Pt,w′+(1−Δ)+∑j∉{w,w′}maxt∈𝒱⁡Pt,j\displaystyle\leq\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,w^{\prime}}+(1-\Delta)+\sum_{j\notin\{w,w^{\prime}\}}\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,j}
≤1−Δ+∑w′≠wmaxt∈𝒱⁡Pt,w′.\displaystyle\leq 1-\Delta+\sum_{w^{\prime}\neq w}\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,w^{\prime}}.
We observe that (74) provides an upper bound for the left-hand side of (73) in terms of ∑w′≠wmaxt∈𝒱⁡Pt,w′\sum_{w^{\prime}\neq w}\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,w^{\prime}}, while (75) provides a lower bound for the right-hand side of (73) involving both ∑w′≠wmaxt∈𝒱⁡Pt,w′\sum_{w^{\prime}\neq w}\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,w^{\prime}} and ∑w′≠wmint∈𝒱⁡Pt,w′\sum_{w^{\prime}\neq w}\min_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,w^{\prime}}.
To connect these bounds, we use the following fact that bridges both ∑w′≠wmaxt∈𝒱⁡Pt,w′\sum_{w^{\prime}\neq w}\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,w^{\prime}} and ∑w′≠wmint∈𝒱⁡Pt,w′\sum_{w^{\prime}\neq w}\min_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,w^{\prime}}:
∑w′≠wmint∈𝒱⁡Pt,w′+(|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1)⋅∑w′≠wmaxt∈𝒱⁡Pt,w′≥(|𝒱|∧|𝒲|)⋅Δ,\sum_{w^{\prime}\neq w}\min_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,w^{\prime}}+(|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1)\cdot\sum_{w^{\prime}\neq w}\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,w^{\prime}}\geq(|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|)\cdot\Delta,
(76)
which follows because mint∈𝒱⁡Pt,w′+∑j∉{w,w′}maxt∈𝒱⁡Pt,j≥1−maxt∈𝒱⁡Pt,w≥Δ\min_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,w^{\prime}}+\sum_{j\notin\{w,w^{\prime}\}}\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,j}\geq 1-\max_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}}P_{t,w}\geq\Delta.
Combining the inequalities (74), (75), and (76), we complete the proof of (73) for a fixed ww. Since the same argument holds for all ww, this establishes (73).
(iv)
Finally, we show that 𝑺Δ⋆:=(1−Δ1+Δ|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1,0,0,…,0)∈𝒟Δ{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}:=\left(\frac{1-\Delta}{1+\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1}},0,0,\ldots,0\right)\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta} by explicit construction. We consider two cases based on the relative sizes of |𝒲||{{\mathcal{W}}}| and |𝒱||{\mathcal{V}}|:
•
If |𝒲|≥|𝒱|+1|{{\mathcal{W}}}|\geq|{\mathcal{V}}|+1, we construct the first NTP distribution 𝑷t\bm{P}_{t} as follows:
𝑷t=(1−Δ,Δ|𝒱|−1,Δ|𝒱|−1,⋯,Δ|𝒱|−1,0,⋯,0).\bm{P}_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}1-\Delta,&\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|-1},&\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|-1},&\cdots,&\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|-1},&0,&\cdots,&0\end{pmatrix}.
(77)
For i=2,…,|𝒱|+1i=2,\ldots,|{\mathcal{V}}|+1, we define the ii-th NTP distribution by setting the first entry to 1−Δ1-\Delta, the ii-th entry to zero, and all other entries among the first |𝒱|+1|{\mathcal{V}}|+1 positions to Δ|𝒱|−1\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|-1}. A direct computation shows that all such distributions yield this very 𝑺{\bm{S}}-vector:
𝑺Δ⋆=(1−Δ1+Δ|𝒱|−1,0,⋯,0).{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1-\Delta}{1+\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|-1}},&0,&\cdots,&0\end{pmatrix}.
•
If |𝒲|≤|𝒱||{{\mathcal{W}}}|\leq|{\mathcal{V}}|, we instead construct the first NTP distribution as
𝑷t=(1−Δ,Δ|𝒲|−1,Δ|𝒲|−1,⋯,Δ|𝒲|−1,0,⋯,0),\bm{P}_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}1-\Delta,&\frac{\Delta}{|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1},&\frac{\Delta}{|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1},&\cdots,&\frac{\Delta}{|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1},&0,&\cdots,&0\end{pmatrix},
and define the remaining NTP distributions by cyclically shifting the zero entry among the first |𝒲|+1|{{\mathcal{W}}}|+1 positions while keeping the first entry fixed at 1−Δ1-\Delta. The argument mirrors the previous case and is omitted for brevity.
∎
B.4 Proof of Lemma 7.3
We first introduce an ancillary lemma that establishes the Schur-convexity of CDF, that is the mapping 𝑺↦F𝑺​(y){\bm{S}}\mapsto F_{\bm{S}}(y), in Lemma B.1.
Lemma B.1 (Schur-convexity).
For any y∈[0,1]y\in[0,1], the map 𝐒↦F𝐒​(d​y){\bm{S}}\mapsto F_{\bm{S}}({\mathrm{d}}y) is Schur-convex in 𝐒{\bm{S}}.
Proof of Lemma B.1.
For simplicity, we define G​(𝑺)=F𝑺​(y)G({\bm{S}})=F_{\bm{S}}(y) for any fixed y∈[0,1]y\in[0,1]. It is straightforward to verify that (i) GG is invariant under permutations of its coordinates, meaning that G⁡(𝑺)=G⁡(π⁡(𝑺))G({\bm{S}})=G(\pi({\bm{S}})) for any permutation π∈Perm⁡(𝒲)\pi\in\mathrm{Perm}({{\mathcal{W}}}), and (ii) all first partial derivatives of GG exist. By the Schur–Ostrowski criterion, GG is Schur-convex in 𝑺{\bm{S}} if and only if, for any 𝑺∈ℝd{\bm{S}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d} and any w,w′∈𝒲w,w^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{W}}}, the following condition holds:
(Sw−Sw′)​(∂G∂Sw−∂G∂Sw′)≥0.(S_{w}-S_{w^{\prime}})\left(\frac{\partial G}{\partial S_{w}}-\frac{\partial G}{\partial S_{w^{\prime}}}\right)\geq 0.
Direct calculation shows that
∂(Sw​y1/Sw)∂Sw=y1/Sw​(1+ln⁡1ySw)and∂2(Sw​y1/Sw)∂2Sw=y1/Sw​ln2⁡1ySw3.\frac{\partial(S_{w}y^{1/S_{w}})}{\partial S_{w}}=y^{1/S_{w}}\left(1+\frac{\ln\frac{1}{y}}{S_{w}}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\partial^{2}(S_{w}y^{1/S_{w}})}{\partial^{2}S_{w}}=y^{1/S_{w}}\frac{\ln^{2}\frac{1}{y}}{S_{w}^{3}}.
As a result,
(Sw−Sw′)​(∂G∂Sw−∂G∂Sw′)\displaystyle(S_{w}-S_{w^{\prime}})\left(\frac{\partial G}{\partial S_{w}}-\frac{\partial G}{\partial S_{w^{\prime}}}\right)
=(Sw−Sw′)∑wSw​[y1/Sw​(1+ln⁡1ySw)−y1/Sw′​(1+ln⁡1ySw′)]\displaystyle=\frac{(S_{w}-S_{w^{\prime}})}{\sum_{w}S_{w}}\left[y^{1/S_{w}}\left(1+\frac{\ln\frac{1}{y}}{S_{w}}\right)-y^{1/S_{w^{\prime}}}\left(1+\frac{\ln\frac{1}{y}}{S_{w^{\prime}}}\right)\right]
=(Sw−Sw′)2∑wSw⋅y1/S~w​ln2⁡1yS~w3≥0\displaystyle=\frac{(S_{w}-S_{w^{\prime}})^{2}}{\sum_{w}S_{w}}\cdot y^{1/\widetilde{S}_{w}}\frac{\ln^{2}\frac{1}{y}}{\widetilde{S}_{w}^{3}}\geq 0
where the last equation uses the mean value theorem and S~w\widetilde{S}_{w} lies between SwS_{w} and Sw′S_{w^{\prime}}.
∎
Now, using Lemma B.1, we can proceed to prove Lemma 7.3.
Proof of Lemma 7.3.
For any non-decreasing score function hh, by integration by parts, we have that
𝔼F𝐒⁡[e−h⁡(Y𝒱)]=∫e−h⁡(y)​F𝐒​(dy)=e−h⁡(1)+∫01F𝐒​(y)​e−h⁡(y)​h​(dy).\EB_{F_{\bm{S}}}[{\mathrm{e}}^{-h(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}})}]=\int{\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}F_{\bm{S}}({\mathrm{d}}y)={\mathrm{e}}^{-h(1)}+\int_{0}^{1}F_{\bm{S}}(y){\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}h({\mathrm{d}}y).
(78)
This implies that 𝔼F𝐒⁡[e−h⁡(Y𝒱)]\EB_{F_{\bm{S}}}[{\mathrm{e}}^{-h(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}})}] is a non-negative weighted sum of F𝑺​(y)F_{\bm{S}}(y) evaluated over all possible values of yy.
By Lemma B.1, we know that the mapping 𝑺↦F𝑺​(y){\bm{S}}\mapsto F_{\bm{S}}(y) is Schur-convex in 𝑺{\bm{S}} for any fixed y∈[0,1]y\in[0,1]. Using this result and applying Definition 7.1, we conclude that the function 𝑺↦∫e−h⁡(y)​F𝑺​(𝑑y){\bm{S}}\mapsto\int e^{-h(y)}F_{\bm{S}}({\mathrm{d}}y) is isotonic, order-preserving, and therefore Schur-convex in 𝑺{\bm{S}}.
∎
B.5 Proof of Lemma 7.4
Proof of Lemma 7.4.
The integration by parts implies that
∫e−h⁡(y)​F𝑺​(𝑑y)=e−h⁡(1)+∫F𝑺​(y)​e−h⁡(y)​h​(𝑑y).\int{\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}F_{\bm{S}}({\mathrm{d}}y)={\mathrm{e}}^{-h(1)}+\int F_{\bm{S}}(y){\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}h({\mathrm{d}}y).
(78)
It suffices to prove that
max⁡∫𝑺∈𝒟Δ⁡F𝑺​(y)​e−h⁡(y)​h​(𝑑y)≤max⁡∫𝑺∈𝒟Δ∩ℋΔ⁡F𝑺​(y)​e−h⁡(y)​h​(𝑑y).\max_{{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}}\int F_{\bm{S}}(y){\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}h({\mathrm{d}}y)\leq\max_{{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{\Delta}}\int F_{\bm{S}}(y){\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}h({\mathrm{d}}y).
To achieve this, it suffices to prove that
max⁡∫𝑺∈𝒟Δ∖ℋΔ⁡F𝑺​(y)​e−h⁡(y)​h​(𝑑y)≤∫F𝑺Δ⋆​(y)​e−h⁡(y)​h​(𝑑y).\max_{{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}_{\Delta}}\int F_{\bm{S}}(y){\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}h({\mathrm{d}}y)\leq\int F_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}}(y){\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}h({\mathrm{d}}y).
(79)
where 𝑺Δ⋆:=(1−Δ1+Δ|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1,0,0,…,0){\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}:=\left(\frac{1-\Delta}{1+\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1}},0,0,\ldots,0\right).
From Lemma 7.2, it follows that 𝑺Δ⋆∈𝒟Δ{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}.
By the definition of ℋΔ{\mathcal{H}}_{\Delta}, it is clear that 𝑺Δ⋆∈ℋΔ{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\Delta}.
Consequently, 𝑺Δ⋆∈𝒟Δ∩ℋΔ{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{\Delta}.
With this result, once (79) is established, it follows that
max⁡∫𝑺∈𝒟Δ∖ℋΔ⁡F𝑺​(y)​e−h⁡(y)​h​(𝑑y)≤∫F𝑺Δ⋆​(y)​e−h⁡(y)​h​(𝑑y)≤max⁡∫𝑺∈𝒟Δ∩ℋΔ⁡F𝑺​(y)​e−h⁡(y)​h​(𝑑y),\max_{{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}_{\Delta}}\int F_{\bm{S}}(y){\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}h({\mathrm{d}}y)\leq\int F_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}}(y){\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}h({\mathrm{d}}y)\leq\max_{{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{\Delta}}\int F_{\bm{S}}(y){\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}h({\mathrm{d}}y),
which completes the proof.
In the following, we will prove (79).
For any 𝑺∈𝒟Δ∖ℋΔ{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}_{\Delta}, by Eqn. (78), Lemma 7.3, and the definition of Schur-convexity, it follows that
∫F𝑺​(y)​e−h⁡(y)​h​(𝑑y)≤∫F𝑺1​(y)​e−h⁡(y)​h​(𝑑y),\int F_{\bm{S}}(y){\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}h({\mathrm{d}}y)\leq\int F_{{\bm{S}}_{1}}(y){\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}h({\mathrm{d}}y),
where 𝑺1:=(∑wSw,0,…,0){\bm{S}}_{1}:=\left(\sum_{w}S_{w},0,\ldots,0\right) majorizes the given 𝑺{\bm{S}}.
Next, note that 𝑺∈𝒟Δ∖ℋΔ{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}_{\Delta} implies ∑wSw≤1−Δ1+Δ|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1\sum_{w}S_{w}\leq\frac{1-\Delta}{1+\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1}}. Since F𝑷​(y)=y1/S1F_{\bm{P}}(y)=y^{1/S_{1}} is increasing in S1S_{1} when 𝑺{\bm{S}} has only one non-zero entry (that is, 𝑺=(S1,0,…,0){\bm{S}}=(S_{1},0,\ldots,0)), we deduce that F𝑺1​(y)≤F𝑺Δ⋆​(y)F_{{\bm{S}}_{1}}(y)\leq F_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}}(y) for any y∈[0,1]y\in[0,1].
As a result, the last inequality holds, which completes the proof.
∎
B.6 Proof of Lemma 7.5
Proof of Lemma 7.5.
1.
Since 𝒦Δ{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta} is the intersection of several half-spaces, it forms a convex polyhedron. We now prove that the extreme points of 𝒦Δ{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta} are precisely the elements of ℰΔ{\mathcal{E}}_{\Delta}.
First, observe that both 𝑷Δ⋆\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star} and 𝑺Δ⋆{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star} belong to 𝒦Δ{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta}, and by the permutation invariance of 𝒦Δ{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta}, we have ℰΔ⊆𝒦Δ{\mathcal{E}}_{\Delta}\subseteq{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta}. This implies conv⁡(ℰΔ)⊆𝒦Δ\mathrm{conv}({\mathcal{E}}_{\Delta})\subseteq{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta}.
To prove the reverse inclusion, it suffices to show that any point in 𝒦Δ{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta} can be expressed as a convex combination of points in ℰΔ{\mathcal{E}}_{\Delta}. Consider an arbitrary 𝑺∈𝒦Δ{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta}, and define C=∑wSwC=\sum_{w}S_{w} as the sum of its coordinates. By the definition of 𝒦Δ{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta}, the largest coordinate of 𝑺{\bm{S}} satisfies
maxw⁡Sw≤(1−Δ)​(1−1−C|𝒱|∧|𝒲|).\max_{w}S_{w}\leq(1-\Delta)\left(1-\frac{1-C}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|}\right).
We assert that 𝑺{\bm{S}} is majorized by the following vector
𝑺new=((1−Δ)​(1−1−C|𝒱|∧|𝒲|),C−(1−Δ)​(1−1−C|𝒱|∧|𝒲|),0,…),\bm{S}_{\mathrm{new}}=\left((1-\Delta)(1-\frac{1-C}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|}),C-(1-\Delta)(1-\frac{1-C}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|}),0,\dots\right),
which, by definition, belongs to 𝒦Δ{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta}.
By Lemma B.2, 𝑺{\bm{S}} can thus be expressed as a convex combination of permutations of 𝑺new\bm{S}_{\mathrm{new}}. Since 𝑺new\bm{S}_{\mathrm{new}} itself is a convex combination of 𝑷Δ⋆\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star} and 𝑺Δ⋆{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}, it follows that 𝑺{\bm{S}} is a convex combination of points in ℰΔ{\mathcal{E}}_{\Delta}. This completes the proof.
Lemma B.2 ([30]).
Given two vectors 𝐱,𝐲∈ℝd{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}, if 𝐱{\bm{x}} majorizes 𝐲{\bm{y}}, then 𝐲{\bm{y}} is a convex combination of 𝐱{\bm{x}} and its permutations.
2.
By Lemma 7.2, we know that 𝑺Δ⋆∈𝒟Δ{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}. Additionally, we have 𝑷Δ⋆∈𝒟Δ\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta} because setting 𝑷t1=⋯=𝑷tk=𝑷Δ⋆\bm{P}_{t_{1}}=\cdots=\bm{P}_{t_{k}}=\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star} results in a corresponding 𝑺{\bm{S}}-vector equal to 𝑷Δ⋆\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}, which, by definition, belongs to 𝒟Δ{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}. Combining these observations with the permutation invariance of 𝒟Δ{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}, we conclude that ℰΔ⊆𝒟Δ{\mathcal{E}}_{\Delta}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}.
On the other hand, by definition, we also have ℰΔ⊆𝒦Δ{\mathcal{E}}_{\Delta}\subseteq{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta}. The conclusion then follows.
3.
It suffices to prove that 𝒟Δ∩ℋΔ⊆𝒦Δ⊆conv⁡(𝒟Δ∩ℋΔ){\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{\Delta}\subseteq{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta}\subseteq\mathrm{conv}({\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{\Delta}) since 𝒦Δ{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta} is convex.
By Lemma 7.2, we know that 𝒟Δ∩ℋΔ⊆𝒦Δ{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{\Delta}\subseteq{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta}. We now turn to the opposite direction.
By the first point, we have 𝒦Δ=conv⁡(ℰΔ){\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta}=\mathrm{conv}({\mathcal{E}}_{\Delta}). We have that ℰΔ⊆𝒟Δ∩ℋΔ{\mathcal{E}}_{\Delta}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{\Delta} from the second point. Consequently, it follows that 𝒦Δ⊆conv⁡(𝒟Δ∩ℋΔ){\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta}\subseteq\mathrm{conv}({\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{\Delta}), which completes the proof.
∎
B.7 Proof of Lemma 7.6
Proof of Lemma 7.6.
We note that
sup𝑺∈𝒦Δ∫F𝑺(y)e−h⁡(y)h(dy)=supC∈[1−Δ1+Δ|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1,1]sup𝑺∈𝒦Δ:∑wSw=C∫F𝑺(y)e−h⁡(y)h(dy).\sup_{{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta}}\int F_{\bm{S}}(y){\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}h({\mathrm{d}}y)=\sup_{C\in[\frac{1-\Delta}{1+\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1}},1]}\sup_{{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta}:\sum_{w}S_{w}=C}\int F_{\bm{S}}(y){\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}h({\mathrm{d}}y).
On the intersection of the plane ∑wSw=C\sum_{w}S_{w}=C and 𝒦Δ{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta}, we assert that λ​𝑺Δ⋆+(1−λ)​𝑷Δ⋆\lambda{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}+(1-\lambda)\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star} majorizes any other points because it has the largest possible first entry.
The calculation shows that here
λ=(1−C)⋅(|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1+Δ)Δ⋅|𝒱|∧|𝒲|∈[0,1].\lambda=\frac{(1-C)\cdot(|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1+\Delta)}{\Delta\cdot|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|}\in[0,1].
By the definition of Schur-convexity, we have
sup𝑺∈𝒦Δ:∑wSw=C∫F𝑺(y)e−h⁡(y)h(dy)=∫Fλ​𝑺Δ⋆+(1−λ)​𝑷Δ⋆(y)e−h⁡(y)h(dy)=:G(C).\sup_{{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta}:\sum_{w}S_{w}=C}\int F_{\bm{S}}(y){\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}h({\mathrm{d}}y)=\int F_{\lambda{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}+(1-\lambda)\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}}(y){\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}h({\mathrm{d}}y)=:G(C).
We denote the largest and the second largest entries in λ​𝑺Δ⋆+(1−λ)​𝑷Δ⋆\lambda{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}+(1-\lambda)\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star} by S1S_{1} and S2S_{2}.
One can see that
S1=λ​1−Δ1+Δ|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1+(1−λ)​(1−Δ)andS2=(1−λ)​Δ.S_{1}=\lambda\frac{1-\Delta}{1+\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1}}+(1-\lambda)(1-\Delta)\quad\text{and}\quad S_{2}=(1-\lambda)\Delta.
(80)
It then follows that
Fλ​𝑺Δ⋆+(1−λ)​𝑷Δ⋆​(y)\displaystyle F_{\lambda{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}+(1-\lambda)\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}}(y)
=S1​y1/S1+S2​y1/S2S1+S2\displaystyle=\frac{S_{1}y^{1/S_{1}}+S_{2}y^{1/S_{2}}}{S_{1}+S_{2}}
≤(a)​λ​1−Δ1+Δ|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1​y1+Δ|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−11−Δ+(1−λ)​(1−Δ)​y11−Δ+S2​y1/S2λ​1−Δ1+Δ|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1+(1−λ)​(1−Δ)+S2\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\frac{\lambda\frac{1-\Delta}{1+\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1}}y^{\frac{1+\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1}}{1-\Delta}}+(1-\lambda)(1-\Delta)y^{\frac{1}{1-\Delta}}+S_{2}y^{1/S_{2}}}{\lambda\frac{1-\Delta}{1+\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1}}+(1-\lambda)(1-\Delta)+S_{2}}
≤(b)​λ​1−Δ1+Δ|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1​y1+Δ|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−11−Δ+(1−λ)​(1−Δ)​y11−Δ+(1−λ)​Δ​y1Δλ​1−Δ1+Δ|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1+(1−λ)​(1−Δ)+(1−λ)​Δ\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\frac{\lambda\frac{1-\Delta}{1+\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1}}y^{\frac{1+\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1}}{1-\Delta}}+(1-\lambda)(1-\Delta)y^{\frac{1}{1-\Delta}}+(1-\lambda)\Delta y^{\frac{1}{\Delta}}}{\lambda\frac{1-\Delta}{1+\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1}}+(1-\lambda)(1-\Delta)+(1-\lambda)\Delta}
=(c)​λ​1−Δ1+Δ|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1​F𝑺Δ⋆​(y)+(1−λ)​F𝑷Δ⋆​(y)λ​1−Δ1+Δ|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1+(1−λ),\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{=}\frac{\lambda\frac{1-\Delta}{1+\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1}}F_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}}(y)+(1-\lambda)F_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}}(y)}{\lambda\frac{1-\Delta}{1+\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1}}+(1-\lambda)},
where (a)(a) uses the fact that the map S↦S​y1/SS\mapsto Sy^{1/S} is convex in SS, (b)(b) uses the fact that y1/S2≤y1Δy^{1/S_{2}}\leq y^{\frac{1}{\Delta}} due to y∈[0,1]y\in[0,1] and λ∈[0,1]\lambda\in[0,1] and (c)(c) follows from arrangement.
Therefore, for any C∈[1−Δ1+Δ|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1,1]C\in[\frac{1-\Delta}{1+\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1}},1], it follows that
G⁡(C)\displaystyle G(C)
=∫Fλ​𝑺Δ⋆+(1−λ)​𝑷Δ⋆​(y)​e−h⁡(y)​h​(𝑑y)\displaystyle=\int F_{\lambda{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}+(1-\lambda)\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}}(y){\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}h({\mathrm{d}}y)
≤∫λ​1−Δ1+Δ|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1​F𝑺Δ⋆​(y)+(1−λ)​F𝑷Δ⋆​(y)λ​1−Δ1+Δ|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1+(1−λ)​e−h⁡(y)​h​(𝑑y)\displaystyle\leq\int\frac{\lambda\frac{1-\Delta}{1+\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1}}F_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}}(y)+(1-\lambda)F_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}}(y)}{\lambda\frac{1-\Delta}{1+\frac{\Delta}{|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1}}+(1-\lambda)}{\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}h({\mathrm{d}}y)
≤max⁡{∫F𝑺Δ⋆​e−h⁡(y)​h​(𝑑y),∫F𝑷Δ⋆​e−h⁡(y)​h​(𝑑y)},\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{\int F_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}}{\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}h({\mathrm{d}}y),\int F_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}}{\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}h({\mathrm{d}}y)\right\},
where the last inequality uses the fact that the maximum value of a linear function on a line segment is attained at the endpoints.
∎
B.8 Optimal Score in the Intermediate Regime
In this subsection, we detail the discussion in Remark 4.1.
Specifically, if we do not require the optimal score function to be part of a saddle point solution, that is, the optimal score function solves the following minimization problem
minh⁡J⁡(h)​where​J​(h):=max𝑺∈𝒟Δ⁡L⁡(h,𝑺)​and​L​(h,𝑺):=𝔼0⁡[h⁡(Y)]+log⁡𝔼F𝐒​[e−h⁡(Y)],\min_{h}J(h)~~\text{where}~~J(h):=\max_{{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}}L(h,{\bm{S}})~~\text{and}~~L(h,{\bm{S}}):=\EB_{0}[h(Y)]+\log\EB_{F_{\bm{S}}}[{\mathrm{e}}^{-h(Y)}],
(81)
then the optimal score function always exists in the intermediate regime.
However, it doesn’t have a closed form. In the following, we formally state this result.
Lemma B.3.
Any score function that minimizes JJ in (81) is non-decreasing.
Proof of Lemma B.3.
For any score function hh, we can construct a non-decreasing transformation h↑h^{\uparrow} such that L⁡(h↑,𝑺)≤L⁡(h,𝑺)L(h^{\uparrow},{\bm{S}})\leq L(h,{\bm{S}}) for all 𝑺∈𝒟Δ{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}.
Specifically, let Gh​(z)=ℙ0​(h⁡(Y)≤z)G_{h}(z)={\mathbb{P}}_{0}(h(Y)\leq z) with Y∼Unif⁡(0,1)Y\sim\mathrm{Unif}(0,1) under H0H_{0}, and define h↑h^{\uparrow} as the generalized inverse of GhG_{h}:
h↑​(y)=Gh−1​(y):=inf{z∈ℝ:Gh​(z)≥y}.h^{\uparrow}(y)=G_{h}^{-1}(y):=\inf\{z\in{\mathbb{R}}:G_{h}(z)\geq y\}.
By construction, h↑h^{\uparrow} is non-decreasing. Moreover, for any z∈ℝz\in{\mathbb{R}},
ℙ0​(h↑​(Y)≤z)=ℙ0​(Gh−1​(Y)≤z)=ℙ0​(Y≤Gh​(z))=Gh​(z)=ℙ0​(h⁡(Y)≤z),{\mathbb{P}}_{0}(h^{\uparrow}(Y)\leq z)={\mathbb{P}}_{0}(G_{h}^{-1}(Y)\leq z)={\mathbb{P}}_{0}(Y\leq G_{h}(z))=G_{h}(z)={\mathbb{P}}_{0}(h(Y)\leq z),
so h↑​(Y)h^{\uparrow}(Y) and h⁡(Y)h(Y) have the same distribution under H0H_{0}.
We now examine the two terms in L⁡(h,𝑺)L(h,{\bm{S}}).
For the first term, 𝔼0⁡[h⁡(Y)]=𝔼0⁡[h↑​(Y)]\EB_{0}[h(Y)]=\EB_{0}[h^{\uparrow}(Y)] since the distributions coincide.
Let f𝑺f_{\bm{S}} denote the alternative PDF, which is non-decreasing in yy.
Consider the second term ∫01e−h⁡(y)​f𝑺​(y)​𝑑y\int_{0}^{1}{\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}f_{\bm{S}}(y){\mathrm{d}}y.
The Hardy–Littlewood inequality [4, Chapter 2] implies that the integral of the product of two functions is minimized when the functions are ordered in opposite monotonicity.
Since f𝑺​(y)f_{\bm{S}}(y) is non-decreasing, this integral is minimized when e−h⁡(y){\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)} is non-increasing, which is equivalent to h⁡(y)h(y) being non-decreasing.
Hence, L⁡(h↑,𝑺)≤L⁡(h,𝑺)L(h^{\uparrow},{\bm{S}})\leq L(h,{\bm{S}}) for all 𝑺∈𝒟Δ{\bm{S}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}.
∎
By Lemma B.3 and Lemmas 7.4, 7.5, and 7.6, for any non-decreasing function hh,
J⁡(h)=max⁡{L⁡(h,𝑷Δ⋆),L⁡(h,𝑺Δ⋆)},J(h)=\max\{L(h,\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}),\,L(h,{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star})\},
(82)
where 𝑷Δ⋆\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star} and 𝑺Δ⋆{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star} are the two distribution vectors defined in Lemma 7.5.
Since L⁡(h,𝑺)L(h,{\bm{S}}) is strictly convex in hh for any fixed 𝑺{\bm{S}}, the above objective, being the pointwise maximum of two strictly convex functions, is also strictly convex.
This ensures the existence and uniqueness of the minimizer of JJ, which is characterized in the following lemma.
Lemma B.4 (Optimal score function).
When Δ∈(Δ1⋆,Δ2⋆)\Delta\in(\Delta_{1}^{\star},\Delta_{2}^{\star}), that is, we have L⁡(h𝐏Δ⋆,𝐏Δ⋆)<L⁡(h𝐏Δ⋆,𝐒Δ⋆)L(h_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}},\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star})<L(h_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}},{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}) and L⁡(h𝐒Δ⋆,𝐒Δ⋆)<L⁡(h𝐒Δ⋆,𝐏Δ⋆)L(h_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}},{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star})<L(h_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}},\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}), the optimal score that minimizes JJ defined in (81) is
hλ⋆gum​(y)=log⁡(λ⋆⋅y(|𝒱|∧|𝒲|)​Δ(|𝒱|∧|𝒲|−1)​(1−Δ)+(1−λ⋆)⋅(yΔ1−Δ+y1−ΔΔ))h_{\lambda^{\star}}^{\mathrm{gum}}(y)=\log(\lambda^{\star}\cdot y^{\frac{(|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|)\,\Delta}{(|{\mathcal{V}}|\wedge|{{\mathcal{W}}}|-1)(1-\Delta)}}+(1-\lambda^{\star})\cdot(y^{\frac{\Delta}{1-\Delta}}+y^{\frac{1-\Delta}{\Delta}}))
where λ⋆\lambda^{\star} is the solution to this equation L⁡(hλgum,𝐏Δ⋆)=L⁡(hλgum,𝐒Δ⋆)L(h_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{gum}},\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star})=L(h_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{gum}},{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}).
As shown in Lemma B.4, the optimal score hλ⋆gumh_{\lambda^{\star}}^{\mathrm{gum}} takes the form of a log-likelihood ratio score associated with a mixture alternative distribution, where the mixing parameter λ⋆\lambda^{\star} has no closed-form expression.
For this reason, we do not pursue it further in the main text.
Proof of Lemma B.4.
For simplicity, let h⋆h^{\star} denote the optimal score function.
We first claim that the unique minimizer h⋆h^{\star} must satisfy the equalization condition:
L⁡(h⋆,𝑷Δ⋆)=L⁡(h⋆,𝑺Δ⋆).L(h^{\star},\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star})=L(h^{\star},{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}).
(83)
Suppose, for contradiction, that L⁡(h⋆,𝑷Δ⋆)>L⁡(h⋆,𝑺Δ⋆)L(h^{\star},\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star})>L(h^{\star},{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}).
Then we have J⁡(h⋆)=L⁡(h⋆,𝑷Δ⋆)J(h^{\star})=L(h^{\star},\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}) from (82), so h⋆h^{\star} is also a local minimizer of L⁡(h,𝑷Δ⋆)L(h,\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}).
By strict convexity, this forces h⋆h^{\star} to equal the unique global minimizer h𝑷Δ⋆h_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}}.
But substituting back yields L⁡(h𝑷Δ⋆,𝑷Δ⋆)>L⁡(h𝑷Δ⋆,𝑺Δ⋆)L(h_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}},\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star})>L(h_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}},{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}), which contradicts the condition that L⁡(h𝑷Δ⋆,𝑷Δ⋆)<L⁡(h𝑷Δ⋆,𝑺Δ⋆).L(h_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}},\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star})<L(h_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}},{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}).
A similar argument rules out the case L⁡(h⋆,𝑺Δ⋆)>L⁡(h⋆,𝑷Δ⋆)L(h^{\star},{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star})>L(h^{\star},\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}).
Thus, the equalization condition (83) must hold. This means that at the optimal point h⋆h^{\star}, both component functions are active and attain the same value.
From the first-order stationary condition, the zero function belongs to the subdifferential set at h⋆h^{\star}, that is, 0∈∂J⁡(h⋆)0\in\partial J(h^{\star}). By standard convex analysis, the subdifferential of the maximum of functions is the convex hull of the gradients of the active functions, namely ∂J⁡(h⋆)=conv⁡{∇hL​(h⋆,𝑷Δ⋆),∇hL​(h⋆,𝑺Δ⋆)}.\partial J(h^{\star})=\mathrm{conv}\{\nabla_{h}L(h^{\star},\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}),\nabla_{h}L(h^{\star},{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star})\}.
This implies the existence of a mixing parameter λ⋆∈(0,1)\lambda^{\star}\in(0,1) such that
λ⋆​∇hL​(h⋆,𝑷Δ⋆)+(1−λ⋆)​∇hL​(h⋆,𝑺Δ⋆)=0.\lambda^{\star}\nabla_{h}L(h^{\star},\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star})+(1-\lambda^{\star})\nabla_{h}L(h^{\star},{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star})=0.
(84)
We remark that we must have λ⋆∈(0,1)\lambda^{\star}\in(0,1); otherwise h⋆h^{\star} would equal h𝑷Δ⋆h_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}} or h𝑺Δ⋆h_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}}, which contradicts Δ∈(Δ1⋆,Δ2⋆)\Delta\in(\Delta_{1}^{\star},\Delta_{2}^{\star}).
We now derive the explicit form of h⋆h^{\star}.
Let f𝑷Δ⋆f_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}} and f𝑺Δ⋆f_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}} denote the alternative PDFs associated with F𝑷Δ⋆F_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}} and F𝑺Δ⋆F_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}}, respectively, and let f0f_{0} be the null PDF. The functional gradient of L⁡(h,𝑺)L(h,{\bm{S}}) with respect to hh at a point yy is
∇hL​(h,𝑺)​(y)=f0​(y)−e−h⁡(y)​f𝑺Δ⋆​(y)𝔼F𝐒⁡[e−h].\nabla_{h}L(h,{\bm{S}})(y)=f_{0}(y)-\frac{{\mathrm{e}}^{-h(y)}f_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}}(y)}{\EB_{F_{{\bm{S}}}}[{\mathrm{e}}^{-h}]}.
By the equalization condition (83), the denominators are equal: 𝔼F𝐏Δ⋆⁡[e−h⋆]=𝔼F𝐒Δ⋆⁡[e−h⋆]\EB_{F_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}}}[{\mathrm{e}}^{-h^{\star}}]=\EB_{F_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}}}[{\mathrm{e}}^{-h^{\star}}]. Let this common value be C⋆C^{\star}. Substituting the gradients into the optimality condition (84) gives
f0​(y)−e−h⋆​(y)C∗​(λ⋆​f𝑷Δ⋆​(y)+(1−λ⋆)​f𝑺Δ⋆​(y))=0.f_{0}(y)-\frac{{\mathrm{e}}^{-h^{\star}(y)}}{C^{*}}\left(\lambda^{\star}f_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}}(y)+(1-\lambda^{\star})f_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}}(y)\right)=0.
Solving for h⋆​(y)h^{\star}(y) and noting that the additive constant −log⁡C⋆-\log C^{\star} does not affect detection performance, we obtain the explicit form of the optimal score function:
h⋆​(y)=log⁡(λ⋆​f𝑷Δ⋆​(y)+(1−λ⋆)​f𝑺Δ⋆​(y)f0​(y)).h^{\star}(y)=\log\left(\frac{\lambda^{\star}f_{\bm{P}_{\Delta}^{\star}}(y)+(1-\lambda^{\star})f_{{\bm{S}}_{\Delta}^{\star}}(y)}{f_{0}(y)}\right).
Thus, the optimal score function is precisely the log-likelihood ratio between the null distribution f0f_{0} and a mixture of the two extremal alternative distributions.
∎
Appendix C Proof for Inverse Transform Watermarks in Section 5
We begin by introducing the notation and terminology used throughout this section, as the analysis of the inverse transform watermark involves several technical components.
General notation.
Throughout the proof, we use (⋅)+(\cdot)_{+} to denote the positive part function, that is, (x)+=max⁡{x,0}(x)_{+}=\max\{x,0\}. For a function f:A→ℝf:A\to\mathbb{R} and a constant M>0M>0, we define the clipped extension [f][−M,M]:ℝ→ℝ[f]_{[-M,M]}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R} as a continuous function satisfying:
[f][−M,M]​(x)={f⁡(x),if ​x∈A​ and ​f​(x)∈[−M,M],M,if ​x∈A​ and ​f​(x)>M,−M,if ​x∈A​ and ​f​(x)<−M,a continuous value in ​[−M,M],if ​x∉A.[f]_{[-M,M]}(x)=\begin{cases}f(x),&\text{if }x\in A\text{ and }f(x)\in[-M,M],\\
M,&\text{if }x\in A\text{ and }f(x)>M,\\
-M,&\text{if }x\in A\text{ and }f(x)<-M,\\
\text{a continuous value in }[-M,M],&\text{if }x\notin A.\end{cases}
(85)
We denote the permutation group over 𝒲{{\mathcal{W}}} by Perm⁡(𝒲)\mathrm{Perm}({{\mathcal{W}}}), and use π∈Perm⁡(𝒲)\pi\in\mathrm{Perm}({{\mathcal{W}}}) to represent a permutation of the vocabulary. The permutation π\pi acts on token indices, so that π⁡(w)\pi(w) denotes the token to which ww is mapped. For brevity, we denote the set {1,2,…,m}\{1,2,\ldots,m\} by [m][m].
Belief classes.
We formally reformulate the conditions from Assumption 5.1 and collect all NTP distributions within a minimal unit 𝒱\mathcal{V} of type τ\tau that satisfy Assumption 5.1 into the class 𝒬τ,𝚫{\mathcal{Q}}_{\tau,\bm{\Delta}}.
As defined, 𝒬τ,𝚫{\mathcal{Q}}_{\tau,\bm{\Delta}} depends only on the type τ\tau and the regularity levels 𝚫=(Δt)t∈𝒱\bm{\Delta}=(\Delta_{t})_{t\in\mathcal{V}}, as this information is sufficient to determine all valid NTP distributions in the asymptotic regime we consider.
Definition C.1 (Fixed-parameter belief class).
For a minimal unit 𝒱=ℐζ\mathcal{V}={\mathcal{I}}^{\zeta} of type τ\tau and a sequence of regularity levels 𝚫=(Δt)t∈𝒱\bm{\Delta}=(\Delta_{t})_{t\in\mathcal{V}} with each Δt∈[Δ,1−δ]\Delta_{t}\in[\Delta,1-\delta] as in Assumption 5.1, we define the class 𝒬τ,𝚫{\mathcal{Q}}_{\tau,\bm{\Delta}} as the set of all joint NTP distributions 𝐏𝒱\bm{P}_{\mathcal{V}} over the tokens in 𝒱\mathcal{V} that satisfy Assumption 5.1:
𝒬τ,𝚫={𝑷𝒱:∀t∈ℐζ,Pt,wt=Pt,(1)=1−Δtandlog|𝒲|⋅Pt,(2)≤ε|𝒲|},{\mathcal{Q}}_{\tau,\bm{\Delta}}=\left\{\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}:\ \forall t\in{\mathcal{I}}^{\zeta},\ P_{t,w_{t}}=P_{t,(1)}=1-\Delta_{t}\ \text{and}\ \log|{{\mathcal{W}}}|\cdot P_{t,(2)}\leq{\varepsilon}_{|{{\mathcal{W}}}|}\right\},
(86)
where 𝐏𝒱:=(𝐏t)t∈𝒱\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}}:=(\bm{P}_{t})_{t\in{\mathcal{V}}} is the collection of marginal distributions of tokens in 𝒱{\mathcal{V}}, and Pt,(1),Pt,(2)P_{t,(1)},P_{t,(2)} denote the largest and second-largest probabilities in the NTP distribution 𝐏t\bm{P}_{t}.
C.1 Proof of Lemma 5.1
Proof of Lemma 5.1.
To establish the asymptotic distribution of the pseudorandom numbers and tokens, we first characterize their exact joint distribution in Lemma C.1. Since our analysis focuses on a fixed minimal unit ℐkζ{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta}, we omit the subscript kk for simplicity and denote it by 𝒱=ℐkζ{\mathcal{V}}={\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{\zeta}, which contains mm sub-blocks. We adopt this notational convention throughout the proof of Theorem 5.1 as well.
Lemma C.1 (Exact joint distribution).
Fix a minimal unit (or block) ℐζ{\mathcal{I}}^{\zeta} consisting of mm sub-blocks, denoted by ℐℓY{\mathcal{I}}_{\ell}^{Y} for ℓ∈[m]\ell\in[m], such that ⋃ℓ=1mℐℓY=ℐζ\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{m}{\mathcal{I}}_{\ell}^{Y}={\mathcal{I}}^{\zeta}.
Let Assumption 3.1 hold.
Assume the shared pseudorandom variables for this block are (U,π)(U,\pi), where U∈[0,1]U\in[0,1] is uniform and π∈Perm⁡(𝒲)\pi\in\mathrm{Perm}({{\mathcal{W}}}) is a permutation of the vocabulary.
Denote the token associated with each sub-block ℐℓY{\mathcal{I}}_{\ell}^{Y} by wℓw_{\ell} for ℓ∈[m]\ell\in[m].
Then the joint distribution of (U,π⁡(w1),…,π⁡(wm))(U,\pi(w_{1}),\ldots,\pi(w_{m})) conditioned on the fixed block ℐζ{\mathcal{I}}^{\zeta} is given by
ℙ1(U≤r,π(wi′)=wi for i=1,…,m|ℐζ)\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{1}\left(U\leq r,\;\pi(w_{i}^{\prime})=w_{i}\text{ for }i=1,\dots,m\;\middle|\;{\mathcal{I}}^{\zeta}\right)
(87)
=\displaystyle=\,
1|𝒲|!​∑π∈Perm⁡(𝒲)π⁡(wℓ′)=wℓ,ℓ∈[m]ℙ⁡(U∈⋂ℓ=1m⋂t∈ℐℓY(aπ,wℓ′−1(t),aπ,wℓ′(t))∩[0,r])1|𝒲|!​∑w1′,…,wm′distinct∑π∈Perm⁡(𝒲)π⁡(wℓ′)=wℓ,ℓ∈[m]ℙ⁡(U∈⋂ℓ=1m⋂t∈ℐℓY(aπ,wℓ′−1(t),aπ,wℓ′(t)))\displaystyle\frac{\frac{1}{|{{\mathcal{W}}}|!}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathrm{Perm}({{\mathcal{W}}})\\

aπ,wl(t)=∑j=1wlPt,π⁡(j),∀t∈ℐℓY,∀ℓ∈[m].a_{\pi,w_{l}}^{(t)}=\sum_{j=1}^{w_{l}}P_{t,\pi(j)},\quad\forall t\in{\mathcal{I}}^{Y}_{\ell},\quad\forall\ell\in[m].
The proof of Lemma C.1 is provided in Section C.4. Following the convention in [27], we analyze the expectation of an arbitrary test function JJ of (U,π⁡(w1),…,π⁡(wm))(U,\pi(w_{1}),\ldots,\pi(w_{m})), as it characterizes the joint distribution as well, is equivalent to studying the CDF, and facilitates the analysis of the asymptotic behavior.
Corollary C.1.
Under the same notation and assumption as in Lemma C.1, for any measurable test function J:[0,1]m+1→[0,∞)J\colon[0,1]^{m+1}\to[0,\infty), we have:
𝔼1,𝐏ℐζ⁡[J⁡(U,η⁡(π⁡(w1)),…,η⁡(π⁡(wm)))]\displaystyle\EB_{1,\bm{P}_{{\mathcal{I}}^{\zeta}}}\left[J(U,\eta(\pi(w_{1})),\ldots,\eta(\pi(w_{m})))\right]
=∑w1′,…,wm′distinct∑π∈Perm⁡(𝒲)π⁡(wℓ′)=wℓ,∀ℓ∈[m](∫maxℓ,t⁡aπ,wℓ′−1(t)minℓ,t⁡aπ,wℓ′(t)J⁡(u,η⁡(w1′),…,η⁡(wm′))​𝑑u)​𝟏minℓ,t⁡aπ,wℓ′(t)≥maxℓ,t⁡aπ,wℓ′−1(t)∑w1′,…,wm′distinct∑π∈Perm⁡(𝒲)π⁡(wℓ′)=wℓ,∀ℓ∈[m]ℙ⁡(U∈⋂ℓ=1m⋂t∈ℐℓY(aπ,wℓ′−1(t),aπ,wℓ′(t))).\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,w^{\prime}_{m}\\
